# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum II

## Funding proposal package for FP142

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Argentina REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014-2019";

b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or fiscal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

d) Secretary's assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Response from accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

# Funding Proposal

## REDD-plus results based payments

### Version 1.0

Accredited entities are expected to develop a funding proposal in close consultation with the relevant national designated authority for REDD+ and the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development for the Pilot Programme on REDD+ results-based payments (Decision B/2014). The funding proposal should follow the terms of reference of that Board decision and will be assessed per Stage 2 (sections 2 - 3) of the screencast annexed to the same Board decision.

**Programme Title:** Argentina REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014-2016

**Country:** Argentina

**Results period in this proposal:** 2014 – 2016

**National Designated Authority:**
Under-Secretariat of International Financial Relations
Development, Secretariat of Strategic Affairs of the President of the Republic

**REDD-plus entity/local point:**
Ministry for the Environment and Sustainable Development (Ministerio de Ambiente y Desarrollo Sostenible)

**Accredited Entity:** Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

**Date of first submission/ version number:** [2020-05-04] [V. 001]

**Date of current submission/ version number:** [2020-10-07] [V. 008]

# A. Proposed and projected REDD+ plus results

## Please provide the following information:

### Total volume of REDD-plus results achieved in the reporting period (1 December 2015 to 31 December 2016) within the country's HUR technical annexes (tCO2e):

Indicate the total volume of achieved results during the reporting period (1 December 2015 to 31 December 2016), as reported in the Technical Annexes to the IAP offered to the GCF.

The total volume of REDD+ results achieved by Argentina and reported on the REDD+ Technical Annex to the IAP offered to the GCF for the period 2014-2016 period was 165,172,705 tCO2e (Table 1).

These results stem from the gross emission reduction (ER) of CO2e achieved in Argentina's forests and forested land cover: primary forest regions: Purque Chaqueño, Tucuman-Bolivian Rainforest, and the Argentine portion of the Atlantic Forest (a.k.a. Paramarino Rainforest) and Argentine Espinal.

### A*. Achieved volume of REDD-plus results offered in the pilot programme as this proposal

Indicate the volume of achieved results starting on the earliest date that the project was eligible to be included in the pilot programme.

As indicated above, in the period 2014-2016, Argentina achieved the reduction of 165 million tonnes of CO2e. The country will not offer the same volume of ER results offered to other entity or private market scheme for these results.

After having received the IAP assessment report and as a consequence of the communication received from the GCF Secretariat regarding the financial envelope of the GCF REDD+ BRRP, the volume of emission reduction (ER) results available for Argentina is limited to $82,000,000 in this fund. This limit will be applied to the total volume offered to the GCF RBP pilot programme the ER volume experienced by the country during 2014-2016 and the carbon benefits. The volume of ER offered to the GCF is therefore limited to 79,853 tCO2e (after a score of 41/60 in the IAP assessment).

On top of this volume, the country will use other mechanisms to manage the risk of reversals: $7,492,883 tCO2e (49% of the offered volume, as described in Annex A.1) from the GCF REDD+ BRRP pilot programme for 2015-2016.

The country confirms it will not offer the same volume of ER results offered to the GCF (including the volume set aside to mitigate the risk of reversals) to other schemes or mechanisms. The country will use REDD+ results attained

### Emission Reduction Total

Total: **109,458,446**

The increase in emissions due to the loss of native forests in the period 2017-2019 is mainly attributable to the impact of the droughts experienced by the country during this time. In particular, in the provinces of La Pampa and San Luis. In these two provinces, deforestation was identified as the cause of the 95% and 63% of the forest loss, respectively.

The REDD+ results achieved under the project are aligned with C2.2 enhanced response to forest fire (one of the components of the National Strategy for Forest Conservation). The reduction in the annual forest loss was also influenced by the reduction of the export taxes on wood and other products generated from forests of areas for related land use change (mainly in conservation category III - "green" of the Forest Law) as well as the strengthening of the precautionary mechanisms of the Forest Law and its implementation, which are aimed at protecting native forests. This project will support these efforts.

#### B.I.1. UNFCCC Technical Assessment and Analysis process

(1) Consistency of the FREL/FRL with any additional information that represents the information provided in the REDD+ Results Reporting System, the consistency of the FREL/FRL with the GGG inventory, consistency of the FREL/FRL with the GGG inventory, and consistency between the GGG inventory and FREL/FRL; and finally how they will be resolved in the next GGG inventory or FREL/FRL.

On 8 January 2019, Argentina voluntarily submitted its subnational Forest Reference Emission Level (FREL), with a value of 103,416.848 tCO2e, to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).

The FREL was prepared following the guidelines of UNFCCC decisions, particularly Decision 12/CPC 17.

On 9 October 2019, as a result of the recommendations received during the UNFCCC technical assessment, Argentina submitted the revised version of its FREL, which included the level of uncertainty associated with

**Page 7**

The FREL Technical Assessment Report (TAR), published on 23 November 2019, states that the information used by the National Forest Inventory (NFI) is complete, transparent, and in accordance with the guidelines included in the Annex to Decision 12/CP.17.

With regard to consistency of this information with the National Greenhouse Gas Inventory (INGEI), its Spanish version, and the Global Forest Resources Assessment (FRA) 2020, it was noted that the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) for National Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Inventories. Baseline information was used from the FRA 2015 Country Report, Argentina clarified that, although planted forests are reported to the FRA 2015 Country Report, they were not included in the baseline because they do not meet the criteria established by the FRA; GHG emissions from the transformation of planted forests into other use were not considered. It was also noted that the NFI considers forest land as a single category and sub-categories that INGEI considers for land use, being consistent with all FL and OIL data that change into other use (tropicals, pastures). It should also be mentioned that the information reported by the National Forest Monitoring System (Argentina’s NFMN) does not differentiate the final land use.

### Forest Definition

Argentina has constantly minimized the loss of native forests considering the types of land cover that the country reports to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change - through Forest Resource Assessment (FRA), 2000, adapted according to national circumstances.

- **Forest land (FL):** Land constituting a natural ecosystem with a canopy cover of native tree species of more than or equal to 20%, with trees reaching a minimum height of 7 meters (m).
- **Other forest land (OFL):** Land constituting a natural ecosystem with a canopy cover of native tree species ranging from 5% to 20%, with trees capable of reaching a minimum height of 7 meters, or with a canopy cover of more than 30% and trees capable of reaching a minimum height of 1.5 m. It includes areas where native species shrub cover, with shrubs reaching a minimum height of 0.5 m. Included herein are palm groves.
- **Other land (OIL):** Land not classified as FL or OFL, constitute the class Other Land (OL). It includes pasture and other agricultural production, planted forests, inland water bodies, salt flats, areas without vegetation, urban areas and infrastructure.

FL and OFL represent native forests and these two categories constitute the forest definition used in the INGEI, HUR, FREEL, REED+ Technical Annexes and other official reports related to deforestation at the national level.


### Data and Carbon Pools

Dual carbon pools (soil and aboveground) were not included in the FREL because this information is not available in Argentina’s First National Forest Inventory (PNBNA), its Spanish acronym.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND | Page 8 of 14

Although during the technical assessment it was noted that Argentina included estimates associated with soil organic carbon pool in its INGEI, the country clarified that this pool was not included in FREL because the INGEI does not include land use change. Since there is no other INGEI variable to account for variations in soil organic carbon is reported broadly aggregated, in a separate category [INGEI 307] variation in soil organic carbon is not considered in the REDD-plus RBP. As a result of this clarification and that land use change is included, it is not feasible to separate these soil carbon variations for cropland and pastureland. Therefore, the INGEI does not include estimates associated with soil organic carbon pools which have no associated variation in carbon stock.

With regard to NO2 gases, emission from fires are reported in the INGEI, in the assumption that all deforestation is due to fire or other human causes. Although this is normal practice, there is no registry to be able to adjust that assumption. Therefore, NO2 gases cannot included in FREL.

It was noted during the technical assessment that Argentina has not considered land use change as a separate element from INGEI considering, moreover, that fires are one of the main drivers of deforestation and that their inclusion could lead to an overestimation of the FREL. More details on this issue can be found in the main elements addressed in the FREL, BURs and the REDD+ Technical Annex (TA) as shown in Table 3.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND, Page 1 of 4M

In future INGE reports, INDBN2 results will be available for all forest regions by the end of 2020. Furthermore, with a view to obtaining more robust information on carbon pools not currently included in the INGE, the country is carrying out specific studies to quantify their significance in terms of carbon content and also to assess the potential impact of these carbon pools on future REDD+ activities.

With regard to the forest definition, the country has a future operational definition split out in Resolution No. 230/2012* of the Federal Environment Council (COFEMA) — please refer to section C.2 context for further details. This new definition is based on the following criteria: minimum area of 5 hectares (spanning at least 0.5 hectares), minimum height of 3 meters, and 20% minimum canopy cover). This new definition will be applied in all future INGE reports. It should be noted that the current forest definitions for each category do not fully match the definitions being used so far by the country's NMS. In this regard, the aim is to ensure consistency with the national forest inventory (NFI) and the National Forest Monitoring System (INBN), among other reports, without this leading to a loss of currently available information on INI, OHI, and GCI. The new definition will be applied in all future INGE reports for areas above surface area land, and thus, recalculation of GHG emissions and of FRL. Once Argentina's NMS fully adopts the new definitions, this additional information will be included in the FREL update.

So far, Argentina's NMS has adjusted this definition in the Argentine Monte and Andean Patagonian Forests (APF) category for the period 2013-2015. This is reflected in the last INDI annual statistics report for 2018*. Argentina is currently working at the adjustment of forest coverages according to this forest definition also on the remaining categories, which will be completed by mid-2019.

The FREL was built based on historical average of gross CO2 emissions* from deforestation, defined as the shift from a forest (FL and O/W) or to a non-forest (OF), for the historical period 2002-2013. AD used for its preparation are for the delineated area, by year and type of cover, taken from official data reported by the National Forest Monitoring System (INBN) and processed at the provincial level using the methodology of the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Food (MAGyF), and analyzed by the National Directorate of Climate Change (DNCC) based on a model prepared by INB. This model uses adjustment coefficients according to the dates of the satellite images used for each period.

The difference between forest classes (FL and O/W) or FL was drawn using a native forest map depicted on the basis of the 1:50,000 scale of the National Forest Inventory (NFI). The difference between OF and non-native forest cover loss, at an approximate scale of 1:50,000, using the bands for near infrared, mid-infrared and shortwave infrared. This map was processed by INB and used to generate a digital coverage of the native forest in Argentina, with a resolution of 30 meters. The difference between FL and O/W or OF (deforestation) is estimated for the following periods: 2002-2006, 2006-2010, 2010-2014 and 2011-2015. Information was identified at the provincial level and grouped for this report by forest region.

* Available at: <https://www.indb.gob.ar/indice.php?publicaciones/informe_inalambrico_2018.pdf>

**Available at: <https://www.indb.gob.ar/indice.php?publicaciones/informe_inalambrico_2019.pdf>

* The minimum mapping unit used for monitoring the loss of native forests in the period 1996-2006 was 10 hectares. For subsequent years, information was obtained at a greater scale, with a resolution mapage of approximately 4 ha.

### Figure 1: Deforestation Map for the Reference Period 2003-2015

EF (Table 4) were estimated based on the carbon content of aboveground and below-ground biomass, by forest region and forest class (FL and OWL). Carbon contents were estimated using field data surveyed by PINNIN. Databases on diameter, height and species collected by PINNIN were used to estimate the average total volume per hectare for each forest class in each forest region. The total volume was then converted into carbon content based on the average density of each forest class. This information is given as Primary Sampling Unit (PSU) times the average wood density of that PSU, and this information was converted to kilograms of carbon per hectare (kg/ha), using default values provided by the 2006 IPCC Guidelines, for each of the forest regions.

### REDD-plus BRF FUNDING PROPOSAL

#### (a)(3) If a country is considered HFLD: Please provide the basic justification for this classification.

**N.A.: Argentina is not an HFLD country.**

**(i)** **FREL/FRL adjustments for a carbon stock in a HFLD country:** If elements were made, please provide information that the adjustment does not exceed 5% of the carbon stock over the eligibility period in the relevant area and/or exceeds 10% of the FREL/FRL. Please also include any other information that may be relevant to demonstrate that likely underestimate future rates of deforestation or forest degradation during the eligibility period.

**(ii)** **FREL/FRL, in accordance with COP-17:** Please provide any additional information that supplements the data and information used for constructing FREL/FRL. Include whether the FREL was constructed in a compliance with guidance in Decision 2/CMP.9, specifically on the use of satellite imagery and other remote sensing techniques.

**The FREL indicates that Argentina fulfilled Decision 13/CPRF Guidelines, by using the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for constructing FREEL as mentioned in paragraph 5 of the TAR. Data and information used for constructing FREEL is transparent, complete and in agreement with the guidelines of the Annex to Decision 12/CPRF. The FREL was constructed at the sub-national level, applying a stepwise approach as an interim measure, pursuant to Decision 13/CPRF. The FREL was constructed by using data from satellite imagery and other remote sensing information has been included on the most significant activities, most important forest regions and most significant areas for improvement were put forward, as follows:**

- Include the number of forest sampling units in each region (see paragraph 70) that could entail a significant contribution to emission losses, emission reduction from forest degradation in particular.
- Include information about soil carbon pools or more information to justify omission thereof, in the assumption that they are not included.
- Increase the number of field sampling units into those forest regions whose surface area and level of activity are higher than 10% of the total in surveyed information (particularly in Parque Chaqueño region).
- Use data to estimate EF from OWL.
- Use densities by species instead of weighted average density, to provide more accurate biomass values.
- Enhance the scope of FREL to all of the country's forest regions, provided enhanced information is available.

**Most significant activities:**

Regarding the most significant regions included in the FREEL Parque Chaqueño, Missiones Rainforest, Tucumán Bolivian Rainforest and Argentine Espinal (Caldén and Sandhurst districts), they represent the majority of the important forest areas in Argentina. These regions are characterized by high deforestation rates, particularly where the greatest historical forest cover loss occurs and therefore where most of national initiatives for the management of forests have been implemented.

Argentina's NMSs focused on these regions at the beginning due to the quite different social demands and to have information to attend environmental consequences of deforestation. By having strengthened Argentina’s NMSs, it is possible to include other forest regions in the future. The NMSs are being extended into other regions, and work is ongoing to improve information for the Paraná Delta and islands forest region. Annual average value of native forest loss (obtained from data corresponding to 2001 to 2004) is 7.64 ha/yr, which represents a significant reduction in comparison with previous years. The deforestation rate in 2018 in Argentine Monte region represented 1.6% of total deforestation in the country, and native forest loss in Andean forests was 35.9%.

# REDD plus BRF FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND

On the forest land, the remaining forest suffers degradation processes due to livestock use (grazing and over grazing within the forest), agriculture (use of agrochemicals), unsustainable extraction of timber and non-timber products, pollution and invasion of exotic species. Forest degradation is a gradual process that alters its ability to provide ecosystem services. The FREL does not include carbon stocks in degraded forests as this definition that allows monitoring change in state of forests, since it is difficult to select criteria, quantify and locate the forest land with high probability of being degraded. This is why the FREL does not cover this type of forest.

In the future, although a time target has not been established yet, this progress will allow a nation-wide FREL.

## Most significant points

Based on information obtained from the PINDE, the FREL includes carbon pools of above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass (estimated from the fraction of above-ground biomass). Regarding the inclusion of other carbon pools (dead organic matter and soil carbon), there is no information available in the PINDE. The FREL does not include carbon stocks in degraded forests as this definition that allows monitoring change in state of forests, since it is difficult to select criteria, quantify and locate the forest land with high probability of being degraded.

In the case of mineral soil carbon, Argentina has a very low surface area of organic soils and they are located in the province of Chaco. In the case of mineral soil carbon, Argentina has a very low surface area of organic soils and they are located in the province of Chaco. The FREL does not include carbon stocks in degraded forests as this definition that allows monitoring change in state of forests, since it is difficult to select criteria, quantify and locate the forest land with high probability of being degraded.

(vi)FREE/LRIL consistency: The Technical Assessment Report is in relation to the consistency of the methodology used over the time series used for the compilation of the FREEL. If applicable, please provide a plan on how to address and overcome issues that were not material to the compilation of the FREL (TA Report, Section 3.2). This includes data and evidence requirements.

The FREL was calculated based on the historical average of gross emissions of the period 2002-2013. This period reflects country's deforestation dynamic, since the difference in emissions between the years prior to the enactment of the law and those following its implementation after that date. This reference period is also in compliance with GCF requirements to access to RBP.

The TAR mentioned that Argentina submitted a consistent FREEL although the report includes a few areas of improvement for future work:

AD: The technical assessment process identified OWI-related activity data as an area for improvement.
- Better data are expected from information generated by the INBIO to adjust OWI coverage and reduce uncertainty.

EF: With respect to estimating EF for forests, the TAR identified that the use of field data will improve accuracy in estimates compared to the use of biogeochemical information. In this regard, based on information surveyed by the INBIO, Argentina will have new field data for this kind of forest as of the end of 2020 and will thus improve its estimation.

The INBIO is now at the stage of sample pilot installation, data processing, results generation and preparation of the technical contents of regional reports for their publication. So far, 94% of the sampling units have been installed in the Atlantic Forest region. The INBIO has also started to collect field data on forest inventory information, in parallel with the preparation of regional reports to present preliminary inventory outcomes (details are already available for the Atlantic/Amazonian Forests and Northern Patagonia regions). Advancing

### REDD plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

#### IN2N2 - The IN2N2 currently entails a considerable technical effort bearing in mind the scale of work, besides the outlining and application of stringent protocols which lead to obtaining new reliable estimators and updated EF.

**vi) FREE/FRL accuracy:** Please provide are additional information that implements the information contained in the Verification Report (VR) regarding the accuracy of the FREE/FRL. This should include information on whether the data and methodologies used under prior ver- ification were adequate for the purpose of obtaining reliable estimates, as well as any other relevant information related to the VR report that were not material to the accuracy of the FREE/FRL and that could be resolved in the future.

The TAR mentions that Argentina submitted an accurate FREE/FRL, although the report does mention a few areas to be improved in the future for obtaining EF:

- The use of more precise data for each species to calculate volume, which would increase estimates accuracy. The country argued that average wood density values were used for each PESI, resulting in underestimation of biomass and carbon content.
- The number of PESIs installed by the PIN2N is low; an increase in the number of sample plots would allow for a more accurate estimation of biomass from regions with higher GHG emissions from deforestation, such as Parque Chagualcocha.

Argentina has addressed the latter by improving the design of the IN2N2—underway and expected to be published soon. The country also mentioned that it is currently working on developing a methodology for provincial and regional forest inventories. The basic design of the new inventory has a 10 by 10-km square grid, in which each intersection point placed on a forest to be covered by the inventory is surveyed (volume, diameter at breast height, species, etc.) with the aim of estimating biomass and carbon content for purposes such as: Demographic, site (landscape type, altitude, slope, exposure, soil, other living types), and human activity (e.g., logging, fire, grazing). The country also mentioned that it is currently working on including other variables have been included, such as forest biological diversity, conservation status, non-timber forest products, etc. Once the field data has been collected, the information will be processed to estimate biomass and carbon content.

**vii) Sources of emissions:** Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Verification Report (VR) regarding sources of emissions. The country should also indicate whether it is implementing REDD plus activities that have a significant source of emissions included. If they were not, justify whether alternative methodologies are being used to account for these emissions or if the activity will be implemented in future.

The TAR stated that other activities could also be a significant source of emissions, besides deforestation, particularly emissions from forest degradation. The country recognizes the importance of this source and intends to present information on how it is being accounted for in its REDD plus activity.

A series of Projects on Scientific and Technological Research on Sustainable Management of Native Forests are being implemented with a view to obtaining better information on sustainable forest management and its impact on degradation. Specifically, one of the three topics covered by these projects is focused on forest resources monitoring, including land use change and degradation processes, using geomatic multisensor technologies at the provincial scale.

Tests are being carried out at a regional scale in the Andean-Patagonian Forest region, by analyzing time series, with the purpose of identifying gradual changes in forest cover losses and gains. These tests are in line with the objectives of the projects on sustainable management of native forests, as well as with the areas in which these processes could be more relevant, as well as assessing methodologies for their application to other forest regions.

On the other hand, the assessment team identified that a proxy to evaluate the significance of degradation processes in Argentina’s forests could be the conversion from FL to OWL.

### (a) Significant pools: These provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report (TAR). The TAR does not include significant pools for several reasons, namely whether significant pools were not included due to lack of data and/or the omission does not constitute an omission of the entire pool. For example, the TAR did not include soil organic carbon because it was considered a non-significant pool.

As stated in paragraph 40 of the TAR, it is recognized that Argentina included the most significant carbon pools.

FREE includes above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass pools. Litter, deadwood and soil organic carbon pools have not been included due to a lack of robust information for estimation purposes. During the technical assessment process, Argentina provided additional information to justify the non-inclusion of the above.

With regard to soil organic carbon, the country has very few organic soils and these are located in the province of Tierra del Fuego (TF) and in the South Atlantic Islands. TF is not included in the area covered by FREE. In the case of mineral soils, it was noted that Argentina reports consistently on its land, using the IPCC 2006 GLLs. However, there are no data available for the period prior to 1990 (the year when the TAR regarding land use conversion). It is reported in an aggregate manner and does not cover the whole of the country. The country has a large number of forests that have been converted from grassland or savannah. Since these conversions were not included in the TAR, it was not possible to estimate the associated carbon variation.

Some forest types are dominated by above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass, the ratio is 1:82 (4% to 4.12%). According to the first region (using 2006 IPCC default values for litter). Furthermore, it was stated that the deadwood pool does not have IPCC GLLs.

### (b) The National Forest Monitoring System (NFSM)

Within the framework of the project plan, it was noted that at present the NFSM is in the process of gathering information. During this process, information will be collected systematically and in pilot studies on other pools (dead organic matter and soil carbon), once information collection has been completed, it can be analyzed and used to update the methodologies of INGEI and then update the FREE.

### (c) Technical Assessment Report in relation to the inclusion of all gas fluxes that are significant sources or sinks. If any of these fluxes are significant, they should be included as a separate pool in the NFSM. This is due to the lack of data and/or whether the country incorporates emissions or underestimates. Provide also a plan to include all significant fluxes.

The TAR states that addressing non-CO2 gases is an area for future improvement to keep it consistent with INGEI considering, moreover, that fires are one of the main drivers of deforestation in the country and their inclusion would be very important.

FREE includes gross CO2 emissions from deforestation. Non-CO2 gas emissions from fires are not included because available information in the country is not sufficiently robust to estimate only emissions from deforestation. The TAR does not include non-CO2 gases, as it was decided that they should be included in BUR 2 and BUR 3 in the assumption that all deforestation comes from controlled burning. Although this is a common practice, there are no records to be able to adjust this assumption. Therefore, non-CO2 gas emissions were not included.

### (g) IPCC guidance for FREEL/FLR: Please indicate if whether the construction of the FREEL/FLR ideas, methodologies and estimates was guided by 2003 GLLs or 2006 GLLs.

The construction of FREEL was done on the basis of the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for national GHG inventories, consistent with the 2018 INGEI submitted in BUR 3.

### Source: National Directorate of Climate Change (DNBC), based on information provided by the National Directorate of Forests (DNF - MCF).

The data and information provided in the REDD+ TA are in overall accordance with the guidelines contained in the annex to decision 14/CP.5, as a result of the technical assessment process LUCU experts noted that Argentina has not yet implemented the activity reducing emissions from deforestation for 2014-2016 by:

* Using consistent methodologies and data to generate GEF and GEF gross deforestation based on data from Argentina's National Directorate of Forests (DNF) and the same methodology and data for each year in the reference and the results periods, which was based on the same minimum mapping unit of 4-10 ha.
* Using consistent methodologies and data to generate EFs, in particular the same above-and below-ground biomass pools, forest types, land use change functions, and the stratification of the four native forest regions for FL and, for OWL, some the EFs derived from bibliographic review and expert knowledge for each of the forest regions.
* Including only the same GEF carbon sources (CO2e), including the same gases: CO2e.
* Covering the same reference period and land area: the four native forest regions of Parque Chagres, Misiones Rainforest (Paranaense Rainforest), Tucuman Bolivian Rainforest (Argentine Yungas) and Argentine Patagonia.

Assuming that all carbon from both biomass pools is lost after the deforestation event, as detected by visual interpretation owing to the lack of spatially explicit information on carbon content in the final land use, the GEF gross deforestation for OWL and the estimated results for REDD+ consider only native forests; the conversion of native forest into other land uses is not considered.

Using the same forest definition that was as used in constructing its FREL.

### (i) Consistency of the data: 

The LULUCF experts concluded that the data and information provided by Argentina is overall consistent. Argentina provided data and information on all the required elements in accordance with the guidelines contained in the annex to decision 14/C.P.5.

The AD estimates were obtained based on the same four forest regions and the two forest classes (FREL and OWL) reported for the assessed FREL. Deforestation estimates were obtained using the same methods as those used to construct the FREL and the estimated values are consistent with the data and information provided in the technical annexes.

The LULUCF experts concluded that Argentina has provided the same EFs as those used for constructing the FREL for each of the four forest regions considered. According to the technical annex, the EFs used were based on the carbon content of above-and below-ground biomass by forest region and forest class (FREL and OWL). For the FL class, the carbon content values were estimated using field information obtained from a bibliographic review and consultation with experts. During the T.A., Argentina explained that the INDEC provided data for the calculation of the EFs for the FREL and OWL classes in the technical annexes to decide 14/C.P.5, and estimate the specific EFs for OWL.

Overall, the LULUCF experts conclude that Argentina has maintained consistency with the data and information (i.e., AD estimates) used when constructing its FREL and in estimating the results presented in the technical annexes. The LULUCF experts found that the data and information provided in the technical annexes are consistent with the guidelines referred to in decision 14/C.P.5, paragraph 9.

Argumentation provided information related to the uncertainties associated with the estimates of gross emissions from deforestation at the forest region level for 2002-2013 for the FREL and a worksheet**6**for calculating the associated uncertainty of the estimated emissions for 2014-2016. The FREL also provided information related to the uncertainty assessment of the combined sum of errors of AD EFs within each individual deforested area in each forest class (FL or OW) of each forest region on an annual basis, and the associated uncertainties of the estimated emissions for 2014-2016. The LULUCF experts noted that the total uncertainty, combined over all forest regions, is estimated to be approximately 3 per cent.

The LULUCF experts concluded that results are accurate to the extent possible and uncertainties have been reduced, based on the assumptions used. Argentina provided detailed information on how gross deforestation area was determined for each forest region.

Nevertheless, the experts identified the following areas for future improvement:
- Using available species-specific wood densities for deriving the biomass values used to obtain the EF in terms of carbon content.
- Establishing additional sampling units in the forest regions of concern to enhance the accuracy of estimates.

This issue is currently addressed with the INDBN as mentioned above. The updated data and information derived from the INDBN will enhance the accuracy of the estimations for the REDD reporting.

The Forest Law[2] described in more details in section C1 and in section C.2.1 General description of this proposal and in Section 5.4 as a key element for the legal regulation of the granting framework for forest concessions. The implementation of the Forest Law and the budgetary allocation of the National Fund for the Enrichment and Conservation of Native Forests (FNCINB, in its Spanish acronym) by the national government (through the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources) as well as the public participation in the development of conservation and management plans have significantly influenced the results in ERs achieved under this proposal. The reduction of deforestation (and related ER results) derives from several concurrent variables such as other complementary regulations (regulation of forest fires, regulation of CO2 emissions of CEMPA[3] emissions), priorization of crops and livestock products, land value, social mobilization, among others.

Thanks to the implementation of the Forest Law and the mentioned concurrent variables and efforts mentioned above, the rate of deforestation in Argentina has been reduced from 1.5% per year between 2004 and 2016 (see Table B-2.1). The decline in the rate of native forest loss started in 2008, which coincides with the enactment of Forest Law in late 2007, and continued until 2014, although it stabilized at close to zero between 2015 and 2016. This reduction is explained by the implementation of the Forest Law together with other variables, reduced the deforestation rate (information also available in the EAS annex of this proposal).

The total surface of native forest under conservation category (as declared by each jurisdiction) in the Territorial Planning of Native Forests (OTINB in its Spanish acronym), specific information on the OTINB is reported in Table B-2.1, has increased from 30 million hectares in 2004 to 57.8 million hectares in 2016 (the “forest plan”) (preparation of plans and implementation of related special interventions) received a total of ARS 1,311,997,000 (ARS 321,374 MUSD between 2010 and 2015).

During and beyond the period of ER results Argentina - with support from various partners as detailed below - implemented several projects and programmes that strengthened forest governance, increased participatory processes, promoted a full and participatory REDD+ process, positioning REDD+ in the national agenda, and supported the development of conservation and management plans. These actions reduced deforestation reduction across the territory. Such projects and programmes are the Forests and Community Project (FCCP) (co-financed through the National Budget), the Forests and Communities Programme (FCP) (co-funding BIRD TIT0968-2012-P2041), or UN-REDD National Programme (FAO, UNEP, UNDP 15D0372), among others. The Forest Law also supports the Forests and Community Native Forests Protection Programme (UNDP 12-013, financed through the National Budget). A short summary of some key projects/programmes mentioned above is reported herein for ease of reference:

* **Support to the Implementation of the National Native Forest Protection Programme:** strengthen the capacity of communities in the implementation of the programme and its missions and duties commissioned by the Forest Law. In the specific through this project, UNDP supports the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN) in the development of a new regulation for the Forest Law, which was approved on June 2020, with an extension to December 2020. It is worth to mention though that considering the current context, the implementation of this project will be suspended until further collaboration with UNDP (to support MARNs with the management) would not be extended; MAJDS will continue to manage the programme using the funds and their executives.

#### Actions consistent with national forest programmes and relevant international conventions and agreements.

Actions carried out under the REDD+ BIP are consistent with Argentina's National REDD+ strategy (called the National Action Plan on Forests and Climate Change, PAnFCy), its actions under the UN-REDD Programme, and the Law No. 256740, which sets out the framework for the fulfillment of the Forest Law. This Law and its regulations are the guiding framework for REDD+ in the country.

The PanBNC was elaborated in the framework of the National Climate Change Cabinet (GNCC) (in its Spanish acronym). It was prepared and developed by the MAyDES, through the joint efforts of the National Directorate of Forests (DNF), the National Institute of Natural Resources (INAR), the National Forestry Service (DNS) (in its Spanish acronym) and with the support of the Argentina UN-REDD National Programme. It was developed based on a participatory process from 2014 to 2016 and involved relevant stakeholders, including private sector, civil society organizations, small-scale farmers and indigenous peoples. The process was essential to consolidate coordination among different actors in the country's forest governance system at both national and subnational levels.

The PanBNC is structured into three main components: strategic planning, institutional framework, structural and operational pillars. To prepare the PanBNC, drivers of deforestation and forest degradation were analyzed, as well as the current state of forests and their role in climate change mitigation. The National Forest Coordination Committee (CNF) The PanBNC was submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) for consideration at COP21.

Below is a summary of the relevant regulatory framework (domestic law and international law), which supplements actions carried out to achieve the 2014-2016 results.


#### Domestic Law

- **Constitution:**
Action 4 sets the legal base for the country's environmental policy, establishes the principle that natural resources belong to the State and defines the ownership of the property over natural resources in their territories. Actions were carried out according to the provisions of this framework, and coordinated by COFEMA, MARN and provincial environmental agencies (O.N.E.S., in its Spanish acronym) almost at all country's jurisdictions, for the Results Period (this is further explained in Subfigure 10).

- **Law No. 256740:**
On Minimum protection standards for sustainable and adequate environmental management of forests and forest resources, as well as on the creation of a National Forest Management System (hereinafter "General Law on the Environment") (O.G. of 28 November 2002). To this end, the law establishes the following:

- **Law No. 26313/07:**
On Minimum environmental protection standards for native forests (Forest Law) (O.G. of 14 December 2007).

- **Law No. 22558/10:**
On National Parks (O.G. of 12 December 1980). Executive Order No. 43346 on Natural Reserves (O.G. of 28 October 1990) and Executive Order No. 43349 on Strict Natural Reserves (O.G. of 18 October 1990), for protecting these natural reserves.

- **Law No. 2815/18:**
On Minimum Environmental Protection Standards for Forest and Rural Fires (O.G. of 16 January 2018).

- **Law No. 2743/17:**
On the creation of a National Forest Management System, as well as on the establishment at the national level, in coordination with the regional and provincial levels. REDD+ actions in the country are carried out according to this law.

The results achieved by the BIP are analyzed under Subfigure 10 (F) in this document.


#### International Law

- **Law No. 256740:**
On Minimum protection standards for sustainable and adequate environmental management of forests and forest resources, as well as on the creation of a National Forest Management System (hereinafter "General Law on the Environment") (O.G. of 28 December 2019). The Law establishes minimum standards for environmental protection to ensure that the country's forests are managed in an environmentally sustainable manner.

The BIP actions carried out under this law have been implemented at all national territories according to Article 41 of the National Constitution. The Law has the following objectives: it is to establish strategies, measures, policies and instruments related to impacts

### National Forest Governance Framework

The country has a regulatory and institutional framework to promote the transparent governance of native forests, which is in force and applied throughout the Results Period of this Proposal. Moreover, the country has ratified international agreements that establish obligations for REDD+ initiatives.

It is worth noting that the legal framework on transparency and access to information, which may be directly or indirectly linked to native forest management, is binding for public agencies and has its own institutions and mechanisms for implementation and monitoring. This applies not only to the national government but also to all REDD+ initiatives and actions in the country. In this regard, the following institutions, which were fully operational during the Results Period, play a key role:

* **MAyNBR** and the Federal Environment Council (COFEMA, its Spanish acronym) are the most

### Context

The Office of the National Agency (AGEN) (in Spanish acronym), is an oversight body of the National Public Sector, which issues publicly available reports on the Forest Law, including information on transparency and management of funds, access to information, and other relevant matters. The AGEN published its first report in July 2013, and its second report in July 2013-December 2016, on the implementation of the Forest Law, indicating institutional and technical progress made by the National Public Sector.

o The Office of the Comptroller-General (SIGEN) (in its Spanish acronym), is an oversight body of the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources. It has been responsible for implementing SIGEN audits provincial agencies and Mayores to review the allocation of the financial resources allocated under the Forest Law.

o The Anti-corruption Office of the Ministry of Justice and Human Rights develops and coordinates anti-corruption programs. The Office has a web site to report allegations on line.

The Forest Law has provided a framework for the sustainable use of forests and other natural resources, which has improved cooperation between different public agencies gain more experience in this regard. And from 2013, S/N°47/2016 Resolution No. 82/2016 established a new system of compensation for forest users who have been affected by the Forest Law. This system is designed to help verify that the beneficiaries of the resources allocated by the FSCINR maintain or increase the environmental services provided by forests, and that they are those who are certified to compensation, which is awarded based on a works completion certificate.

### Relevant regulatory framework

#### Domestic law

o National Constitution: Article 41 establishes the authorities that must provide environmental information.

o Law No. 27275, on the Right to Access Public Information (O.G. of 29 September 2016), ensures the right to access public information and promotes citizen participation, as well as transparency in public administration.

o Law No. 24583, General Law on Environment (O.G. of 24 November 2002), requires individual or collective entities that carry out activities related to environmental protection, the activities they perform (Articles 16, 17 and 18).

o Law No. 29581, National Forest Code (O.G. of 26 December 2007), requires the Office of the AGEN for the SIGENs to oversee and audit the FSCINR (Article 36).

o Law No. 24583, General Law on Environment (O.G. of 24 November 2002) establishes the duties, prohibitions and incompatibilities when holding public office.

#### International law

o United Nations Convention against Corruption (UNCAC), 2014 (Law No. 26907; O.G. of 6 June 2006).

o The American Convention against Corruption (SACAC), 1996 (Law No. 24579; O.G. of 13 January 1997).

o American Convention on Human Rights, 1969, and International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, 1966.

No. 39 December 2019). GNCC works in the endearment of the Chief of Cabinet of Ministers as a technical body that coordinates the implementation of the National Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation Action Plan, and all of the public policies related to established standards in the Law, such as foster the emission reduction strategies and reduce the human and environmental impact of climate change. The GNCC is composed by representatives from different government levels and has participatory bodies where the annual work plan is discussed and priorities are set for each year (Decree 108/2016). Sustainable Management Plans (MPS): A form of conservation measure category in Chapter 8 of the Law. The goals and units that must always occur are: a) To protect the native forest, b) To ensure the maintenance of the forest category, c) When these activities do not affect them, their nature neither manually or artificially shall be technically assured.

Conservation Plans (CPs): A form for focus on one of the three conservation categories; provide the proposed goals and activities measures and/or increase the conservation achievement.

Formation Project IPs: seek to promote MPS or CPs by allocating funds for the planning process that will ultimately lead to a plan (MSP/CP, 2017).

### Administrative decentralization.

The tools that supported this performance are:

### Mechanisms for addressing claims and complaints

To prevent conflicts and make it easier to address claims and complaints within the native forest policy - Forest Law (Ley de Bosques Nativos) - and for the MAyDS (MAyDS), the following citizen participation instruments have been operational since 2007:

- Mandatory participatory process for preparing OTMAs.
- Mechanism for the preparation of Plans for Land Use Change (PUCs, in its Spanish acronym) in low conservation value forests (III - green), prior to the authorization of the Local Environmental Impact Assessment (AEI).
- COFEMA, as a political-discussion forum at the federal level (and specifically, in Native Forests) that includes representatives from the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources.
- Buena Verde (Green Mailbox): MAyDS has enabled this on-line public consultation mechanism that receives citizen complaints and claims. The system also includes the contact details of each directorate within the MAyDS, in case of more specific questions.
- From Desk of the MAyDS: when a query is received at the front desk, a file is opened through the Electronic Management System (GIDE, in its Spanish acronym) and it is referenced to the corresponding area.

The five-year study (2010-2014) carried out by the NGO Fundación Ambiente y Recursos Naturales (FARN), called "Indicadores de Acceso a la Información Pública Ambiental" ("2017)Indicators on Access to Public Environmental Information", found that the MAyDS has a high level of access to public environmental information, and states that “SADS (...) shows a high response capacity through its participatory mechanisms.”

In 2014, the DNBL of MAyDS created the DNBL Social Participation Unit (APS-DNBL) to support participatory methodologies, and to address claims and complaints, as well as requests for information (more information can be found in the USA annex).

## Tools and Initiatives that helped to implement the safeguards during the Results Period

The instruments and initiatives implemented in Argentina during the period, from implementation of the Forest Law and other actions regarding forest cover change. Below are a few of the most relevant:

- **Argentina National Forest Monitoring System (Agenor's NFMES):** provides updated information on native forest resources in the country since 1986, representing key institutional tool for national decision making and to communicate to society the importance of native forests.
- **The National Forest Management Strategy (NFMES):** is a strategic document that continuously strengthens its technical and institutional capacities. The NFMES serves also to generate information on forest resources and their use in Argentina.
- **National Implementation Monitoring System (NIMS):** is also key for the country to comply data reporting on international conventions to which Argentina has signed up, such as the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change Environmental policies are agreed upon by COFEMA, the Committee on Native Forests within COFEPRIS. NIMS and agrees on strategies and actions for forest resources.
- **Argentina Greenhouse Gas National Inventory System (SINGEAG):** a tool that serves to follow up on the implementation of the Kyoto Protocol in Argentina. It provides information methodologies and emission estimates to ensure the consistency of the reports that are submitted to the UNFCCC.
- **Office of the AGN report on Implementation of the Forest Law:** The Office of the AGN regularly audits the management of forest resources, as well as the effectiveness of the measures implemented available and operates as a transparent mechanism that audits the management of the FENCA. It also provides information about the effectiveness of the actions implemented.
- **Evaluating Implementation Status Report (MAyDS):** is required by the National Congress MAyDS must periodically reports on progress made in implementing the Forest Law across the country and its impact on forest resources.
- **The report covering the period 2018-2019:** is also a key source for analyzing the safeguards.

As indicated in the ESA report (annexed to this document), it is also important that the technical support, capacity building and institutional strengthening of the National Forest Service (AGN) continues to further fulfill this safeguard (see ESA Annex: *Powers and Community Projects* - Argentina UN-REDD Project).

## Respect for the knowledge and rights of indigenous peoples and members of local communities

Taking into account relevant international obligations, national circumstances and laws, Argentina has adopted the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

### In the Results Period, REDD+ readiness actions and those leading to emissions reductions were carried out in a context that respects the knowledge for the recognition of rights and obligations of indigenous peoples and forest-dependent communities. These are to ensure compliance with the pertinent national and international regulatory framework, as stated below (in the ESA Report, annexed to this document).

It should be noted that the Argentine legal framework does not have a definition of "local communities" in the sense of the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples. However, it recognizes "peasant communities", "Circles communities and "family agriculture," among others. Therefore, and for the purposes of this document, the term "indigenous peoples" shall be used to identify and classify local communities.

2. Provide technical assistance to land enforcement authorities on the participatory process, according to the Forest Law.
3. Outline participatory mechanisms, and act in accordance at meetings for National DNR (NDR) 3/19/2018.
4. Manage claims filed with the National Directorate of Forestry by different authorities such as regional OITEN.

With regard to the legal framework for indigenous peoples' rights, the Argentine Republic’s National Consultation recognizes the existence of 35 indigenous peoples (Article 75, paragraph 1) and, also, according to Law No. 23362 on Indigenous Policy (O.G., 8 November 1985) defines the indigenous peoples as “groups of families that recognize themselves as such because they have a common origin, culture, language or religion; are not subject to colonization or colonization; and defines natives or indígenas (“indio” in the original text) as members of said communities.” According to this law, there are 35 indigenous peoples in Argentina. The National Consultation accounts with 31 indigenous peoples’ groups, often also referred to as Argentine Indigenous Nations (Naciones Indígenas Argentinas), which represent a total population of approximately 953,027 persons, or 2.38% of the national population, who self-identified as descendant of or belonging to an indigenous people in the National Census 2010. The largest groups are: Guaraní, 46.5%; Quechua, 19.7%; Guarani, 7.60% in the Daguana, 6.81% to the Kolla, 5.81% to the Quichua and 2.58% to the Weño. These seven groups represent over 84% of all indigenous people living in Argentina. The largest group is the Quechua (304,006 people out of more than 94 years old), 52.8%; are followed by Guarani (104,428 persons of twenty-five and above). The provinces with the highest proportion of indigenous peoples are Chubut (47%), Neuquén (38%), Misiones (36%) and Corrientes (45%). These regions overlap with the highest poverty rates. Indigenous peoples are therefore among the population groups most affected by poverty, lack of access to basic services such as health care, education, in addition to resources, particularly land and job opportunities. According to a study by the Ministry of Health, 70% of indigenous people live in households with less than one room, times lower than that of all the country’s households36.

National Law No. 26150 addresses the law survey of indigenous communities (Extensiones 26554 / 28804 / 23800 / 27900 / 28000 / 28001 / 28002 / 28003 / 28004 / 28005 / 28006 / 28007 / 28008 / 28009) and the emergency of indigenous communities in the country, consistent with Article 75, paragraph 17, of the National Consultation, and to partly fulfill Article 14, para. 3, of International Labour Organisation (ILO) Convention No. 169. This is a law that establishes the survey of the emergency of indigenous communities in the country; says judgment enforcement, court or administrative action, as well as the establishment of an inventory of the land occupied by the indigenous communities and, if necessary, of the currently, traditionally and publicly occupied lands.

Furthermore, there is a map with geo-referenced information on the location of indigenous communities which was developed in 2016. This map is based on the National Register of Indigenous Communities (ReNaCI), in its Spanish acronym) and from the Programme for Territorial Development of Indigenous Peoples (PTDI). The PTDI is a programme carried out by INAI in compliance with Article 3 of Law No. 26146, and in force since 2007; with the purpose of promoting the development of indigenous peoples’ communities through the implementation of projects that are consistent with their cultural identity and the characteristics of each indigenous people community, in each of the provinces) and approved by the National State in 2007. Re NaCI surveys the country’s indigenous communities and, if necessary, their currently, traditionally and publicly occupied lands. The map is based on the information provided by the INAI, which has been updated with the characteristics established by Law No. 26146, it is a first step with regard to the Federal State's responsibility for the land of indigenous peoples' communities. The PTDI is an important programme carried out by this programme is still in force and permanently updated, which is available at http://www.inai.gob.ar/ptdi.

This survey is of utmost importance when considering any action concerning native forests, bearing in mind that 60% of the country's indigenous communities live in native forests. Although land tenure is still not entirely clear for these areas, it has been estimated that 40% of native forest cover is in areas without clearly defined territories; forest dwellers can use them through a management plan approved by the ALA, respecting the general procedures of the

#### Forest Law and UTEN
On the other hand, and particularly concerning forests, the Forest Law (O.G. of 28 December 2017) includes several provisions to promote the participation and to safeguard the rights and livelihoods of indigenous peoples and peasant communities. In this regard, within the framework of the participatory process for consultation with the Indigenous Peoples, the Government of Argentina has issued a draft law that warned that the participation of indigenous peoples was one of the weaknesses of the process. Therefore, it included in the law the obligation to hold consultations with the Indigenous Peoples and to include them in the consultation with the Indigenous Peoples.

Such guidelines, as well as guidelines strictly related to REDD+ mentioned in this PP or its annexes, were prepared by the Working Group on Safeguards (WG) of UN-REDD. The WG is composed of 104 indigenous and Tribal Peoples in Independent Countries (Law No. 24871; O.G. of 07 April 1992), through which government shall consult the indigenous peoples concerned, as provided for by law. In a good faith and in a form appropriate to the circumstances, in the objective of achieving agreement on the subject matter, the WG has provided details on the ILO convention in the next sections and in its REMF paper.

The country paid special attention to the engagement of indigenous peoples and to safeguard overall participation in the REDD+ process. The Working Group on Safeguards was set up, comprising different stakeholders that could contribute their knowledge and experiences to help the country achieve this objective. The WG is composed of representatives from the Ministry of Indigenous Peoples at the Parliament of the Indigenous Peoples of the American Chaco and ZOSUR (Chaco), as well as representatives from the National Commission for the Development of Indigenous Working Groups in Argentina (UN-REDD, 2015, pages 7–8). In response to the above suggestions of the indigenous peoples, the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources created the REDD+ Directorate, and other MAYES technical units, NGOs, INAI, the Ombudsman’s Office – supported by the Argentine National Commission for the Development of Indigenous Working Groups in Argentina within the UN-REDD process, which resulted in the creation of the Working Group (WG) on Indigenous Peoples. The WG is composed of representatives from indigenous peoples and their organizations, Approaches to Safeguards, promoting the engagement of indigenous peoples, and building capacities especially in the context of REDD+.

Therefore, following the ILO 169 convention, of which Argentina is a signatory, the PBNDCs includes objectives that are aimed at ensuring the participation of indigenous communities of all kinds – either forest-dependent and/or forest-dweller. In this regard, one of our PBNDCs strategic structural pillars (UE 2) is to ensure the active participation of indigenous peoples in the design of local community building and strengthening capacities; likewise, another of its pillars, that of Strengthening Governance, promotes and reinforces participatory spaces (UE1, Action 7.1).

### Institutional Framework
In Argentina there is no single forum or organization representing all communities or indigenous peoples across the country, although there are several organizations with a national coverage, including the following:

- **AIREA**: Association of Indigenous Women for the Rights and Development of the Aymara People (non-profit organization that brings together indigenous peoples belonging to various ethnic groups in Argentina). It was created in 1987 by women from the Chaco region. Among its objectives, AIREA seeks to defend indigenous peoples’ rights and develop indigenous communities in all their perspectives including the economic, social, health, language and legal ones.

- **Encuentro nacional de organizaciones territoriales de pueblos originarios (ENOTOP)**: In the Spanish-speaking region, a space grouping up 45 indigenous peoples territorial organizations (IPTOs) in Peru. ENOTOP put forward proposals and coordinating indigenous territorial policies at the national level, with a view to strengthening their capacity.

- **The Organization of Indigenous Nations and People (ONAI)**: Founded in 2003 is one of the first organizations to represent several communities. It aims to politically and technically strengthen indigenous peoples' organizations, as well as to promote dialogue between them and other actors (governments, international cooperation and private companies). By the time of the results period for this proposal, ONAI will have been recognized by indigenous people's organizations from 15 provinces in Argentina.

- **Consejo Nacional de la Mujer Indígena en Argentina (CONAMI)**: Founded in 1996. CONAMI gathers together women from different regions and ethnic groups of the country, with a view to affirming the identity and strengthening indigenous women's organization. The organization has managed to establish a dialogue on gender issues at various levels of the civil society as well as national and international organizations.

Indigenous peoples' organizations with a regional coverage amongst many, include the following:

- **Organización de Comunidades Aborigenes de Santa Fe (OCASATEF)**: The Organization of Aboriginal Communities of Santa Fe is an indigenous organization that represents communities throughout the province. The organization is involved in the recognition of the rights to legitimate land ownership and biological diversity.

- **Consejo de Caciques de la Nación Maya Guarani**: The organization is active in the recognition of indigenous lands, territories and related natural resources.

- **Asamblea Pueblo Guarani**: Founded in 1986, the Assembly strives for the inclusion and participation of Guarani communities in decision-making processes at all levels.

- **Confederación Mapuche de Neuquén**: The Neuquén Indigenous Confederation is the legal representative of the indigenous peoples of the province. It is a non-profit organization independent from the Government and is representative of the Mapuches of the province. It participates in meetings and seeks dialogue with the Mapuche communities.

- **Consejo Indígena de la Selva del Valle de la Provincia de Salta -Quilmes**: Founded in 2007, the Quilmesara is made up of 80 Kollas communies and its objective is to protect, conserve and sustainably use the selva territory; including water, land, biodiversity and everything it contains.

The country has institutions made up daily or partly of indigenous peoples and forest-dependent communities, to facilitate their participation. The following are two worth highlighting:

- **Indigenous Peoples' Rights Unit (UPRI)**: Created in 2016, the UPRI is a public entity with enforcement authority for Law No. 23304 on Indigenous Policy and Support to Indigenous Communities and it is a key public institution of the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources. The UPRI has as its objective to represent representatives of indigenous peoples, together with those from different ministries and secretariats of the adhering provinces. INAI's overall objectives are related to tenure rights, education and health plans for indigenous communities, and the promotion of gender equality in indigenous communities; particularly relevant for respecting indigenous peoples' communities' rights.

It is also worth noting that the National Institute of Indigenous Affairs (INAI) has, in this regard, in 2016, INAI implemented the project Awareness-raising and Promotion of Gender issues in Indigenous Communities.

- **Indigenous Peoples' Council (IPC)**: Created in 2004 in the remit of INAI. It is a part of the Indigenous Participation System at focus on the national level, and includes representatives of indigenous peoples from all regions of the country. The IPC has as its objective to represent and speak on behalf of the indigenous peoples. IPC was created with emphasis "the urgent need for participation of the indigenous peoples' organizations" (INAI Resolution 152/2004), especially "for indigenous peoples".

### FAO Environmental and Social Management Guidelines (2015)

The guidelines facilitate the early and systematic identification and assessment of environmental and social risks and the integration of management measures into project design. They are applicable to many layers of its geo-referenced information system, one of them allows the location of communities and indigenous peoples within a country.

### FAO Policy on Free Prior and Informed Consent (2016)

The policy has been formulated so as to ensure that FAO will make all due efforts to respect, include and promote indigenous issues in relevant work. In doing so, it joins the international community's increasing mobilization in favour of the rights of Indigenous Peoples.

### FAO Manual for Project Practitioners on Free Prior and Informed Consent: An Indigenous Peoples Right and a Good Practice for Local Communities (2016)

The manual is deeply rooted in a framework that recognizes the right to free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) as an essential human rights principle to respect the right to FPIC when developing and implementing projects affecting Indigenous Peoples.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy (2018)

This GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy recognizes that indigenous peoples are often marginalized by national policies and societal structures. They are also national societies and are disadvantaged by traditional models of mitigation, adaptation and development. The policy aims to ensure that the rights of indigenous peoples are respected in all aspects of GCF's engagement with them.

### GCF Environmental and Social Policy (2018)

The policy articulates how GCF integrates environmental and social considerations into its decision-making and operations to effectively manage risks associated with adverse impacts and improve outcomes. It includes a specific performance standard on indigenous peoples' rights.


## Legal Framework

During the Reporting Period, the country had the following relevant regulatory framework in place regarding indigenous peoples' rights:

### International Law
- ILO Convention No. 169 of 1989 concerning Indigenous and Tribal Peoples in Independent Countries (Law No. 2043/07 of 1 April 2002) recognizes the right of indigenous peoples to "maintain, control, protect and develop their cultural heritage" and their traditional knowledge (...). It importantly, the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP) (Law No. 2438/09) grants the right to participate in the adoption or decision-making that may affect their rights. Article 18 and ILO convention states that State Parties shall adopt special measures "to safeguard" their peoples, institutions, property and cultural heritage. The UNDRIP also provides for the participation of indigenous peoples in all matters within the scope of this Convention (...). Governments: (a) shall consult the peoples concerned, through appropriate representative bodies, with due respect to the will of the latter; (b) shall not impose legislative or administrative measures which may affect them directly; 2. The consultations carried out in application of paragraph one shall be conducted, as a minimum, on an individual basis and, where appropriate, in good faith and in good faith and in good faith and in accordance with international human rights standards, with the objective of achieving agreement or consent to the proposed measures.

### National Law
- Argentina's Constitution (Law No. 26044) of 1994: Argentina commits to respect, preserve and maintain knowledge, innovation and practices of indigenous and local communities concerning conservation and sustainable use of biological diversity.
- United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP): Argentina voted in favour of the UNDRIP. The UNDRIP defines and defines the individual and collective rights of indigenous peoples, including their rights to participate fully in decision-making processes related to employment, health, education, and other subjects. The objective of the declaration is to encourage countries

### Domestic law

- **National Constitution**, which recognizes the ethnic and cultural pre-existence of indigenous peoples (Article 75, paragraph 17).
- **Law No. 26084 on Indigenous Communities** (O.G., of 3 November 2006) defines indigenous peoples as “groups of families that recognize themselves as such because they are descendants of the original inhabitants of a territory and whose culture is based on their ancestral traditions”.
- **Forest Law** (O.G. of 26 December 2007) establishes that any native forest clearing or sustainable management project shall recognize and respect the rights of the indigenous communities that have traditionally occupied such territories.

### Executive Order No. 91/2009

- The Forest Law (O.G., of 26 December 2007) establishes that any native forest clearing or sustainable management project shall recognize and respect the rights of the indigenous communities that have traditionally occupied such territories.
- **Executive Order No. 91/2009** regulating the implementation of the Forest Law (O.G. of 16 February 2009; Article 3) establishes that any native forest clearing or sustainable management project shall recognize and respect the rights of indigenous communities.

### Other REDD+ Initiatives

- In Argentina, in line with the country interpretation of safeguards, “the implementation of the PANDCC and other REDD+ initiatives must be carried out in a participatory manner involving all relevant stakeholders, in particular of indigenous peoples and local communities, with a gender perspective.” The General Secretariat of the National Forest Law (O.G., 26 December 2007) establishes that indigenous peoples are entitled to participate in the formulation of forest policies and express their opinion on administrative procedures involving overall preservation and protection of the environment, whether general or specific in nature” (Article 19).

### Participation within the framework of the Forest Law

- The Forest Law (O.G., of 26 December 2007) establishes a set of rules and mechanisms to promote multi-stakeholder participation through different key stages of implementation of its scheme. For instance, Article 6 states that the OTBN should be done in a participatory manner, and Article 15 establishes that “any clearing or sustainable management project shall recognize and respect the rights of indigenous communities.” The General Secretariat of the National Forest Law (O.G., of 26 December 2007) establishes that public participation is mandatory and must be ensured by the provinces for the following: (a) preparing and updating the OTBN (Article 4b), (b) before authorizing forest clearing projects (Article 26b).

### Participation in the Forest and Community Project

The Forest and Community Project was approved in November 2015 (end date forecast for November 2026), with the objective to implement the Forest Law, Chaco Laws, and Missions. This has provided MAyDES and sub-national agencies with important inputs and capacities in participatory processes with forest-dependent communities through “Integral community plans” (ICP) in Spanish-speaking provinces and “Community Forest Management Plans” (CFMPs) in indigenous languages.

A National Advisory Council (ANC), set up to ensure participation, and a Local Advisory Council (LAC) in each province are the main bodies of coordination. The LACs are made up of local institutions dealing with forests, land production, and by representatives of indigenous communities. The Forest and Community Project has an ICP in a time-ministerial working group to bring about synergies for the project’s implementation.

The CFMPs are being prepared in each province, with the aim to go on to be implemented by the communities that are interested in participating in the project. To CFMs provide an initial territorial framework and a long-term vision requiring commitment, certifying that it is principally accepted at institutional and organizational levels. The first phase of the process includes technical assistance for grant formation.

When the CFMPs are established, they are submitted to an agreement to promote through community meetings—participatory processes, applying a gender approach, to achieve the intended territorial management of forests in accordance with the Forest Law. The CFMPs will be developed based on the policy on Community Forest Management, framed within the Forest Law promoted by MAyDES. Since these are forest communities that have been working for decades and have their own experience, the Forest and Community Project has a portfolio of 80 PICS, reaching out to over 2500 families (60% of them are indigenous).

### Participation in defining Strategic Guidelines for the Forest Law Implementation

The strategic technical guidelines for the implementation of the Forest Law were prepared and agreed upon by consensus, through a participatory process with government institutions, academic, scientific experts and civil society organizations. The guidelines were presented to the National Cabinet held on 2015-09-30.

The process resulted in the definition of five strategic guidelines that are the target of the FNECBIN and were adopted by MAyDES:

1) Sustainable Forest Development;
   - 1.1 Forest Management at Basin Level;
   - 1.2 Forest Management with Integrated Livestock;
2) Restoration of degraded forests;
3) Sustainable use of biodiversity and strengthening of conservation areas;
4) Decentralization of forest management;
5) Urban – Forest interface.

The PANBioCC was designed following a broad participatory process undertaken by MAyDES, supported by the Argentine National Forest Fund. The Province of Argentina also received funding from the FCPC. Both initiatives—headed by MAyDES together with UN agencies and World Bank—particularly highlighted the importance of participation in the design phase.

Participation and consultation became more focused specifically on building and consolidating the PANBioCC, which was presented to the National Cabinet held on 2015-10-30. The National Cabinet formed the Climate Change Cabinet, which was formalized through the Law No. 27520 for Minimum Standards for Global Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation approved at the end of 2019 (and originally formed in 2016
