GENDER APPROACH IN THE FANCC

The Gender Approach has been working for quite some time on mainstreaming gender in different public policies. In this regard, the following institutional and regulatory frameworks are worth underlining:

o Law No. 26485, on the Full Protection for Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women, which establishes a National Plan to Prevent, Punish and Eradicate Violence against Women in Argentina, to ensure the rights recognized by international conventions ratified by this country in this field and to promote gender equality.

o Law No. 27118, on Family Agriculture (O.G. 28 January 2015): its objectives are, inter alia, to contribute to the improvement of family agriculture; to ensure that women have access to land and the rights and benefits enshrined in this law; tailoring actions and implementing specific policies for women; and, furthermore, to reinforce spatial social mobility within family peasant and indigenous agricultural communities.

o Law No. 25490, on Mandatory Training in Gender Issues for all National Officials working in the Three Branches of Government (O.G. 18 January 2013): this law establishes a National Plan for Institutional Training in Gender and Violence against Women, with a view to "training and raising awareness among public officials about gender equality."

Echoing these efforts, MAPPES, with the support of the Argentina UN-REDD+ developed a roadmap in 2015 to mainstream gender in the PanCIC ("") and organized the first workshop on"mainstreaming gender in forest policies" (O.G. 3 February 2016). Over two years later, this workshop, which was held in June 2017, reviewed the main gender regulations within national and international frameworks and their link to forest policy.

### (v) That actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity, as well as with the promotion of sustainable use of forest resources that is not used for the conversion of natural forests, but instead used to incentivize the protection and conservation of these ecosystems, while enhancing social and economic benefits.

As required by Article 4.1 (c) of the Forest Law and any other REDD+ initiative in Argentina must be consistent with the Forest Law, incentivize the protection and conservation of native forests (i.e., natural ecosystems), biodiversity, as well as enhance the environmental, social, and economic benefits.

REDD+ actions, which are the purpose of this proposal, were implemented in accordance with the legal framework established by the Forest Law. The Forest Law views about diversifying social and environmental benefits for the owners of native forest management plans, forest species, and related natural ecosystems through the different tools included in the Forest Law.

In Argentina, native forests cover an area close to 536 million ha. Argentina's wealth of biodiversity is expressed by its high number of endemic species (10% of all known species) and its unique flora in Antarctic and coastal marine environments, among others. To date, Argentina's Biodiversity Inventory (until different information is available).

The Argentine National Constitution states that it is the day of the authorities to preserve the "natural and cultural heritage and biological diversity" (Article 41), and the main goal of the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) is to conserve biodiversity. The Forest Law seeks to preserve natural forests and categorizes them by conservation value categories through a process called forest zoning, which designates forest zones with prior authorization and approval of the relevant Plan on Land Use Change, and an environmental impact assessment.

The Forest Law also includes key legal definitions:
- **native forests**: natural forest ecosystems that predominantly include native tree species, diverse vegetation types, and are part of the surrounding environment - soil, subsoil, atmosphere, climate, water resources - that constitute an interdependent network with its own characteristics. These forests have a high degree of biodiversity, are naturally self-regulating, dynamically balanced and provide different environmental services to society, including diverse natural products (e.g., timber, fruits, nuts, medicinal plants) and non-material benefits.
- **secondary forests**: secondary forests where no human intervention has occurred; secondary forests which grow after clearing, and forest regeneration that occurs in areas of degraded land. These are also called "regrowth forests" (Article 2, paragraph 10 of the Forest Law). Based on these definitions, the Law categorizes forests according to their conservation interest as:
    - Category I: **forests with very high conservation value** that should not be transformed.
    - Category II: **forests of medium conservation value** that may be degraded but if reforested could recover their original state. These are also called "regrowth forests" (Article 2, paragraph 10 of the Forest Law). Once these forests have been certified as sustainable use, tourism, gathering and scientific research are permitted.
    - Category III: **forests with low conservation value** that can be partially or totally transformed but only after an environmental impact assessment.

# REDD plus BHP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Environmental services of natural forests: the "tangible and intangible benefits generated by native forest ecosystems (...)", including water regulation, conservation of biodiversity, fixation of greenhouse gases and others (Article 1). As far as I am not in conflict with this article, this could serve as a logical basis for REDD+.

The definition of conservation categories for native forests (red, yellow and green) requires methodologies that valorize and weight the Environmental Sustainability Criteria (ESC) associated with their connection to other natural resources and ecosystem services.

In line with the analysis carried out by MADS in early 2017, between 2010 and 2016, the ESC most frequently used by the provinces to validate the importance of native forests for their OTBN are: CVs (basis conservation), Biodiversity (basis conservation), Water regulation (basis conservation) and Ecosystem services (basis conservation). This shows that biodiversity, within the protection framework laid down in the Forest Law and, therefore, in the context of REDD+, is a key criterion for the conservation of native forests. It also highlights the co-benefits of REDD+. Additionally, to strengthen biodiversity conservation within the framework of the Forest Law, it would be advisable to consider the inclusion of the "Guidelines for Implementation and Methodological Guidelines for Implementation", which served -like one on the Forests- as a basis to promote ecological corridor planning when updating OFIs.

It is important that all actions taken by provincial governments in decisions within the framework of the PANSBC are designed to be consistent with, and support the country's regulatory framework for the protection and non-exploitation of native forests. This includes the conservation of biodiversity and other social and environmental services. Furthermore neither the PANSBC nor the Forest Law include afforestation or reforestation with exotic species. By Law there is a prohibition on the planting of exotic trees in areas where native forest exists, unless it is for the purpose of 2500 ha of Forest Plantations denoted the necessity of respecting the OFIs. Likewise, REDD+ actions in the context of the PANSBC must be consistent with the objectives and principles of the Forest Law, which protect natural ecosystems and, therefore, lose of their biodiversity and ecosystem services, for example, by afforestation or reforestation with exotic species (in areas where native forest exists) or by logging (and beyond) were in line with such safeguard or else there would have been non-compliance with the scheme of the Forest Law and other above-mentioned regulatory frameworks.

### Endangered tree species

Argentina ratified the Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (CITES) in 1987. As a result, the following species are included in Appendix I (highest level of protection): Fitzroya cupressoides (alerce), Pilgerodendron uviferum (agüita de las Guayanas) and Araucaria angustifolia (araucaria). The following species are included in Appendix II: Cephalotaxus harringtonii (cervo) in the Argentine Fagus region. Appendix III (medium level of protection) includes Bahiaea samanoides (bahiaea), Podocarpus nubigena (pino de las nieves) and Podocarpus totara (totara). The latter includes continuous monitoring of export volumes and interventions involving Cedrela fissilis (cedro mesquitero) in the Paranáense Rainforest region, and Cedrela angustifolia (cedro satélite) and Cedrela odorata (cedro de la sierra).

With regard to Palo Santo, the Forest Management Requirements for the Exploitation of Palo Santo (Subsection 10.2.3.4.1 of the Forest Law) are in line with the requirements established by Resolution 593/2013 and are in line with supplementary provincial regulations that substantially reduced exports and increased the sustainable use of the species (Table 9). Furthermore, during this period, MADS assisted the three provinces in the development of a training program for forest workers on the subject. This also training, strengthening and adoption of practices to enable a sustainable management of this resource.

## Tools and Measures to be Implemented in the Areas Safeguarded by the Results Period

Some of the most relevant projects that contributed to implementing this safeguard are:

### Forests and Community Project
Among other purposes, this project strategically targets sustainable production and marketing of native forest goods and services, with transformation and multiplication of community-based enterprises. The project also aims at promoting and respecting and promoting biodiversity conservation. The project focuses on the Purae Chapueiro region (Eastern Amazon).

### National Native Forest Research and Development Programme
An initiative to strengthen forest governance that COFEPA declared as federal environmental interest (Res. No. 206/13). In response, MADES has developed a national strategy for the sustainable management of native forests in Brazil. The project focuses on "Economic and social aspects of use, transformation and marketing of native forest-derived resources."

### Argentina UN-REDD National Programme
Between 2015 and 2017, MAyDS, with the support of UN-REDD NP, and the technical support of the World Conservation Monitoring Centre (UNEP-WCMC), developed a national strategy for the sustainable management of native forests. The project focuses on "Economic and social benefits of native forests, together with other entities and stakeholders in the WCA." The project also aims at reducing deforestation and increasing forest cover while improving environmental benefits (by reducing carbon emissions) of native forests in each of the forest regions of Argentina. The strategy includes a national inventory of native forests and protected areas for the PANACC in order to maximize forest benefits, beyond those of carbon. The Booklet on "Safeguarding the Amazonia and other Brazilian forest regions" may be downloaded from the Country Approach to REDD+: Safeguards webpage.

Some of the tools are listed below:

- **Amazonas Forest Information System (HIS)**: under development in compliance with the CBD Article 7. It was established to initiate the process of gathering, classifying, organizing and providing to the community biological information on protected areas under the remit of the National Parks Administration. The system will later be expanded to include the rest of the country.

#### Actions to address the risks of reversal

Addressing the risks of reversal of emissions requires measures to ensure long-term permanence of ER and an increase in the resilience of forests. The Forest Law provides a framework for addressing these risks through its approach to safeguards[1]. Safeguards have measures in place to tackle the risks of reversals of emissions released through the FCPF and other BRRF+ initiatives*, that were operational during the Results Period.

To that end, one of the goals of the Forest Law is to conserve, regulate and control the decree in native forest surface areas in the country, with instruments and tools that minimize or avoid the risks of reversal of emissions.

For this purpose, the National Government allocates 30% of the resources of the Fund provided for in the Law, to develop and maintain the monitoring network and NEMIS (Article 15). Between 2014 and 2016, ARS 285,360,681 was allocated to this purpose. This amount is used to develop a system of forest information systems at a national-level, thus strengthening the mechanisms to avoid and/or reduce the risk of reversal (Table 19).

### Forest Fires

One of the risks of reversal that was identified was forest fires (see Annex 5: Analysis of Risk of Reversal and Buffer Estimation). In Argentina, forest fires are due to both natural and anthropic causes. The most significant cause is anthropic, which includes uncontrolled fire for land preparation, as well as lack of rainfall, high temperatures, low humidity, conductive frost and strong winds affect fire spread and impact. The National Forest Fire Management System (Sistema Nacional de Manejo de Incendios Forestales) Programme of the DSN of the MYADS, which maintains and publishes Forest Fire Statistics since 1993. State authorities report forest fires to SINAGIR (Servicio Nacional de Gestión Integral del Riesgo in Argentina), however it does not differentiate the exact number of hectares burnt due to anthropogenic causes from those caused by natural phenomena such as lightning or drought. The National Action Plan for Forests and Climate Change reports the combined total area burnt. The Sistema Nacional para la Gestión Integral del Riesgo (SINAGIR) recognizes that 95% of the forest fires are linked to human negligence – in conjunction with natural phenomena such as drought, lack of rainfall or high temperatures. Anthropogenic causes include cigarettes, badly extinguished campfires and un-controlled fire for preparation of land for grazing.

Part of this reduction could be due to the additional efforts to combat forest fires with support of the Forest Law. Some of the measures used by local authorities to reduce forest fires include the installation of water tanks (AGN, 2019) and firebreaks maintenance in key locations, together with establishing and reinforcing firefighting teams.

In terms of reporting contributions of fires to national GHG emissions, forest fires are reported in category "3.C.1.a - Biomass Burning in Forest Lands (Native Forest)" of the INGEI. Considering that the National Forest Fire Service is not a public entity, specific actions were taken by the service to distinguish it from natural burning (see paragraph above), but according to the INGEI considers the same burnt surface as due to anthropogenic causes. According to the latest INGEI for the year 2016, emissions from fires (Biomass Burning in Forest Lands) accounted for approximately 3% of total national GHG emissions (8.5 Mio tCO₂e), which is less than 1% of the national GHG emissions (0.85 Mio tCO₂e).

Despite the direct contribution of forest fires to the INGEI is limited, fires and especially inefficient fire management practices are a major source of land-use change caused by livestock or agriculture in the country, and Argentina put in place various mitigation policies and measures.

To avoid and mitigate these risks of reversal, one of the goals of the Forest Law is to conserve, regulate and control the decrease in native forest surface area across the country, with institutions and tools to minimize or avoid those risks. Specifically regarding forests, Article 26085 of the Law of 1983, sets up a “Permanent National System” (SNEF) for the conservation of native forests. The SNEF is structured in three levels: (i) the national level; and fight forest fires (... ) in native forests and other non-urban ecosystems with their three operational levels:

(i) National Coordination Office, which coordinates the actions of the different actors involved as well as warning and informing the Regional Coordination Office what resources the jurisdiction has assigned to combat forest fires. The National Coordination Office also provides technical support for the implementation of the SNEF.

(ii) Regional support from the National Fire Management Organizational Unit, through the regional coordination office, which is responsible for the planning, organization and equipment of other jurisdictions in the region;

(iii) Enforcement jurisdiction, which acts as case of default that exceeds the required proportion of resources used in previous stages, with prior agreement of the authorities in the affected jurisdiction.

There are also other elements of Argentina’s regulatory framework which support forest fire prevention and control:

* Argentine Criminal Code (Law No. 11379): causing fires in forests, prairies, trees or bushes, among others, is a crime punishable by imprisonment for three to ten years (Art.VI).

* Forest Law No. 26533 states that native forests degraded by fire or other natural or anthropogenic events are subject to the same protection as those not affected by such events, according to the category defined in the OITP (Art.40), as a measure to avoid speculation or intentional forest fires in order to obtain benefits.

* Law No. 26562 (Dec.16/2010) on Minimum Environmental Protection Standards to Control Burning Activities, aimed at prevent fires, environmental damage and risks to public health and safety. The use of fire for agricultural purposes is prohibited throughout the national territory (Art.3). Penalty fines are established.

Regulations related to forest fires:

It is worth underlining that during the Results Period, some of the resources of the FNECNB (the National Fund for the Enrichment and Conservation of Native Forests) were allocated to improve the SNEF in different areas.

The relevant institutional framework to respect this safeguard includes among others:

* **MADS:** is the enforcement authority of the SNEF throughout the country, responsible for operational firefighting. MADS was created by Decree 706/2020 on August 2020. This transfer allows for enhanced coherence between forest prevention and control policies, considering that the MAYDS is the authority in charge of the National Forest Fund (FNECNB) and the SNEF. The FNECNB provides contributions and collaboration with provincial jurisdictions through the COFEFA.

* **DNCC:** under MADS responsibility, DNCC is responsible for managing Argentina’s NIFs. Within the framework of the SNEF, DNCC is responsible for fire prevention, monitoring and control in the areas where it has jurisdiction.

* **DNCC under MADS:** DNCC is responsible for the SNE+GEAR and for calculating, compiling and reporting data to UNCCED, as well as monitoring of NIF mitigation measures, among others.

* **National Parks Administration:** with competence in the SNEF, it is responsible for prevention in the areas under its jurisdiction.

Furthermore, there are international forums to reach regional agreements which are essential to maintain the forest cover across the country. Of these, COFEFA and the GNCC can be highlighted.

## Tools and Initiatives that Contributed to Implementing the Safeguard in the Results Period

### Argentina: NMSIS

The system provides updated information on native forests resources in the country, which is a key element for Argentina's commitment to comply with international conventions to which Argentina is a Party, such as UNFCCC, including monitoring and reporting obligations. The data generated by Argentina’s NMSIS is made available to the provinces before it is published to ensure their participation in the process of updating the national forest inventory and other relevant bodies.

Argentina’s NMSIS has a web portal[1] which includes information on native forests and climate change. The portal enables access to available forest coverage information (with layers of land use, deforestation, etc.) and provides tools for users to assess their compliance with this safeguard. The portal allows users to discover, visualize and download the content. The tool, which is very relevant for this specific safeguard, also facilitates collaboration to avoid deforestation in areas where there are high levels of risk of displacement of emissions.

### Actions to Reduce Displacement of Emissions

The displacement of emissions refers to a situation where efforts to reduce emissions in a certain area result in an increase in emissions in another area (for example, an initiative to avoid deforestation in one province brings about a rise of deterioration in a neighboring province).

According to Argentina’s National Interpretation of Safeguards[2], the country has measures in place to reduce displacement of emissions. These include the implementation of the FANBCC and other REDD+ initiatives.[3] In the understanding that to mitigate the risk of displacement of emissions, it is necessary to consider the potential for deforestation in adjacent areas when designing projects, a series of workshops and studies were carried out for the design of the FANBCC, and regional workshops were held with the participation of representatives from working groups on forest management and deforestation.

During the results period, but also in subsequent years, actions have been taken to reduce the risks of displacement of emissions in its legal framework and, particularly, a monitoring system for deforestation was established. This system is based on the use of remote sensing technology to identify the most significant deforestation drivers; it may be safely stated that the risk of emission displacement in Argentina is mitigated also due to the physical characteristics of the country.

From a legal framework perspective, the Forest Law addresses this safeguard where the scheme provides for application of measures and/or restrictions on activities that prevent the displacement of deforestation to other forests that fall under any of the three conservation categories defined by the law (red, yellow, green).

From the physical/geographical perspective, as detailed in footnote 7 of the REDD+ TA, the country assumes there is no risk of deforestation shifting from one forest region to another (and especially to regions not covered by the FANBCC) because the characteristics of the forests and their soils are such that the same productive activities cannot be carried out in different forest regions. Specifically, the advancement of the agricultural frontier into areas with high levels of native forest cover, is very specific to regional, climate and soil conditions (see Figures 182 and 183 of the NMSIS). The maps in Figure B.3 provide more evidence in this regard, showing the Soils, hydrology and Isolocha Maps of Argentina.


---
**Page 49**

As indicated in the previous safeguard, Argentina has a very advanced NFMS, including an Early Warning System and tools for users that are currently using NAIFS to be able to continue monitoring forest fires and other natural disasters.

It is also worth noting that since November 2018, Argentina's NFMS has extended monitoring of native forest loss to the Argentine Music and the Andean-Patagonian Forest regions. The system monitors deforestation in these areas by comparing satellite images with a reference image taken before 2005. It was found that only 1.7% of native forest coverage loss occurred in the forest regions of the Andean-Patagonian Forest and Amazonia.

### Tools and initiatives for implementing this safeguard

- **OTBN:** Forest mapping at the provincial level, within the framework of the Forest Law, which includes information on forest types, their uses, and their conservation categories (red, yellow, green).
- **Argentina's NFMS and web portal.** See above, section on safeguard (v)
- **Early Warning System (EWS for deforestation):** The system is operational since November 2018 and issues warnings every 16 days that forwards to the provinces to take action if a deforestation event has been detected in their territory. This tool allows early warning of forest fires, used to control and prevent illegal deforestation. All alerts (100%) are validated and processed in a GIS system by the National Forest Service (SNAF) and forwarded to the provincial authorities.
- **Finally,** a report with the list of warnings is sent to each province together with a request to report where they have detected forest fires or deforestation events. The provinces must provide information on what, were, what instrument allowed them, the dossier number, and the measures that will be taken in the case of a fire or deforestation event.
- **The EWS was expanded from the Chaco region (region I) to include the entire Chaco region (region II and Semirad, in 8 provinces: Salta, Jujuy, Tucumán, Formosa, Chaco, Córdoba, La Pampa, and Neuquén).** The system also includes a tool for monitoring forest fires and deforestation from declarations. Currently the EWS was expanded to other areas of the Parque Chaqueño region (and Corrientes in Corrientes province) and some area of the Argentine Tungas region (provinces of Tucumán and Jujuy). The EWS foresees the progressive inclusion of the rest of the forest regions.

### C.12 Stakeholder involvement

*There is sufficient evidence that the Country safeguard report was made transparently available to stakeholders.*

The development of the PASBIFC and the crafting of the National Interpretation of Safeguards greatly benefited from the full participation of the Working Group on Safeguards and Social and Environmental Benefits (WGSSEB). The WGSSEB is a multi-stakeholder group that includes representatives at all levels, including representatives from MAsDFS, GNCCE, COFEMA, MTGF, NGOs at national and provincial level; academia; and technical institutions) that could contribute knowledge and experience to enable the country to develop its safeguard system. The WGSSEB also provided input into the development of the DPNS (in its Spanish Acronym) under MAsDFS, other technical areas of MAsDFS, NGRS, NGOs and the National Interpreters of Safeguards. The WGSSEB has been instrumental in providing feedback to the country and disseminate and explore in depth issues related to the Country safeguards. Likewise, the workshops and multistakeholder meetings held during the development process (see section 2.1) have provided input (already mentioned in previous sections), and which continued taking place after the Results Period, were often multi-stakeholder ones for this purpose.

Thanks to these meetings and dialogue forums, Argentina was able to move forward in the development of its Country Approach to Safeguards (CAS), which includes the following elements: National Interpretation of Safeguards, Safeguard Information System (SIS-AR), Summary of Information on Safeguards, and Tools for implementing safeguards on the ground.

## The CAF and its components were prepared following a broad participatory process headed by MAJDS, through DNCC, together with DNBX, with the support of the Argentina UN-REDD National Programme. The participatory process included meetings of the WG on Safeguards and Social and Environmental Benefits, and other relevant stakeholders such as indigenous peoples, civil society organizations, the private sector, and the country’s forest regions.

More specifically, CAS is the output of a broad dialogue process with key stakeholders from governmental, social and environmental organizations, the private and academic sectors. Throughout different stages, technical aspects were discussed, such as the national interpretation of each of the REDD+ safeguards, the application of the PAnBCCC strategy, the identification of the most relevant forest carbon stocks in Argentina, and also the review and inputs of technical studies and relevant practices concerning the purpose of this document.

Through this process, the country identified, organized and made available transparent and accessible information on the Cancun safeguards and was able to submit its First Summary of Information on REDD+ Safeguards to the UNFCCC in December 2010. Additionally, Argentina’s CAS web site has public available information on the national interpretation of each safeguard, as well as a summary of the PAnBCCC strategy that develop as more experience is gained in implementing the PAnBCCC on the ground. Furthermore, a series of tools to support the implementation of REDD+ safeguards and the PAnBCCC strategy were developed and are actively used on the ground. (For more information see: First Summary of Information on REDD+ Safeguards.)

A prioritization process of the PAnBCCC strategic operational pillars was undertaken in the second round of dialogue, which resulted in the following prioritization:

1. The provinces prioritized “land planning” pillar and 83% prioritized the “sustainable forest management” pillar for their respective forest areas. The importance of working at different scales (from local to national) is highlighted.
2. The importance of working at different scales (from local level, within family systems, at the municipal level and between provinces) was also highlighted, with special focus on participation and attention to indigenous peoples’ rights.
3. The provinces prioritized “Restoration and Recovery” pillar and 11% prioritized “Prevention of forest fires” pillar for their respective forest areas. The importance of working at different scales (from local level, within family systems, at the municipal level and between provinces) was also highlighted, with special focus on participation and attention to indigenous peoples’ rights.
4. Finally, 8% of the provinces prioritized the “Conservation in Productive Landscapes” pillar.

The components of this REDD-plus BHP proposal (described in section C.2) were defined largely basing on these results and considering the dynamics of drivers of deforestation and forest degradation, together with the current forest management policies.


## Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits

### C.2. Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits

Provide a description on how the proceeds will be reinvested in a context consistent with the country’s NAP, national REDD+ strategy and/or other relevant plans or strategies. The use of proceeds must be consistent with the terms of reference for the BHP Pilot Programme adopted by Decision 18/3 R.07.

Forests in Argentina are composed mainly either of native or planted forests. The latter are cultivated with exotic species, mostly for timber production for commercial purposes (mainly production of timber and paper). Due to its readiness phase to access REDD+ results-based payments, and potentially during a long process of commitment to the BHP Pilot Programme, it is expected that the use of proceeds will be mainly related to native forests. However, other types of forest such as plantations or even mixed forests could be considered within the REDD+ framework.

As a consequence, to be fully consistent with and support the implementation of the PAnBCCC and country’s NAP, the use of proceeds must be focused on either: (i) native forest, directly or indirectly addressing related drivers of deforestation and degradation, including boosting the growth of the native forest mass and ensuring regeneration of native forests in the framework of integral community Plans (PICs);


---
**Page 51**
---

Public policy implementation related to the management of native forests is in the remit of MAyES, as National Enforcement Authority (ANA) in its Spanish acronym) for the Forest Law, and the program entrusted to foster the sustainable use of natural resources is the Management of Agricultural Technology (INTA in its Spanish acronym). In the framework of the BIRP project, considering that it will entail activities related to forest management with integrated livestock (MIGR), made up of MAyES, MAGP and INTA, as well as implement the activities included mainly in components A.1., and A.2., that is to say, within the framework of the component A.1., the project will be implemented by the National Technical Committee on Forest Management with Integrated Livestock (MIGR), made up of MAyES, MAGP and Argentina Institute for Agricultural Technology (INTA in its Spanish acronym).

Additional information can be found in the specific sectors of this funding proposal (C.2.2 - Outputs and outcomes, and C.2.5 - Implementation arrangements).


Context: Argentina, its native forests and the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation

In the Argentine Republic, native forests cover an area of 53.6 million ha (as of 2018) distributed across seven ecoregions. The most important are: Paranaense Forests, Chaco Wetlands, Andean Patagonian Forests, Pampas Grassland, and the Argentinean Yungas (Tucuman Bolivian Rainforest), Misiones Rainforest, and Andean Patagonian Forests. These ecoregions have specific characteristics, dynamics and experience different human and natural pressures, as reported more in detail in the Environmental and Social Management Framework (Annex 3 of this funding proposal).

The prevailing causes of deforestation and forest degradation in the country are related to the expansion of the use of forest land for agriculture and livestock farming. This is the consequence of a competitive and profitable agricultural sector that has been able to expand its production area and increase yields by being benefited by the incorporation of new technologies and high relative prices of agricultural products worldwide, which have made it possible to obtain higher returns on investments in this sector than those considered marginal for agricultural production. Traditionally, livestock farming in forests is carried out through extensive systems that are characterized by low productivity per unit area, low stocking density, and low cover of adult tree species without regeneration capacity. These productive systems are developed with intensive use of forest resources, which makes them very characteristic by a high fragmentation of the forest landscape, the loss of native fauna habitat and the release of large amounts of greenhouse gases. Besides, these systems are simplified and inefficient, with high energetic costs, without control and monitoring of the system components.

On the other hand, illegal logging is a historical practice that has led to the development of poorly controlled

Other drivers that put pressure on forests are related to demographic growth, urban sprawl and very large real estate developments, and forest fires — both natural and human-induced. All these factors are related, as uncontrolled urbanization can lead to the expansion of agriculture and livestock production, illegal logging and services; legal uncertainty regarding land tenure; weak control and oversight policies and lack of institutional capacity.

Additionally, the Forest Law also created the National Fund for the Enrichment and Conservation of Native Forests (FNECNB), intended to establish a compensation mechanism by jurisdiction, for the purpose of incentivizing the conservation of native forests in Argentina. The FNECNB is administered by the National Forestry Service (DNB in its Spanish acronym) within the Ministry for the Environment and Sustainable Development (MAESDS in its Spanish acronym) and the Local Enforcement Authority (ALA in its Spanish acronym) in each province, which are responsible for the distribution of funds to the provinces. The FNECNB will finance forest management measures, which could ensure the continuity and enhancement of the forest cover in accordance with the Forest Law.

The distribution of the FNECNB is carried out annually among those provinces that have their OTIN approved by provincial law and accredited by the ANA. For its distribution, COFEMA has established a methodology for calculating the amount to be allocated per province, based on the total area of each province; the ratio between the total area of the provincial territory in its native forest areas and the conservation categories defined as OTIN.

Every intervention carried out under the FNECNB must be authorized by the ALA through Conservation (CP), Management (MP) or Land Use Change Plans (LUCP) presented by forest users/owners and signed by a proper professional. Every other intervention—which does not follow this process—is considered illegal, especially those that could lead to deforestation. The FNECNB funds are allocated in accordance with the type of plan, which can be financed by the (FNECNB) including formulation plans (FP); while LUCP and its formulation, are not allowed under the Forest Law.

The table below shows a brief description of the types of activities that can be carried out:

| Type of Plan | Description of the activity |
| --- | --- |
| Conservation Plans | Plans aimed to forest conservation and enhancement, including recreation, subsistence and research activities. |
| Management Plans | In addition to conservation, forest management activities that involve the commercial exploitation of non-timber products can be carried out, and the harvesting of timber is allowed under certain conditions. |
| Land Use Change Plans | These plans allow deforestation activities for agricultural or urban purposes, but must be presented accompanied by an environmental impact study and submitted to a public hearing prior to their approval. |

Source: National Directorate of Forests, MAESDS

The management and conservation plans implemented with FNECNB funding are uploaded into the Integrated Forest Information System (SIFOR) annually by each ALA, and thus ANA has information on types of plans in each conservation category, amounts allocated, holders and responsible technical teams. The ANA also provides support to the provinces for the formulation of management and conservation plans, and the ANA also carries out field monitoring through the territory-based teams of the DNBF in the different provinces.

From the very beginning of the Forest Law and FNECNB's implementation, supplementary regulations were passed related to funding and its allocation under the Federal Environmental Cost (COFEPA) (created in the framework of the retention of the environmental contribution that the federal governments achieved, one of the main objectives of the National Strategic Structure for a Sustainable Development approved by Resolution 2016/2017), as it facilitates comprehensive planning of the territory at different levels. On the other hand, the FNECNB is an important tool to support the implementation and monitoring of the process of consolidating its National Climate Response Plan as the main mechanism to comply with the commitments undertaken by the country in the revision of the NDC. Argentina was the first country

### Sectional parts are an essential part of this instrument, including the National Action Plan on Forests and Climate Change (PANBCCC), which is considered to be the country's REDD+ strategy and was presented to UNFCCC in 2013. The document outlines a series of actions that will contribute to achieving the objective, which spells out the agricultural sector response options both regarding mitigation and adaptation, and which is classified into structural pillars and operational pillars.

Native forests play an essential role in fulfilling Argentina NDC through the implementation of the PANBCCC; this establishes specific measures so as to mitigate 27 MTCO2e by the year 2030.

The PANBCCC is a public policy instrument and an operational management tool with the overall objective of strengthening the capacity of forest-dependent communities in order to contribute to the forest-dependent communities to climate change. A contribution will be made to diminishing GHG emissions from deforestation, degradation, and forest fires through the restoration and rehabilitation by managing, restoring, and recovering degraded native forests. The Plan defines strategic pillars for actions which are classified into structural pillars and operational pillars (see Figure 4 in section C.1 (iv)). Strategic pillars are defined as a set of objectives that will be achieved during the period of implementation, through different actions. They include cross-cutting actions that are necessary to reach the objective and are not directly linked or considered as emission reductions.

- **SSP 1:** Strengthening of governance.
- **SSP 2:** Strengthening of local communities.
- **SSP 3:** Strengthening of monitoring, reporting, and verification capabilities.
- **SSP 4:** Recognition of the importance of native forests as an asset for society.
- **SSP 5:** Knowledge and capacity building.

Strategic Operational Pillars (SOP). These define specific actions that are linked to a concrete mitigation measure that has been considered for the NDC. They include actions that amount to direct interventions in native forests and have associated mitigation measures:

- **SOP 6:** Land use planning.
- **SOP 7:** Restoration and management of native forests.
- **SOP 8:** Conservation in productive environments.
- **SOP 9:** Prevention of forest fires.
- **SOP 10:** Prevention of forest fires.

The PANBCCC is consistent with the General Procedures and objectives of the Forest Law and the Strategic Guidelines mentioned above.

### Social and economic context of forests in Argentina

The 23 departments of Argentina are subdivided into departments, of which 67% have native forests and are part of the OTBN.

According to the National Population and Household Census 2010, it is estimated that 21,528,088 inhabitants live in these departments (54% of the country's total), which in turn is divided into urban population (87%) and rural population (13%). The rural population is concentrated in and around the OTBN.

By cross-referencing radio data with OTBN forest boundaries, the following data is obtained:

- 6,064,919 inhabitants (13% of the total country) live within the forest reserves.

* The population living in native forests landscapes is distributed: 61% in urban sectors (small towns or urbanizations - within the forest landscapes) and 39% rural (10% grouped and dispersed).
* The rural population that depend on forests for their livelihood is a 1,992,875 inhabitants (54.4% of the country's total).


Also, the latest census of indigenous peoples (2010 Census) identified 955,032 people who recognized themselves as members or descendants of indigenous peoples, with 31 different indigenous ethnicities*, occupying a total area of approximately 78 million hectares. The majority of this population is concentrated in the northwestern part of Argentina, where 64% of them live. The indigenous population lives in districts with native forests, and 80% of the indigenous rural population lives in the forest.


Using the National Registry of Indigenous Communities (Re.NCi) and the Territorial Survey of Indigenous Communities (Re.Tc.Ci) and linking them to the OTBRE, it is inferred that a 2,128 (65.4%) communities live in the native forest or less than 10 km from it; while in the rest of the country, are distributed the remaining 839 communities. The Re.NCi-Re.Tc.Ci official report showing areas with presence of indigenous peoples is available [here](https://www.argentina.gob.ar/indigenas).


The 2010 national census also indicated that the departments with native forests account for 63.5% of the total population, and 74.8% of the indigenous population.


Also, it is estimated that in 71.2% of the districts with native forests, the SBN percentage goes over the national average, which is 12.4%. Crossing data on the migration balance between 2001-2010 population censuses by departments, prepared by Allan BH (2013), and native forests loss from the same or near period, generated a negative correlation of -0.75. This means that in those departments with high deforestation rates, that negative immigration balance mainly occurs in departments with deforestation, as shown in Figure 1. It is not surprising to find that these are precisely the areas where there is a greater concentration of indigenous peoples.


There are many reasons lead to assume that it also related to weak land tenure and UBIN that forces people to migrate in search of other opportunities to settle.

**Parque Chaqueño region supplies 90% of the country's forest products (pods and posts, tannins, firewood and charcoal). The 2015 National Census of Sawmills indicates that 100% of the sawmills in the Parque Chaqueño region are small-scale, with an average capacity of 30 tons per year.**

Forest-dependent communities in the Parque Chaqueño region are among those with the highest percentage of land and indigenous families in the country. They derive approximately 40% of income per ha of land from forest products.

Finally, according to Argentina's Native Forest Cover Monitoring Report for 2016 issued by the Unit for Managing the Forest Ecosystem System (UMFSE) at DNII, in the provinces of Santiago del Estero and Chaco, approximately 40% of deforestation results from livestock grazing. The forests are used as grazing land (thinning and burning of trees), with a significant impact on biodiversity (Figure 12). The Forest Law entailed a paradigm shift in the use of land across the country and is the main legal instrument, together with other concurrent variables, to reverse native forest cover loss trends at the national level (as can be seen in Section B.2. Table 8). However, during the first 10 years of its implementation, the Forest Law has not been fully implemented due to lack of resources and capacity to expand the scope and thus obtain even better results.

a) Low access by the indigenous peoples and local communities to the benefits granted by the Law. One of the reasons for this is that the issue was addressed within the framework of COFEMA and, as present Resolution 277/2014 allows the holders of management and/or conservation plans to have a forest concession, it is necessary to establish criteria for the preparation of these plans targeting these communities. This could be due to various situations such as lack of inter-institutional coordination or insufficient resources in terms of human capacity, financial resources, or possibilities or lack of technical support to accompany the communities in their preparation.

b) High proportion of Management Plans (MPs) under a supralocal system. Silvicultural management plans are eligible under categories II and III of OFIES. However, the intensity of interventions performed in forests with low density of trees is not always appropriate for these categories. In fact, some shrub layers are completely lost, as is a significant portion of the lower diameter classes in the tree layer.

## Green Climate Fund

### Page 9 of 134

#### c) Territorial dispersion of plans: Forest Law Funds were mainly allocated to the private business sector through successive provincial calls for plans, where the forest holders submit Formulation Projects (FPs), (APA), and/or Management Plans (MP). The project aims at strengthening the capacity of these actors striving to provide a response to annual demands that result in a scattered impact across the entire OTBIN region. This is particularly relevant when considering the context of native forests in Argentina, as well as the socio-economic baseline of the native forest regions included in the GCF (section 2 and 4) - annexes I of this funding proposal.

### Objectives of the use of the proceeds in this funding proposal

In light of the above considerations and taking into account paragraphs above, the Project will support the implementation of selected pillars of the PANBFBCC, contributing to the continuous reduction of deforestation, degradation, protection and enhancement of carbon forest stock through different forest management practices. The project aims at strengthening the capacity of actors striving for compliance with the achievement of the NDC and of sustainable development in the country. As mentioned in paragraphs above, the Argentina REDD+ process works within the context of native forests, and not only plantations.

In doing so, the project aims at addressing selected drivers of deforestation and degradation: Sustainable forest management practices; strengthening the control over deforestation and degradation by strengthening economic and social opportunities (and diversified livelihoods), strengthening the control and oversight capacity at provincial and national level, and strengthening the overall forest governance.

More in the specific items included in this proposal details (section 2.2.1 seek to strengthen the initiative of the Forest Law Fund (FLF) as a key instrument for the implementation of the National Technical Guidelines that were designed as a SPV by the PANBFBCC at the same time that SSPs are being developed and implemented, with the aim of reducing deforestation and degradation according to OTBIN. This focus has been decided considering that this Category represents 60% of the country’s forest area and is the most vulnerable one in terms of deforestation and degradation. Illegal logging is still ongoing and management schemes currently being implemented (for example traditional agroforestry) are not always sustainable, and need to be adapted and must be enhanced so as to achieve reduction in forest degradation levels and impacts on the forests.

The increase of deforestation due to extensive agriculture for soybean production was the main boost for the enactment of the Forest Law. Since the enactment of the Forest Law, deforestation for agricultural purposes, such as soybean expansion, has been reduced by 90% (see Figure 1). The FLF is a key instrument in forests under category III (green). The Forest Law permits livestock activity under silvicultural management plans that are developed and implemented with the objective of reducing deforestation. This can be implemented through practices which are impacting the native forests. Considering the strategic value in conserving these areas, the project will support the implementation of forest management plans by enhancing practices, facilitating and boosting sustainable and forest-friendly approaches, preventing deforestation and degradation, and promoting the adoption of best land use and forest management practices. By focusing on this key category, the project will also prevent potential “opening” towards livestock (illegal) soybean expansion.

On the other hand, forest harvesting is an important economic activity in regional economies and especially in the framework of the Forest Law implementation throughout Argentina.

## Summary

Priority will be given to actions aimed at strengthening regional economies through sustainable forest management by adding value to goods and services from native forests, with special attention to forest-dependent communities who have no access to formal economies, offering legal access to their natural resources and guarding their livelihoods. The proposed actions are expected to contribute towards improving the efficiency and competitiveness of micro and small industries, and guaranteeing products harvest, management and processing in a sustainable manner. They will also contribute to the conservation of native forests in an environmentally sound; transforming silvopastoral management practices into sustainable livestock and forest use systems, while ensuring that these activities are compatible with the preservation of biodiversity.

The purpose of the actions is, to increase coverage of forests under sustainable use, as well as under protection, by promoting the implementation of forest management plans at landscape-scale. This will reduce forest economies and activities with impacts on land use change as livestock expansion. In addition, the risk of fires in native forests will be reduced through the development of fire prevention strategies and the consideration of great importance for the country; for this reason fire prevention will be addressed through a transparent and participatory process involving all stakeholders concerned. The project will also contribute to resources at local level, through the ALAS, to develop and implement strategic plans for fire prevention.

Furthermore, with this proposal it is expected to strengthen forest governance and the monitoring and control capabilities of the National Forest Service (SINAFOR).

The intention of the project is to put under management plans an area close to 4,549,075 ha of native forests, which represents 8.5 % of the total area of the OFEN. A considerable amount, taking into consideration that during the last decade the area under forest management has increased from approximately 12% to an area now up to 3,000,000 ha. The use of processes are expected to be used in the following four components:

### A. Territorial forest management

This component aims at landscape-scale forestry planning at two levels: i) forest-based defined around value chains and multifunctional agreements; ii) and collective territories under a participatory process involving all stakeholders concerned. The implementation of forest management projects/interventions on forest land use, and the design and implementation of integral community plans (PACs in its Spanish acronym). PACs are an innovative proposal that seeks to promote the sustainable use of native forests by means of the collective use made of forest lands by local and indigenous communities. In addition, to promoting sustainable use of native forests, it is expected to contribute to the conservation of biodiversity, promote the growth of the forest mass as well as its regeneration and restoration. Community actions related to the management of native forests will be carried out in close collaboration with the following beneficiaries: forest producers; micro and small sawmills and carpentry; logistics providers; indigenous peoples and communities.

### B. Forest management with Integrated Biodiversity (MIBG) in Spanish acronym). This component aims to transform traditional silvopastoral practices towards models that allow livestock grazing while maintaining the sustainability of native forests. Such systems will be designed, implemented with the participation of all stakeholders concerned; including those with interested farmers located in II and III categories of OFEN. Main beneficiaries: small to medium cattle farmers.

### C. Enhanced response to forest fires

This component is intended to implement strategic provincial plans for the prevention of forest fires in priority regions and reinforce initial response capabilities in forest fire management, as well as improve the capacity of communities and indigenous peoples in planning. Main beneficiaries: provincial authorities; forest holders; communities and urban society. It is worth noting that the actions related to or catalytic towards enhanced response to forest fires are also included in project components A and B. The component will be implemented through the development and implementation of management guidelines at the basin, community or farm level. They focus on adopting best practices, addressing the causes of forest fires; including forest fire prevention and support services. It is expected that all actions will be carried out with the beneficiaries: Component A and B. It will contribute to address the antiques theme of forest fires. Component D will also contribute to strengthening initial response through improvements of the early fire detection systems at national and local level.

**V. National and provincial government institution technical capacities to address drivers of deforestation, to monitor forest cover and control are strengthened**: This component entails enhancing local and national monitoring, oversight, technical and institutional capacities, including a capacity enhancement for the use of remote sensing technologies (RSMS) and GIS, as well as training for an enhanced early detection and prevention of fires, and the Safeguards Information System (SIS-AIR). The resources will be used to strengthen forest governance at national level through investments in human and supplementary strategic investments as well as knowledge management. Main beneficiaries: national and provincial governments, civil society organizations, indigenous peoples, and local communities.

These components will be implemented using the general procedures provided by the Forest Law and its monitoring and control instruments. The theory of change of the project is included in Annex 6 of this proposal.


#### Benefit-sharing scheme

The full available funding from the GCF results based payments (total Project budget) is planned to be used in a 94.7% for investments at provincial level and for strengthening capacities at national and provincial level – as detailed in the use of proceeds and in the investment framework sections C and D of this funding proposal. The remaining 5.3% will be allocated to the implementation of the benefit-sharing project, including due diligence with rules and procedures and with an appropriate monitoring and evaluation system.

For the 94.7% of the budget for the proposals referred to above, the project will promote articulated schemes between the ANA and the ALA and territorial stakeholders such as municipalities, public agencies, private sector, indigenous peoples, and local communities. The benefit-sharing scheme is based on a share of the proceeds of this Project envisaging the allocation of funds in follows (as shown in Figure 13):

- **72% allocated to investments at territorial level** in forest regions and provinces, and to strengthen capacities of communities, indigenous peoples and smallholders (components A, B, C).
As mentioned above, these investments will be used for a variety of activities aimed at decreasing deforestation and forest degradation – and related carbon emissions - at terminal levels in forest regions, implementing the territorial strategy. These include: strengthening local governance; improving livelihoods at different levels: forest basins, community territories and private farms (small and medium holders); promoting sustainable land use management practices; supporting the development of agroforestry, tree crop, and livestock production systems; promoting forest-based enterprises and job creation, large-scale planning and the transformation of livestock practices in native forests. A portion of the funds will be allocated to support the implementation of the territorial strategy (components D and comp. C), therefore supporting protection of forest carbon stock. Lastly, a 25% of the total budget funds will be dedicated to strengthening capacities at provincial level for monitoring and control as well as capacities through equipment purchased for forest inspection and strengthening of outposts and checkpoints.

- **28% allocated to strengthen technical capacities** of institutions at provincial and national level to address drivers of deforestation, to monitor and control forest cover, to enhance and operationalize safeguards, and to support the implementation of the territorial strategy. These investments will also include boosting women’s engagement (component C).

The 28% of funds for strengthening technical capacities are allocated to activities carrying out several activities that will also have an impact on the ground, such as implementing forest extension programmes, strengthening monitoring instruments at national level and supporting the development of a national REDD+ strategy. These strategic investments in support of forest basins or to the enhancement of PICS and the implementation of a project for the conservation of forests.

Allocation of funds to the local level will be prioritized in accordance with the areas where the drivers of deforestation are exerting the most pressure on native forests.

### 10.3 Project overview

The Argentina REDD-plus BRP project (period 2016-2019) has been designed and planned to contribute to the implementation of the PANDHCC and to the achievement of Argentina NDC.

The Project will reduce emissions from land-use, combat deforestation and forest degradation and promote sustainable forest management, conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stock (not through forest plantations). The project will focus on the natural forests in OTNII Category II (Yellow), where most of the deforestation of the country is occurring (Fig. 14), with the aim of boosting a successful implementation of the Forest Law and empowering local stakeholders (communities, indigenous peoples, small and medium-sized landowners and forest users) to manage their natural forests in an environmentally sustainable way with social participation, promote roofing through improving the livelihoods of rural communities, and boost a change in the livestock management pattern that guarantees the sustainability of the forage. By promoting the use of non-timber forest products (NTFP), by increasing the productivity of the land and using value with favor approaches that favor rural communities, small landowners and taking into account the rights of indigenous peoples, and by providing access to credit, information distribution and legal access to resources in an equitable and fair manner, the project will promote the inclusion of forest users in the decision-making process. By promoting a more efficient use of natural resources, instead of converting forests to other land-use.

The implementation of the project will be focused in the native forest areas in OTNII Category II (Yellow), where most of the deforestation of the country is occurring (Fig. 14), with the aim of boosting a successful implementation of the Forest Law and empowering local stakeholders (communities, indigenous peoples, small and medium-sized landowners and forest users) to manage their natural forests in an environmentally sustainable way with social participation, promote roofing through improving the livelihoods of rural communities, and boost a change in the livestock management pattern that guarantees the sustainability of the forage. By promoting the use of non-timber forest products (NTFP), by increasing the productivity of the land and using value with favor approaches that favor rural communities, small landowners and taking into account the rights of indigenous peoples, and by providing access to credit, information distribution and legal access to resources in an equitable and fair manner, the project will promote the inclusion of forest users in the decision-making process. By promoting a more efficient use of natural resources, instead of converting forests to other land-use.

### Considerations on post-covid-19 recovery

Conscious of the potential important impacts the current international and national health emergency caused by the COVID-19 pandemic will have on human lives, livelihoods, and worldwide economy, in addition to the tragic health consequences that populations in the world are currently facing, the Project is planning to mainstream its actions, considering for the support of a potential post-covid-19 recovery phase.

While it is more realistic that plans for the recovery phase will be formulated at project inception phase, so as to be able to count with more realistic and updated information on the potential socio-economic impacts in Argentina, the Project is committed to contribute to the universal call of "building back better" by boosting and fostering a sustainable development approach. In this post-covid-19 recovery phase into a real opportunity to achieve transformational and sustainable change at local level.

With its strong approach and emphasis on engagement of local communities, small-holders and indigenous peoples, as well as the promotion of agroforestry systems, the Project is committed to contribute to the development of more sustainable practices of livestock and forest management. The Project already represents an important contribution to enhancing livelihoods in rural areas.

### Components, outputs and activities general description

On its tenure system, the Forest Tenure Act (2015) for forest tenure FAO says: "The condition of forests depends on how they are used and managed, which in turn depends largely on who has rights to their resources. These rights can be formal or informal, and may include ownership rights over land and resources as well as usufructuary rights to land and resources (and usually) set conditions on those who hold land. Tenure regulates access to, and the use of, resources. Tenure arrangements may involve exclusive rights (where only one person or group has access to a resource), joint-use rights (where two or more people have access to a resource), or shared rights to the same resources. Forest tenure is a broad concept that includes ownership, tenancy and other arrangements for the management of forest resources."

Furthermore: "A forest tenure system that is clear, fair and appropriate for local conditions is more likely to result in SFM and lead to a consistent reduction in deforestation and forest degradation because secure

These considerations are the basis of the present proposal, since as mentioned in previous sections, all interventions in the forests must be authorized by the ALAs of each jurisdiction through forest management plans that ensure the conservation of biodiversity, ecosystem services and the provision of goods and services perpetually. This requires specific studies on the potential for forest use and multiple agreements between users, communities, and authorities.

It was also stated that forests in Argentina are subject to various forms of tenure, which are admissible under the Forest Law as long as the ALA considers that the proponents meet the conditions for the approval of a management plan. This is particularly important for forest-dependent communities.

The activities will address preparation and implementation of Management Plans at Landscape Level through sustainable land use planning (SLUP) and territorial planning, ensuring legal access to resources for forest residents in conditions of social vulnerability and local economies based on forests. The objective is to strengthen the capacity of forest-dependent communities at the community or collective territories level to strengthen the permanence of vulnerable communities in their habitats, ensuring forest management plans that are sustainable over time and adapted to the socio-economic context, security, and farm-level management plans to improve silvicultural practices and interventions on private producer farms that put greater pressure on native forests.

While components A, B, and C stimulate, plan and execute interventions at territorial level to further reduce deforestation and degradation of forest resources in Argentina, they also aim to strengthen the capacity for knowledge, tools and capacities in monitoring, forest governance, extension safeguarding, and access to markets. The results will be monitored by the ANA, which is responsible for reporting them at a territorial level, which will be channeled from strategies conducted by the ANA in agreement with the ALA. For instance, component A aims to reduce deforestation and degradation of forest resources through reducing deforestation or enhancing protection of native forests of indigenous communities and rural women. Such activities are carried out at the provincial level (e.g., Picos/Forest) and at the territorial level due to the fact that the ANA can promote initiatives that, although at original stage are not directly prioritized by the provinces. The same applies for component B, which is related to the implementation of the Project, or the MIGBI technical agreement. In this sense, the ANA is also responsible for improving - in accordance with the Forest Law - the capacity of forest-dependent communities and rural women to achieve the environmental and social objectives of the Forest Law. Based on the aforementioned, action D.2.6 aims to have a reserve fund for larger territorial projects that involve more than one jurisdiction (province), such as should be the case when implementing the Project in Picos/Forest or in other provinces where there is no forest free activities at local level (as per Picos/Forest) but implementation of a pilot programme aimed at reducing deforestation and depend on forests to promote FSC where communities have not been reached through the province.

On the three components that stimulate, plan and execute interventions at territorial level and number of beneficiaries reached, and prioritizes the well-being of local communities through management, improvement of living conditions and access to markets. The objective is to strengthen the capacity for knowledge, tools and capacities in monitoring, forest governance, extension safeguarding, and access to markets. This also considers the important role that local communities could play in the territorial defense of forests. Following this approach, they are also key partners in the fulfillment of the objectives.

Component B engages less forest-dependent actors, but some of those who have the greatest influence on deforestation and degradation of forest resources. ### COMPONENT BY COMPONENT DETAILS

#### A. Territorial forest management

This component includes planning and, especially, the implementation of forest management projects at two levels: the basin level (A1 – "sustainable forest basins"), as well as the design and implementation of Integral Community Plans (PIC in its Spanish Acronym) (A2) with an integrate landscape approach. The objective is to ensure that the sustainable use of forests, including the benefits of the Forest Law to local communities, indigenous peoples, forest producers, micro to small sawmills and carpentry, and agroforestry.

The component consists of enhancing socio-economic value of the forest sector in the landscapes/forest basins, and boosting the conservation and protection of natural resources as a mean to recover forest regions' economies (which, as indicated in the context section of this document is where the RBP will be implemented). The objective is to make the forest more attractive and profitable. Making it more attractive and profitable a use of the resource will also contribute to combat deforestation and degradation.

This component will operate at two levels: regionally, promoting sustainable forest management at the basin level; and locally, at the community territorial level, through the implementation of PICs. It is expected that this component will be implemented in the following areas: Chaco Chaparral, Argentine Yungas, Argentine Monte, Andean-Patagonian Forest and Misiones Forest.

The territorial forest management plans and the Integral community plans (PICs) that will be developed, selected and implemented through Component A are expected to contribute to addressing drivers of deforestation and degradation in the basin areas. These include: illegal logging, over-exploitation of livestock and fire risk. At both levels, actions aimed at preventing forests fires and increasing the capacity to respond to them should also be considered.

These management plans will have a three-fold positive effect on local communities: i) addressing the drivers of deforestation and degradation; ii) promoting sustainable forest use in the basin areas (A1); iii) helping to contain "income-led forced migration" from the forest regions towards other areas of the country.

**Output A.1 “Sustainable forest basins” for timber and non-timber products produced**

In the framework of this output, the concept of a sustainable forest basin is not only defined by the geographical regions or areas characterized by the presence of native forests, but also by a strong presence of stakeholders who can use, transform and trade timber and non-timber products in such a way to be CO2-neutral or even carbon negative.

Sustainable forest basins (or landscape level) have the aim of establishing an integrated approach for the use of the territory, in which the vocational use, the sustainable provision of ecosystem goods and services, the conservation of biodiversity, the protection of cultural heritage, the promotion of rural development, protection and sustainable management of forests, also integrating forest products to other productive activities.

Sustainability is the main purpose of forest management. Forest management practices entail a prior planning of the use of forests, based on knowledge concerning their status and growth dynamics, which determines how the different actions are reflected in the Forest Management Plans, which are then analyzed and authorized by the ALCs.

Forest management for commercial purposes entails the possibility of earning an income, but preserving the resource and increasing the number of standing trees. The income is determined by the average growth per hectare (GPH) of the forest, which is calculated based on the age structure of the stand.

Management practices aim at stimulating the growth of forests, promoting their regeneration and the growth of commercial species in a sustainable manner.

After interventions, it is estimated that a managed forest increases its growth capacity and, therefore, in its carbon fixation capacity by 50%. The establishment of sustainable forest basins requires a comprehensive scheme for the management of forests, which must be based on the analysis of social, economic and ecological factors. It aims at organizing forest resources so as to ensure harmonious and coordinated development of the sector's existing or potential value chains, based on the condition of the forest and land

The implementation of the REED+ RHP Project will be seen as tremendous boost to bring up to speed the preparation of basins’ strategic plans and especially supporting the implementation of the related actions. The Project will also contribute to the achievement of the goals set out in the National Forest Law.

The aim is to support at completion of the Project life, a total of 5 sustainable forest basins, empowering local stakeholders not only in the planning phase, but also working with them in the operationalization and implementation phases. This will lead to an increase in the number of hectares under forest management, reduction of emissions and enhancement of livelihoods for local communities, indigenous peoples and medium-holder families.

Importantly, the Project, through this output, will create synergies between sustainable management and conservation of natural resources, and sustainable production and consumption of goods and services derived from native forests, under a framework that promotes integration with other productive activities connected to forest ecosystems.

With this integrated approach and concrete investments on the ground, the Project will play a key and strategic role to up-scale efforts in the region, allow for higher impact and for a reduction of the risk of failure. The Project will also contribute to the achievement of the goals set out in the National Forest Law.

The Project will support at enhancing livelihoods of a large number of beneficiaries, promote an efficient use of financial resources and ensure that the benefits from the implementation of the REED+ RHP are shared by all stakeholders involved. The forest basin approach will reach not to only value chains timber and non-timber forest products beneficiaries but those that are included in land-use planning, from primary farmers to industry (Figure 16).
