### Output 4.2 Local communities manage their collective territories through PCCs, according to regulations in force and in a sustainable manner

Adhesion to the new Forest Law by producers (women and men) access natural resources and establish mechanisms for democratization of their use is essential in order to implement an inclusive forest policy.

Beyond the areas of native forests registered in farming and forestry farms with defined boundaries (EAP- CDE), it should be noted that there are other areas where the Forest Law applies, but where no registration has been made. With regard to the surface areas accredited by the OTERs created since the Forest Law was passed, it is noted that most of these areas might be agriculture and livestock farms without defined boundaries (EAP-SLD). In its Spanish acronym, this area might be agriculture and livestock farms without defined boundaries.

In its Spanish acronym, this area might be agriculture and livestock farms without defined boundaries. Indigenous communities and other local lands, which are in general subject to precocious land tenure conditions.

Most of the deforestation in Argentina has occurred in the Purque Chaqueño region, due to advancements of the agricultural frontier into areas with native forests. This is a very important area for biodiversity conservation. In addition, this region supplies 90% of the country's forest products (logs and posts, timbers, firewood and charcoal). The main economic activities in the Purque Chaqueño region are agriculture and livestock farming. This region comes directly from the sale of forest products, while the region is also used for subsistence livestock farming. In addition, the Purque Chaqueño region is among the population with the highest percentages of native blood needs throughout the country.

Precedent land tenure is a factor that accounts for poor engagement of the indigenous peoples and local communities in the management of their territories. The concept of "plaza de paja" (hay field), which has been traditionally associated with the holders of record owners of the plot of land, has led to a lack of interest from implementing places in areas where the beneficiaries lack proper registration of land ownership.

Therefore, with the new Plans Regulation contained in Annex 1 to Resolution 2737/14, issued by CORFA in 2014, it is possible for indigenous peoples and local communities, as well as other forest users, holders, and indigenous communities with current, traditional and public opinion access the funding facilities provided under the Forest Law. This will allow them to participate more effectively in the OTIN.

Also, to facilitate the planning of small-scale producers' units subject to the same technical assistance, the concept of "Beneficiary Groups" was developed. This entailed a major breakthrough in the regulatory framework for forest management and access to funding facilities provided under the Forest Law, as well as the inclusion of beneficiaries that used to be systematically excluded due to their tenure status.

This outlook seeks to improve collective forest management and to facilitate legal access to small forest producers through integrated forest community management.


---
**Page 76**
---

PICs to be promoted by this output consist of forest planning schemes launched by the DNII within MAyDS, intended for communities and other stakeholders in their territories under the framework of the Forest Law. The initiative started through the Forests and Community Projects (BRDF 8493), which seeks to strengthen livelihoods of local communities in their territories therefore contributing to combating poverty and improving food security, access to water resources, increased agricultural production, enhanced access to water and support to product marketing. The funds of this loan were allocated to the PICs, which are intended for forest management activities, including land tenure, and positive impact more communities and areas beyond the scope of the Forest law. This stems from the institutional need to promote social inclusion through schemes that supplement the benefits of the Law.

At present, there is a portfolio of 80 PICs covering more than 400,000 ha and population scale of approximately 150,000 people in the provinces of Santiago del Estero, Catamarca, San Juan, La Rioja, Chaco, Salta and Santiago del Estero. The average forest cover in these territories is 90%, with management units of approximately 6,000 ha administered, on average, by more than 30 families. Details on the beneficiaries of Output A.2 are included in Table 15.

Figure 17: Example of PIC in the province of Santiago del Estero (frontier) in which forest cover under traditional management is being converted to a community-managed forest compared to the advancement made by entrepreneurial and commercial actors.

Reinforcing livelihoods of communities through their PICs has been considered essential for the protection of forests and the attainment of the purposes of the Forest Law, the PANDeCt, and NDC, while having a Management Plan within the framework of the Forest Law provide the communities with tools to protect and enhance forest resources in their territories. These actions are intended to comply with the goals under the avoided deforestation measure contained in the National Development Plan (PND), which is part of the PANDeCt and NDC, as well as the objectives of the PICs, which are forest-dependent. By doing so such actions would also support the reduction of “fixed-migration” and displacement of communities from their territories. In this regard, it should be noted that under the management measure, it is considered that community management of forests offers a virtuous circle of promotion of sustainable development in rural areas, conservation of forest resources and reinforcement of the sectors that best integrate their lives with forest cover preservation (Figure 17).

The PICs are made up of users under different land tenure conditions: individual property, indigenous community properties, collective property, state-owned lands, and private possession.

The owners are people who have title deeds registered in the provincial property and real estate registries, while possessors are people with a right over the land because they have traditionally and publicly occupied it for more than 20 years. The occupants of public lands are considered as “possessors with an owner’s spirit”. The occupants of public lands can be candidates for ownership, although they require permits from the local land institutes to prove their existence and they must request permits for use, occupation or eventual adjudication to the local Land Institutes.


---
**Page 77**
---

These new forms of tenure are possible to address through the regulations of the Forest Law in agreement between the parties, communities and Local Enforcement Authorities in collaboration with other institutions with interference in land issues so that the former can have an approved forest management plan and thus obtain greater security in access to their livelihoods.

At the local level, this output will be of national scope and will be prioritized provinces with higher percentages of vulnerable rural communities and greater pressure on native forests. This includes the forest ecosystems of the Chaco-Pampean Forests, as well as the Patagonian Andes, the Northern Andean-Patagonian Forest, as well as the Argentine Monte and Argentina Euphrates regions, which have large populations of vulnerable rural communities.

The direct participants will be indigenous peoples and local communities and small family farmers, most of whom live in poverty. The project estimates that it will provide support to 95 communities in the preparation phase, with a total population of approximately 400 people. This includes training and information. The project will be implemented through five investment and application areas.

ALAs may promote calls for communities to submit statements of interest during the first three years of implementation of the proposal. The application shall be reviewed by local and national consultative authorities, as well as the National Technical Committee (NTC). The technical assistance shall be conducted by MAYDFS, and the deliverables shall include: the PIC, prepared via participatory processes; a project plan for each investment area; a justification for final approval; and a plan of investments and activities prioritized by the beneficiary community.

For territory-based calls for statements of interest, training of technical plan developers and follow-up of projects, as well as the preparation of the project plan, the PIC shall be prepared in the region and expanded with resources coming out of the 28% of funding allocated to the national level, according to the proposed benefit-sharing scheme.

Selection criteria:

Social: Indigenous peoples, or belonging to a peasant or small group of producers; Communities under poverty. Interest in community work; and Community forest territories belonging to any of the three concessionaires.

Environmental: Community territories with abundant forest cover. Boundary sharing between properties of the same concessionaire.

For the implementation of activities, it is also necessary to have a PIC written and agreed by the main stakeholders involved, which has been prepared or will be prepared by other sources.

Steps and entities involved in the decision-making process through which local interventions will be selected is reported in section 2.5.3 of this funding proposal.


B. Forest Management with Integrated Livestock - MIGL

As a consequence of the increase in cash crop agriculture, livestock farming has expanded to forest areas, thus exerting greater pressure on native forests.

With a view to eliminating any inconsistencies between livestock farming and the preservation of forest integrity, the Ministry of Environment (known as SAEy) and the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MAGyF) entered into a Technical Agreement for Forest Management with Integrated Livestock programmes in 2013.

The purpose of the agreement is to establish native forest inversion thresholds regarding forestry-livestock systems to ensure the continuity of the forest ecosystem services by implementing best practices, harmonize both national and provincial policies on forest management, and promote livestock production through the promotion of livestock programmes, the FESICIN and other sources.


Therefore, a National Technical Committee was created, which comprises the relevant areas of each ministry and the Argentine Institute for Agricultural Technology (INTA) for its acronym in Spanish). Moreover, ten Argentina Ecosystems are included: Forest Management with Integrated Livestock for the Parque Chaqueño and Andean-Patagonian Forest regions.


---
**Page 79**
---

REDD-plus BFB FUNDING PROPOSAL

GREENCLIMATEFUND | Page 74 of 154

where there is livestock farming and a higher number of silvopastoral systems (figure 18).

Figure 18: Map of the provinces that executed the Technical Agreement for Integrated Livestock and Forest Management in MIGL.

Within this context, MIGL is an alternative to make livestock farming compatible with forest conservation at the plot of land level and beyond, such as the landscape or regional level, so as to avoid deforestation and degradation. The objective of MIGL is to maintain the structure and function of ecosystems and their services. Also, MIGL seeks to maintain and improve the productivity of the ecosystem contributing to the community's well-being. The following are the basic agreements endorsed in an MIGL Plan:

- Protect at least 10% of the area considered a biodiversity reserve that cannot be used for livestock farming, so as to maintain the genetic wealth of existing fauna. The protected area must be that of greater biodiversity value.
- The aim is to maintain the structure and function of forests in the intervention area, preserving all diametric tree classes and maintaining the natural composition of species. In addition, there are other areas that can be used as pasture lands outside the forest.
- Sustainable livestock farming practices should include defining carrying capacity, rotation and grazing schemes, planning intervention frequency and outlining a forest management plan targeted to resource sustainability.
- Native or non-invasive alien species could be sown to increase the grazing capacity.
- Furthermore, the plan should also include a fire prevention plan. And moreover, it should include the proper planting of trees in areas where they are missing.
- Apply adaptive management approaches through monitoring and adjust activities to guarantee the sustainability of all systems components.

Furthermore, MIGL plans must include a system for the prevention and control of fires in native forests and


---
**Page 80**
---

### REDD-plus BRRP FUNDING PROPOSAL GREEN CLIMATE FUND Page 73 of 144

association systems. Such system should include specific actions for prompt control as a mean to prevent or limit the impact of fires in the ecosystem.

In summary, the MRGG agreement defines certain country principles and guidelines that include: (i) minimum elements to be included in the management plans; (ii) maintenance of exclusive areas for conservation; (iii) maintenance of forest cover; (iv) limitation of logging activities to the actual possibilities, taking into account any year-on-year variation in production; and (v) limitation of livestock management to the actual possibilities, taking into account any year-on-year variation in production.

Provincial Technical Committees should propose the required threshold adaptations based on the different types of forests, according to the classification made by each jurisdiction, and these should be validated as appropriate by the National Committee.

### B.1 Forest farmers implement models of forest management with integrated livestock

The purpose of this output is to implement the National Plan for Forest Management with Integrated Livestock (NPFMIL) in pilot projects, based on the guidelines provided in Table 19 (see below). To this end, there is a set of measures to not only implement less intense interventions but also support the development of different market strategies for products derived from lands where MRGG is applied. These include: (i) the implementation of integrated livestock and forest management systems; (ii) other schemes, such as the commercial development of native pasture seeds so gradually replace the current use of cultivated pastures.

### REDD plus BIP Funding Proposal

#### Green Climate Fund

**Page No.:** 134

---

**Introduction:**

The document outlines funding proposals for the implementation of MIRG (Mainstreaming Innovation and Research in Governance) projects under the REDD+ BIP (Biological Intervention Program). The focus is on enhancing the economic performance of forest-based enterprises, particularly small- and medium-sized enterprises.

#### Criteria for Project Selection:

Projects will be selected based on criteria such as:
1. **Priority Areas:** Projects will be prioritized in areas with high forest cover.
2. **Innovation:** Projects that demonstrate innovation or best practices in sustainable forest management.
3. **Capacity Building:** Projects that aim to build the capacity of local communities and enterprises.

#### Project Types:

Projects can fall into two categories:
- **MIRG Projects:** Focus on areas where forest fires are more significant, such as the Amazon and Misiones Forest region.
- **Forest Fire Management Projects:** Target small and medium-sized farmers who may require technical support to transition from traditional cattle production in forests to sustainable forestry systems.

#### Enhanced Response to Forest Fires:

This component aims to prepare institutions and vulnerable ecosystems to prevent and fight forest fires. It includes activities such as:
- **Fire Suppression:** Activities that complement fire management components.
- **Catalytic Actions:** Measures to enhance the effectiveness of existing fire management guidelines at the basin, community, or farm level.

#### Community Involvement:

Communities involved in forest fire response include:
- **Fire Management:** Uncontrolled fire management and lack of coordination among bodies responsible for detection and suppression.
- **Forest Management:** All areas are barriers and threats to native forests. In 2017, over 500 ha of native forests were affected by fires.

#### Fire Prevention Strategies:

In line with the PANDICC output, strategies include:
- **Fire Suppression:** Reducing fire risk.
- **Preventative Actions:** Activities such as fire breaks and other preventive measures to reduce the risk of forest fires.

The plan's activities and management are not carried out by the municipality or local community. Instead, they are managed by the provincial government. This includes breaking roads and purchasing equipment for the prevention and control of fires. Notwithstanding the foregoing, the local communities will be involved in the implementation process, for example, by giving permission to enter property. These participatory processes will be carried out by promoting meetings and workshops and they will be called and conducted by local authorities.

**D.2.4 Continuous improvement of AGRINIS, NFMS, FREIL, SISAR and the SNII-GEA-R system**
This activity is expected to broaden the nationwide reach of the NFMS and the MRV system, including enhancing data collection for REDD+ in degraded and secondary native forests, as well as the safeguards information system. Technical assistance will be provided by the GCF to ensure that the tools and information developed can be used by decision-makers for preventing deforestation and forest degradation, monitoring sustainable use of forests, from early detection and prevention of illegal logging, through strengthening national and local capacities for participatory management and community-based forest governance, upscaling REDD+ readiness. New tools shall be developed to estimate ecosystem recovery, and forest monitoring at the basin level is expected that spatial data will be used in conjunction with other information providing continuity to the National Forest Inventory, National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS), and MRV systems.

The new information generated is expected to improve and enhance national capacity for REDD+ implementation by reducing emissions reduced through REDD+ activities, in addition to the FREIL updated system. The project will also contribute to the SNII-GEA-R system. Resources will also be enhanced to advance the SNII-GEA-R accounting framework, which is expected to support the development of regulations for reporting to the REDDplus national registry, the MS and all other relevant stakeholders in the country.

#### D.2.5 Market development and financing options
Considering the needs identified by project participants, this activity will enhance economic performance of the projects developed through National Market Development (NMD) activities. The NMD activities link legal timber offer with buyers' demand in transformation centers, as well as in markets for small-scale producers and consumer centers. The marketing service supports commercialization of forest products and provides information on the forest products developed in the projects promoted by this proposal at fair prices to both local communities and private sector actors and basin businesses with remunerative activities. This activity is intended to start during the project implementation phase, but will continue beyond that phase to develop special market strategies for new timber and non-timber wood products. In addition to developing innovative financing, the project will connect producers with alternative sources of funding and technical assistance. The NMD activities will pay attention to barriers faced by women, indigenous peoples, local communities, small-scale producers, and other relevant stakeholders along the supply chains.

#### D.2.6 Supplementary strategic investments in forest-based, PFC, and special market strategies
This activity includes complementary or emergent investments that will contribute to the overall success of the project during its phase under components A, B and C and that therefore will start only at the beginning of the project implementation phase.

The Government also specifically requested that FAO act as executing entity for this project; for such request, FAO will serve both as Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entity (EE).

As an AE of GCF, FAO shall be responsible for the overall management, implementation and supervision of the Project. As EE, in addition to its role as AE, FAO will carry out both operational and administrative support activities, as well as advisory and technical support functions during the preparation of the project.

As the EE, the Government Country Office will carry out operational and administrative support activities which include the provision of the following services:

- Payments, disbursements and other financial transactions
- Recruitment and employment of personnel
- Procurement of services and equipment, including disposal
- Operational costs for travel and accommodation expenses, including fellowships.
- Travel authorization, visa requests, ticketing, and travel arrangements.
- Shipping and freight charges, insurance, and related costs, including others.

According to standard procedures, GCF and FAO will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA), under which FAO shall administer the relevant GCF Proceeds to be used for the financing of the Project. In accordance with the terms of the FAA, FAO will be responsible for the implementation of the Project.

Executive will be carried out in collaboration with a variety of partners as (an initial identification of which is reported in the "Project partners" paragraphs in this section).

FAO recognizes that successful development should be driven and owned by countries, as emphasized by Agenda 2030. At this purpose, to achieve the objectives of the project, while reinforcing capacities, ownership and sustainability, the Project will be implemented through a Multi-Party System (MPS) as implemented through the FAO Operational Partner Implementation Modality (OPIM) with MayDS under the modalities set out in the GCF Operational Manual Section 701.81 after expiry of the Operational Partners Agreement, through which MayDS also became Executing Entity, together with FAO. The agreement will follow the terms and conditions as set forth in the OPIM.

Since its launch in 2015, OPIM has been increasingly used by FAO to implement its projects and programs in countries where it is not a direct implementing partner. Under this modality, MayDS can be directly selected as Operational Partner, being a governmental entity. The assessment of MayDS scored the limit to its capacities as "low". There is a clear advantage to jointly deliver the project with MayDS, which has the capacity and experience in the area of REDD+ projects to achieve results between FAO and MayDS, for better suitability of project's results also at project's end, and for better alignment with national strategies, policies, plans, and environmental policies.

In accordance with GCF Operational Manual Section 701.81 after expiry of the Operational Partners Agreement, FAO is responsible for the monitoring of progress in achieving programme/results compliance with the agreed targets and indicators as well as reporting on results achieved to the GCF Board and Operational Partners Agreement. This includes inter alia timely funds transfers and reporting, quality of reporting, project implementation, risk management, including mitigation and response to risks, Risk Mitigation and Assurance Plan, and timely completion and follow-up on the results of Assurance Activities.

In summary, it is herewith confirmed that the Argentina REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014-2016 proposal has two co-EE: FAO and MayDS.

While the project will be implemented in partnership, FAO and MayDS detail out a clear division of roles:

FAO in addition to all AE and EE responsibilities mentioned in the paragraphs above, will provide technical support for the implementation of the Project. This includes capacity building and strengthening capacities of the counterpart key stakeholders and beneficiaries, at different levels. Main areas of technical support will encompass: integrated land-use planning, sustainable forest management,

will approve the Annual Work Plan (AWP). It is responsible for making management decisions by consensus or majority, when guidance is required by the Project Manager, including recommendations for FAO approval of project plans and revisions, and addressing any project-level concerns. To ensure FAO's ultimate accountability to its donors, the Board will be responsible for ensuring that the project has adequate financial management for development results, best value for money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective communication with stakeholders.


* Provide overall guidance and direction to the project, ensuring it remains within any specified constraints.
* Address project issues as raised by the project manager;

Following the structure of other BIP projects implemented in the region, the Board shall be composed of MAYDs (Ministerial Advisory Committee), which will include representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MAGA) in its Spanish acronym), the National Parks Administration (NFA) and FAD. FAD will also act as the Board's technical secretariat through the Project Manager. The Board shall be chaired by the MAGA representative. The Board may include other representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries. MAYDs, as Chair of the Board, may consider including other relevant stakeholders. MAYDs will be responsible for ensuring that the project is aligned with national priorities, including those related to territorial-based activities, as well as for supporting coordination with and participation of territorial authorities.


Project Management Unit

Under the overall guidance of the Project Board, the Project Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for planning, implementing, monitoring and evaluating an Project activities. The PMU will have responsibility for, among others:

* Project operational planning, management and execution, including the direct supervision of project activities;
* Coordinating the management of financial resources and procurement;
* Reporting to the Board on progress against approved work plans;
* Preparing management reports for the Project Board, GCF, NFA and FAO, including annual reports and the final report. The PMU will also manage the Project, if required, and based on its inputs from the Project M&E plan.
* Promoting good project dialogue; and
* Disseminating project results.

The PMU will be headed by a Project Manager (PM), who will administer projects in the Board's name. The PM will also act as the focal point for the Board and liaise with the correct resources. The PM will be responsible for the overall management and implementation of the Project’s activities and for requesting disbursement of the Project’s resources to the EUs for delivery. The PM is recruited by and directly reports to the FAO, and is responsible for ensuring that project activities are carried out in accordance with the operational and managerial matrix. The PM leads the management of the project activities as per approved Annual Work Plans (AWPs) and ensures that these plans are aligned with the GCF’s logical framework. Detailed project annual work plans will be developed in collaboration with the PMU and according to the logical framework.

### Box 1: Decision making process for local interventions investments

Key steps to be followed are as follows:

* **Local intervention proponents/beneficiaries in collaboration with supported by grass-root organizations, NGOs, academics, technicians, as needed** prepare the expression of interest and submission of project proposal to the Provincial Forest Consultative Committee.
* **MADS and Provincial Forest Consultative Committees select projects and grant a first level approval.** MADS through the forest extension service at provincial level and National Directorate of Forestry (NDF) at national level will provide technical assistance in the preparation of proposals as needed. The provincial committees submit the proposal to the national level committees, i.e., the Indigenous Affairs Committee (IAF), the Public Agencies Committee (PAC), the NDF, MADS, NGOs, academia, and other key governmental and non-governmental actors are included in these committees.
* **The national level committees in collaboration with the Project Management Unit of the Project (PMU) and MADS will evaluate and select the proposed projects.** Specific clarifications and/or modifications to the project costs will be requested to the proponents if needed, and prior to approval.
* **Upon final approval by PMU and the national level Committees (point above), the PMU requests to the EFB the disbursement of the funds.**

FAES will be part of the PMU of the project Board and shall participate in the different decision making instances at provincial and national level.

Besides the MADS, other National Government entities will be members of the Project Board such as the NDA, MAGF, NGOs, MAGF and FAES will also be members of the National and Provincial Forest Consultative Committees.

### C.2.6 Non-carbon benefits:

Argentina conducted studies to assess the environmental and social benefits of native forests associated with the implementation of REDD+ activities, with the support of the UN-REDD National Programme. These studies were intended to gather and generate spatial information to identify areas where the implementation of the Forest Law and, subsequently, the PANDUC, particularly those actions related to conservation and


---
**Page 99**
---

REDD+ plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

GREEN CLIMATE FUND | Page No. 4


The studies were carried out on a subnational scale: forest regions: Andes-Patagonian Forest, Parque Chacarito, Missions Rainforest, Tucumán-Bolivian Forest, Argentine Misiones and Argentine Espinal.


The results of these studies were released in 2019 through a set of individual information charts for each forest region. These charts provide an overview of the potential REDD+ benefits that could be achieved from maps resulting from the study can also be viewed through the web portal of Argentina's NFMS (link).


The results of the study suggest that the implementation of REDD+ provides great potential non-carbon benefits, although these vary greatly among the various forest regions.


For example, biodiversity conservation benefits would be particularly important. Argentina is characterized by a rich diversity of species and habitats, being one of the countries with the largest number of ecoregions in the world (15), including 20% of the total area of protected areas in the country. In addition: Argentina is home to 385 mammal species, 1,002 bird species, 175 amphibian species and over 400 fish species.


In addition, Argentina harbours a large part of two of the most globally threatened ecoregions: the Gran Chaco Americans and the Atlantic Forest. In these ecoregions, the conversion of natural ecosystems into agricultural land is one of the main drivers of deforestation. The Gran Chaco Americans is located in the Chaco area. This constitutes a severe threat to the biodiversity present in this region. The actions implemented on forest, within the framework of the Forest Law and the PANSyCC, provide important opportunities for the conservation of these ecoregions.


The implementation of the Forest Law and the PANSyCC in the dry tropical forest of the Gran Chaco Americans -90% of it is located in Argentina-, offers important opportunities to address the drivers of deforestation. The main objective should be to ensure that the forest remains as a natural ecosystem, which would be important to ensure the survival of a large number of endemic terrestrial vertebrates**, so the implementation of the Forest Law and the PANSyCC should be accompanied by actions aimed at ensuring that protection would have a great positive impact. Such measures could include the Forest Law itself, which sets the goals for forest conservation and sustainable use, as well as the National Strategy to Combat Desertification (SENADes), which is based on the principle of integrated management of natural resources and promotes, for example, forest management with integrated livestock practices, as well as sustainable silvopastoral systems. Similar measures in the Missions rainforest region (hosting some of the most important biodiversity hotspots) would also be very beneficial for biodiversity conservation, given their importance as biodiversity "hotspots" at a national and global level.


**Nieri, J., Torres, R., Llancares, J.N., Cruciale, M.I., Périgo, M.E. and Budin, D. (2016). Promoted areas and spatial conservation priorities for endemic vertebrates of the Gran Chaco: one of the most threatened ecoregions of the world. Diversity & Distributions 22(3):252-274.

# REED-plus BRRP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Areas where the implementation of REED+ actions targeted at native forest conservation activities in public and privately-owned lands and in local community territories encompassing high conservation value forests (HCVFs) and biodiversity focal sites with conservation gaps.

One of the ways in which implementation of REED+ could foster native forest conservation activities is by providing incentives for the declaration of private nature reserves, which could supplement the federal and provincial programs that have been implemented to date. The PANDACC includes several actions targeted specifically at this area among conventional protected areas. In this regard, during the 2014-2016 period, 26 new reserves were registered in the Chaco-Pampean plain region, which covers an area of approximately 375,890 hectares and includes a total of 211. These 26 reserves cover 56,113 hectares altogether, taking the total number of privately-owned conservation concessions to 972,400 ha at its 2016.

In addition to biodiversity conservation, another important two-carbon benefit from the implementation of the Forest Law is the reduction of soil salinization and the rising of water tables. In the Chaco-Pampean plain forests have a key role in controlling the water balance and the distribution of salts. The extremely low inclination of the terrain in this region causes the accumulation of rainwater, which stagnates on the surface for long periods, until it is finally absorbed by the soil. This process leads to an increase in salinity levels in these areas usually causes floods by waterlogging and salinization due to the rise of the water table. In these areas, the water tables are very close to the surface, which means that they can be easily affected by evaporation, which keeps down to the subsoil. As a result, water tables rise and salts are transported to the surface". This process is known as "salinization", which has a negative impact on the productivity of soils in the long-term sustainability of the crops and pastures in this area. Therefore, this is a key benefit that the implementation of REED+ would bring about in both the Pampa Chaqueña and the Argentine Espinal regions.


---
**Page 101**
---

Figure 23: Areas where the implementation of REDD+ actions targeted at native forest conservation and sustainable use could provide the greater benefits in controlling the rise of water tables in the Parque Chaqueño (left) and Argentine Espinal (right)

An important benefit from the implementation of the Forest Law is the control of soil erosion. In Argentina, the Forest Law is a pioneering law that, to a great extent, on the biophysical characteristics of the different forest regions. In regions with high topography and poor soils, such as the Misiones rainforest or the Argentine Espinal region, forests contribute especially to controlling water runoff and reducing soil erosion. The implementation of REDD+ actions targeted at forest conservation and restoration have the potential to help protect the soil capability for water storage, reduce surface run-off after heavy rains, decrease wind erosion in areas with sandy soils, and increase the capacity of the soil to hold water.

In contrast, in arid regions with plain terrain and sandy soil, forests play a more important role in controlling wind erosion. In these areas, wind erosion is a key factor in soil degradation processes, like in some parts of the Parque Chaqueño region or the Argentine Espinal. Actions targeted at forest conservation and restoration have the potential to contribute to soil conservation in areas prone to wind erosion.

Figure 24: Soil vulnerability to wind erosion in the Parque Chaqueño region by soil vulnerability to water erosion in the Misiones rainforest region.

In addition to environmental benefits, Argentina also intends to maximize the social benefits from implementation through the strengthening of local communities, "promoting the regularization of land tenure based on the recognition of the territories of native peoples and local communities". The PANBIACC also intends to include REDD+ in its strategy for reducing rural poverty by creating new job opportunities in order to discourage migration to urban areas". The analysis of non-carbon benefits took these factors into account, identifying "the reduction of rural poverty" as one of the "main social benefits of poverty" as possible benefits from the implementation of REDD+ in the different forest regions of the country.

The implementation of REDD+ can effectively contribute to reducing poverty, since poverty rates in Argentina's rural areas are significantly higher than in urban ones. In these areas, the populations' reliance on forests for their subsistence can be relatively high (Figure 23).

According to the latest National Population, Household and Housing Census (2010), the LSH index, a multidimensional index which measures poverty in terms of a number of factors associated with living conditions, is 10 points higher in rural areas (18.2%) than in urban ones (8.3%).

Figure 25: Areas where the implementation of REDD+, if adequately designed, could provide the greatest benefits in terms of poverty

The REDD+ actions, developed with the participation of the local stakeholders, can benefit local livelihoods by helping clarify and strengthen land tenure rights, increasing communities' forest management capacity, and improving ecosystem services which play an important role in food safety and climate change adaptation (Figure 26).

# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND
Page 104 of 134

The funds to be received from GCF through this funding proposal will be used directly to address drivers of deforestation such as unsustainable timber management and fire. The funds will also be used to address underlying causes of forest degradation and strengthen the resilience of forests, including degraded and diversified livelihoods), strengthening the control and oversight capacities at provincial and national level, and improving the capacity for monitoring and reporting on REDD+ activities. The proposal establishes specific measures so to mitigate 27 MtCO2e by the year 2030, making a significant contribution to the GCF’s climate goal.

The resources to be received will be used to support productive transformations and create conditions for low-emission development and resilience to climate change in the country's forest areas, especially in those which have made their initial contribution to the achieved results.

The content of this GCF proposal is designed not only to achieve these goals, not only by a direct manner, but also by creating enabling conditions with the potential to drive greater transformations in productive systems with additional funding (see section D.2 in this document).

### Activities

The activities in this proposal will generate an estimated mitigation of 10 MtCO2e in 6 years. Mitigation potential is calculated based on a combination of avoided deforestation and changes in emissions from the effects of project activities and pressures on forests.

The proposed activities will also generate important adaptation benefits reducing vulnerability of the country to climate change, including improved water security through the use of forest fires, preventing ecosystem services that ecosystems provide, improving the economies of rural communities and transitioning to more sustainable low-carbon production systems.

Many of these activities can be carried out through activities in this proposal take account of landscape or basin-level planning; are consistent and complementary with the approach Argentina is following for the development of its National Adaptation Plan. Such Plan is currently under development and has the main aim of integrating climate change adaptation into national development plans, policies, strategies, and increasing resilience to the effects of climate change.

More generally, project activities will support forest management that is good, so that management should reduce degradation and fragmentation, as supported by project activities; should increase resilience to wildlife, which would otherwise be displaced or killed in search for food.

Protecting or enhancing ecosystem services can serve as important ecosystems-based adaptation measure when the services are relevant for climate resilience. Project activities focused on maintaining or restoring forest cover will contribute to reducing vulnerability of forests and other natural resources that are vulnerable to climate change, with changing patterns and extremes of rainfall, affecting the provision of water for livelihoods and personal use. Forest conservation or restoration areas important for soil erosion control (such as windbreaks, shelterbelts, and buffer strips) and for maintaining biodiversity, such as local landscapes of flora.

Project activities also support livelihoods and contribute to reduce poverty, particularly in rural populations often have greater reliance on forests for sustenance.

### D.2: Paradigm shift potential

The proposed activities will generate an estimated mitigation of 10 MtCO2e beyond the end of project implementation.

## Potential for scale-up and replication

The proposal seeks the transformation of productive activities to reduce or eliminate their pressure on forests, creating new business models as well as generating new opportunities for the well-being of vulnerable and poor communities living in the forest.


---
**Page 105**
---

To achieve this goal, it is fundamental to advance towards ensuring that communities living in forests are entitled to legal land and eventually tenure, which will also make it possible for them to settle down. In the case of Brazil, the project seeks to lay the foundations for the social organization and effective governance of forests. Finally, the project aims at improving forest management through strategic investments which may enable integrated forest management including livestock farming.

Thus, the project seeks to ensure the active and effective participation of local communities in the sustainable use and conservation of their forests. It will do so by supporting them in the establishment of community-based collective forest management, based on their habits and customs, and linking them to the best practices of sustainable forest management and the adoption of productive practices that allow them to ensure the integrity of forest ecosystems and obtain economic benefits.

The traditional peasant and indigenous occupation of forests is communal and open. These are territories where areas of grazing and forest use are shared customarily. This has allowed communities to loosen pressure on forest resources, which has been a key factor in their conservation. The Brazilian Ministry of Environment, Directorate of Forests, through the Forests and Community Project, promoted the first public policy of community forests in Brazil in 1985. In 2003, the project was restructured as the Forests and Community Project, taking of the loan and are in current implementations. From 2003 to the present, 80 PICs have been formulated, covering an area of approximately 4 million hectares, which includes 157,600 hectares of State Estates and including 2,500 peasant and indigenous families. The areas under community management are on average 6,000 hectares, with cases of more than 20,000 hectares of indigenous land.

The above must go hand-in-hand with the development and implementation of business models and investments in forest-based enterprises that will contribute to the strengthening of value chains in forest basins to provide marketing possibilities for sustainable products from the forests. These new businesses will be based on the production of non-timber forest products, such as timber, fruits, nuts, honey, etc., for both timber and non-timber products, including support through the marketing service that will be created with the project.

The shift from conventional silvopastoral systems, in which the forest is degraded in the long term to forest management models with integrated livestock will not only promote recognition of the value of environmental services provided by forests but also improve their conservation and regeneration, but also improve livestock production. As an example companies this will include seeking opportunities for market differentiation, thus increasing the chances that this change will continue in the long term.

The project will also foster the development of new tools to improve inspection and surveillance capacity at the national level as well as at the state and municipal levels. This will contribute to strengthening incentives for the conservation and sustainable use of forests at local levels.

It is expected that the combination of these positive incentives and conditions for effective governance will also generate a virtuous cycle, which will be reinforced by the creation of new activities in areas for populations which currently lack resources and access to funding.

The project will seek to replicate the model of successful experiences (e.g., forest-based value chains) and horizontal ones (among farmers and other stakeholders across forest basins). This replication can become increasingly efficient as the project creates enabling conditions that cause a broader impact on the landscape. These include:

- Possibility to include new local communities and families into value chains which positively diversified their income sources.
- Improved capabilities for monitoring, inspection and oversight in broader areas than those included in the pilot phase of the project.
- Laying the foundations to change the national criteria to promote forest-related productive activities including livestock farming.

Informing the criteria for the future allocation of public funds through the FNEICIN, including the creation of new criteria and criteria for project selection and approval at the provincial level (e.g. through COFEMA).
- Mobilizing private funding by providing greater security as to land tenure, and demonstrating the

# REDD-plus RFP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND
Page 84 of 134

### Knowledge and learning potential

The creation of a system for action follow-up and evaluation is proposed, with the primary purpose of providing feedback to the different interactions and making adjustments in a timely manner. Likewise, this process will allow for the identification and dissemination of lessons learned that can be included in the development of new projects.

The project elements that contribute to this are:
- The Forest Carbon Trust Fund (FCTF) which facilitates communication and coordination with the provinces and local technicians who support project formulation.
- The Forest Carbon Technical Committee, which provides technical advice and technical and advisory platforms, such as the Forest Consultative Council and the MIRGI Technical Committee.
- The project documentation and knowledge management actions.

### Contributing to a broader impact

The project will seek to include enabling conditions that contribute to a lasting and broader impact. A key element of the proposal is the promotion of forest conservation based on sustainable economic activities and government policies, which will help to reduce poverty in rural areas and improve the livelihoods of communities and regions. Communication efforts will reinforce messages intended to drive consumers towards products from certified forests.

The key barriers that the project seeks to remove include: allowing farmers and local communities who live on a low-income area or work in informal economic activities, including settlers who currently have no legal title to their land, to participate in forest management schemes.

Respecting the criteria established by land management schemes is a key element to create a favorable environment, which will be strengthened with the provision of timely information and the creation of response protocols to address those activities which may not conform to land management schemes.

### D.3 Sustainable development potential

#### Objectives
- To contribute to the achievement of sustainable forest management and economic development.
- To contribute to the achievement of poverty reduction, social inclusion, and environmental conservation.

The PANBIIC is the national REDD+ strategy in the country, which was developed within the framework of the GNCC as part of the Government's actions to promote sustainable development, thus meeting the international commitments made by Costa Rica, and is closely related to the 2010 Agenda for Sustainable Development, specifically goals 13 and 15.

In 2016, Argentina submitted its revised NDC to the UNFCCC. In order to identify each sector's contribution to the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), COFEPA, in conjunction with COPAFOR, developed several scenarios for REDD+ implementation.

The PANBIIC is one of these selected plans and represents the commitment made by Costa Rica for the REDD+ mechanism at a national level; it stresses the importance of native forests in meeting the NDC goal. The plan was approved by the National Council on Forests (CNF) as part of the process of land use planning, within the framework of the GNCC and COFEPA, and do not constitute specific sectoral goals. Commitments made under the REDD+ mechanism are included in the national strategy.

The PANBIIC was developed through a participatory process (see detailed description in section 4.E) and, in order to follow up on the social and environmental aspects associated with its implementation, the country developed the CAS for the National Forest Strategy.

The second main objective of PANBIIC will result in environmental, social and economic co-benefits, such as increased ecosystem services, an improved quality of life for local stakeholders and better native forest management practices, also helping to enhance the adaptive capacities of the communities living in the forests.

The main beneficiaries of this proposal are vulnerable rural populations and indigenous peoples, with important mention to those who live in the Purépecha Chiquito region, that is the region in which most of the activities will be carried out and which has the greatest concentration of this population.

The development of the programme's four components will help not only to reduce deforestation and


---
**Page 107**
---

### REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND
Page 104 of 184

**increase forest regeneration, but native forest conservation and management is also expected to generate multiple economic, environmental and social benefits, namely:**

- **Component A:** This proposal intends to strengthen communities' (livelihoods) at the local level and increase the economic development of local farmers by establishing sustainable forest bases (A.1).
- **Component B:** This component will contribute to the conservation of native forests in the country with the conservation of forest ecosystems, through forest management with integrated livestock, environmental education, and community participation, which will generate income for rural communities generation, thus ensuring integrated territorial management.
- **Component C:** This component will contribute to the conservation of forest ecosystems services (biodiversity, regulation of the water cycle, control of erosion) produced by forests types which are unique in the world. It will also contribute to the strengthening of institutional capacities and oversight at a national and provincial level for conservation.
- **Component D:** This component will reinforce incentives for the conservation and sustainable use of forests. One of the measures that will be implemented is the reduction of the number of forest management staff, which has been reduced in the project areas, and the increased presence of MAIS staff on the ground is considered to be a key factor.

The project's benefit sharing scheme considers 72% to be distributed directly at the local level, while 28% remains at the national level. The resources allocated to the local level will be assigned by open competition and through the selection process for projects that are carried out in the project areas or by communities participating in the project’s activities. Beneficiaries will be selected by the Provincial Council and will be authorized to carry out their own projects, which will be monitored by the provincial council. Resources assigned at the national level will be used mainly to reinforce institutional capacities that ensure compliance with existing regulations which, in turn, support execution and operation at the provincial level.

In view of the above, the implementation of the PNH/CC contributes to meeting different sustainable development objectives included in the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development.


#### D.2. Needs of the recipient country

**Description of the needs of Argentina**

A general description of socio-economic (for example as native forests) context of Argentina has been presented in section C.2.1. General description; it is worthwile complementing with additional and more specific considerations on the needs of the recipient here.

Although Argentina is considered one of the important economies in Latin America, with a Gross Domestic Product per capita (GDP/capita) that is 30% higher than the average for the region's industrial countries, including Brazil, historical volatility in its economic growth has hindered the country’s development and has led to significant social inequality.

The 2018 financial turbulence had an impact on the country and led government to reviewing its economic plan and to requesting assistance from the International Monetary Fund (IMF), with a view to stabilizing public finances. As a result of this, Argentina's GDP/capita decreased by 34% between April 2018 and April 2020, and according to the Central Bank, the Argentine peso depreciated 222% between April 2018 and April 2020. The economy shrunk another 2.2% in 2019 (INEGI, 2019).

Argentina’s limited long-term economic performance affects the country's capacity to reduce poverty and increase citizens' income. In addition although economic assessments still have to be developed, it is expected that the impact of the pandemic will further exacerbate this situation, especially in rural areas and rural population in the country.

In the period 1950-2016, Argentina experienced 14 recessions (one or more years running of negative growth), with an average duration of 1.6 years. The country has thus been in recession for one third of the time since 1950.

Macroeconomic instability, the economic crises and periods of high inflation rates have considerably affected the well-being of people and had an impact on natural resource degradation.

Deforestation is a major environmental problem in Argentina. The provinces that inhabit and depend economically on forests; forest deforestation in favor of agriculture has not been matched by an improvement in poverty levels. In fact, 10 out of the 23 provinces of the Argentine Northwest, concentrate the highest poverty rates in the country (Table 27).

This project is carried out in close coordination with ALARM on a national scale.

### The proposal being prepared with a vision of building on the achievements so far by its projects above mentioned, and supporting Argentina in continuing and enhancing its collaboration with local communities for the preparation of this project proposal builds on the participatory experience and mechanisms established through the Forest Community Project (FCP) and the Forest Community Participation Mechanism (FCPM), both supported by the Forest and Community Project (and mentioned in details in the ESA during the PPR preparation and implementation. ### D. Country Strategy

Describe the beneficiary country's ownership, or capacity to implement a funded project or programme (policies, institutions, etc.).

#### National climate strategy in progress

The Project contributes to the country’s priorities of low-carbon and climate-resilient development. Argentina has undertaken several actions that are aligned with the Paris Agreement and the Paris Agreement by Law No. 27370, enacted in September 2016. Within this framework, COFEPA conducted a review of the NAPCC, coordinated among the ministries by the GCNC, with a participatory strategy for the development of the NAPCC. The NAPCC was submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 2016 and accepted under the Global Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation (Law No. 27380), that sets up a structure to maintain climate change adaptation and mitigation.

The NAPCC is a sector-based action plan to PNAMCC, and is Argentina’s REDD+ strategy. This Proposal is fully aligned with the provisions of the PNAMCC as explained in Section C.2.1. Furthermore, this Proposal also contributes to sustainable management of agro-ecosystems to reduce vulnerability.

Accredited entities' capacity

FAO was accredited by the GCNC in October 2016, which allows the organization to develop grant projects for up to USD 250 million, total project size (including co-funding). It is also an implementing partner in Readiness processes.

FAO, in its capacity as AE, is supporting the preparation of projects that meet the Fund's eligibility criteria in


---
**Page 111**
---

28 countries worldwide. Additionally, it is supporting other AEs by providing technical assistance in proposal formulation and implementation.

In Latin America and the Caribbean, FAO is supporting as Accredited Entity 31 proposals within different funding windows of which 11 have been approved. Specifically 4 Funding Proposals have been approved for Costa Rica (USD 20 million - under the GCF pilot programme for REDD+ results-based payments), Chile (USD 63 million - under the GCF pilot programme for REDD+ results-based payments). El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua and Panama (USD 15.7 million - under the GCF pilot programme for REDD+ results-based payments).

Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme (RPSP) for Guyana, Guatemala, Trinidad and Tobago, Nicaragua, Suriname, Belize, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Honduras, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay being the "Strengthening Argentina's National Designated Authority through GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme" (REDD+). Additionally, FAO is also cooperating in the implementation of projects headed by other AEs in Ecuador and Peru.

FAO’s comparative advantage lies in supporting proposals that address climate change mitigation and adaptation in agricultural planning, rural development and food security, increase in family agriculture resilience, sustainable land management (e.g., ecosystems), capacity-building and innovation for the farming sector's adoption and mitigation.

In close cooperation with Member States, FAO supports the planning and implementation of country-related policies, strategies, plans and programmes. Stakeholders and communities are engaged in various ways to involve local communities and women. FAO seeks to improve policy consistency, to ensure climate action in the farming sectors brings about a transformational change.

Additionally, in Argentina, FAO has closely cooperated to conduct REDD+ related actions based on its participation as an implementing partner of the GEF REDD National Programme, and no previous specific targeted support.


### D. Efficiency and effectiveness

Cost-effectiveness with regard to financial and non-financial aspects

The proposal aims at increasing greater efficiency on the basis of a focused allocation of financing to the most vulnerable populations and to areas in which deforestation pressure is higher, and where investments have a greater impact.

- promoting actions focused on priority regions and communities using, inter alia, the team of extensions;
- developing the critical criteria agreed upon at the national level for allocating investments;
- ensuring centralized evaluation of the different proposals presented through the provinces.


Furthermore, The Project will seek to invest efficiently and eliminate funding barriers from other sources, so as to mobilize future investment from other sources by:

- integrating the project into the national and community plans, which will enable harnessing the different income opportunities stemming from the sustainable use of forests, addressing a broader range of forest users;
- promoting business plans, stressing their economic feasibility;
- setting the bases to ensure forest tenure by the communities.


Vulnerable communities and farmers, as the priority beneficiaries of this proposal, do not have any other funding alternative. Therefore, the resources allocated through this proposal would not be displacing any other alternative funding.

Additionally, the project will seek to invest in innovation based on knowledge management mechanisms, which will provide feedback for the local implementation models.


The estimated cost of the project per 100GtCO2e measured as “total investment” (emission reduction + separation and storage) is USD 3.5 billion. The total investment includes the costs of estimating the emissions of the total area impacted by the project as well as a set of assumptions on the effectiveness of project activities and pressures on forests.

# E.1 Environmental and social safeguards

## E.1.3 Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA)

### The ESA report submitted with this proposal demonstrates that during the period for which GCF results-based payments are being requested, activities in Argentina were aligned with the environmental and social safeguard policies of the GCF, FAO at the GCF, and the UNCCD, as well as the relevant legal framework of Argentina.

For the purposes of the ESA, the following documents were reviewed and assessed for their alignment with FAO's Environmental and Social Management Guidelines (ESMG), which are aligned with the IFC Performance Standards on Environment and Development, the Corporation Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability, the UNCCD Country Safeguards for REDD+, and other GCF requirements in this area. This assessment includes information on how environmental and social safeguards were integrated into project design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation.

The assessment also examines how indigenous peoples and local communities were engaged, and how grievance redress mechanisms to receive and resolve complaints were implemented.

The assessment is based on a review of the following documents:

- The National REDD+ Strategy (2015), which provides an overview of the national guiding framework for REDD+ in the country which has helped achieve the EBS during the results period of the project. Other relevant initiatives are also reviewed, including the Forests and Communities Project (FCP) under the UNCCD National Programme, the Forests and Communities Project, and the PanAbyC – demonstration projects.

- The Forest Law (2015), which provides a legal framework for developing Argentina's REDD+ strategy (the PanAbyC in the context of the Forest Law and as a specific public policy instrument. The process was participatory and inclusive, involving relevant stakeholders at all levels, including indigenous peoples and local communities.

Based on such an analysis it can be concluded that during the results period for the funding proposal, Argentina had a robust legal and institutional framework which, together with a number of mechanisms, initiatives (projects), and measures, has allowed to achieve the EBS while minimizing or mitigating negative impacts that could affect people or the environment, through direct or indirect actions resulting from the implementation of the project. These include the development of forest management plans for the development of forest blocks, with a special safeguard for the most vulnerable sector.

The analysis in the ESA demonstrates that the activities that led to EBS for which the country is seeking REDD+ results-based payments are consistent with and supported by the legal framework in the country. Together with associated policies, initiatives and measures, these activities have allowed a reduction of deforestation rates and an increase in forest cover while promoting the development of a legal framework supporting the safeguarding of the rights of indigenous peoples, promoting gender equality and citizen participation, and ensuring compliance with the Country Safeguards for REDD+.

### Key findings and recommendations of the ESA include:

- Argentina's approach to REDD+ entails building on and leveraging the forest law and positive results achieved in the context of other initiatives already implemented in Argentina (e.g., the Argentina UN-REDD National Programme, FCP, the Forests and Community project, and the PanAbyC – demonstration projects).

- High-level engagement with indigenous peoples and local communities. The country has made extensive progress in gathering information and helping to secure the legal and tenure status of these groups, work which has also been supported by other initiatives (e.g., the UN-REDD National Programme). There are opportunities to further engage with indigenous peoples and local communities, for example incorporating simplified or more culturally appropriate processes in schemes to access funding through the Forest Law (such as the payment for environmental services scheme).

#### E.1.2.3 Environmental and Social Analysis (ESA)

*Provide adequate and efficient information describing how environmental and social risks and impacts will be addressed by the project activities.*

In order to ensure that potential environmental and social impacts are addressed properly in accordance with compliance with the GCF’s ESS standards, consistent with FAO’s ESS, as well as the UNFCCC safeguards, an Environmental and Social Analysis (ESA) will be developed. The ESA will be used for clearance process before implementation of the project activities.

As the project is classified as Moderate Risk, according to the FAO Guidelines, an Environmental and Social Analysis for Moderate Risk Projects will be developed at project inception, and once the areas of intervention are identified. This analysis will identify risks and impacts associated with the project activities that have been identified. These same stakeholders will be involved in the development of the Analysis.

Based on recommendations from the ESS, a set of actions will be defined to ensure compliance with the ESS. The Environmental and Social Committee Plan (ESCP) will be set out to pre-set the measures and actions required for project implementation that are consistent with the GCF’s ESS standards, consistent with FAO’s ESS, as well as the UNFCCC safeguards.

The ESCP will set out the project commitments and lists actions that the project will undertake in order to ensure compliance with the ESS. The ESCP will be updated at least once a year to reflect any changes or updates on compliance with the ESS. The ESCP will set out the project commitments and lists actions that the project will undertake in order to ensure compliance with the ESS.

The Environmental and Social Analysis and the ESCP are part of the stakeholder engagement process and will be used as an important tool for communication, consultation and dialogue to reduce or otherwise mitigate the potential environmental and social risks and impacts of the project.

The project, by its design, is intended to have environmental and social benefits. Though the potential negative impacts may not be fully identified at this stage, such impacts could result in larger impacts if they are not identified early during the planning cycle, with appropriate mitigation measures integrated into the project design from inception onwards. The participative assessment activities can help to identify potential or expected impacts, as well as mitigation and monitoring measures.

FAO will be committed to ensuring meaningful, effective and informed participation of stakeholders to help define the project’s environmental and social objectives. This will be achieved through a process that is informed by a continuous process of risk screening and analysis, whereas initial planning and selection of sites is informed by any potential adverse impacts, and either re-planned or developed with accompanying opportunities to mitigate such risks.

The results of this assessment will be used to ensure that the project is not increased, and on potential impacts on protected areas, indigenous peoples, decent work and gender equality. The ESCP will set out the project commitments and lists actions that the project will undertake in order to ensure compliance with the ESS.

FAO will undertake environmental and social screening of sub-activities, following the Environmental and Social Screening Checklist. The results of the screening checklist will be analyzed by the project safeguards specialist and incorporated into the Environmental and Social Management Unit in FAO for endorsement. # REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND
Page 1 of 34

### ESMP Aim, as well as the first Summary of Safeguards Information:

The main areas of participation and dialogue as related to the PanYRBPC include:
- Socialization and capacity strengthening on forests and climate change as well as on key elements of REDD+ strategies: between 2015 and early 2017, workshops were held with representatives of COTs, NGOs, and other stakeholders.
- A workshop attended by 106 participants, including representatives of civil society and academic organizations, and representatives of indigenous peoples and local communities, government officials, and private sector actors.

### Formation of technical working groups addressing key REDD+ issues:
(i) environmental and social benefits and risks associated with the implementation of REDD+, (ii) institutional arrangements for REDD+ financing, (iii) drivers of deforestation and forest degradation, (iv) indigenous peoples.
- Regional and multistakeholder dialogue workshops, developed in collaboration with local institutions such as universities and research centers, were held between 2017 and early 2018. In total, 10 workshops were conducted in a systematic manner between 2017–2018, with a total participation of over 350 people from different sectors linked to land use, forests and climate change, validated by COFEMA representatives.
- Workshops on safeguards, gender and free prior and informed consultation processes with indigenous peoples, which are described in detail in the document.

As part of the ESMP, a participatory process was carried out in Argentina carried out on national exercises to identify the risks associated with REDD+, as well as the SON of the PanYRBPC, in order to design and implement REDD+ safeguards consistently, avoid or mitigate potential social and environmental risks, and ensure that the benefits are shared equitably.

The participatory process was carried out in six forest regions of the country in 2017 and 2018 served to analyze the potential social and environmental benefits and risks of the different pillars and actions associated with the REDD+ strategy. The results were presented at a national workshop held on June 29, 2018, which served to report on the progress of the CAS, and the future adjustments that would be made in the PanYRBPC.

It should be noted that the analysis was conducted within the national safeguard development framework therefore only the benefits and risks of potential actions of the SOFs, and not those of the SSFs, of the PanYRBPC are considered. The main objective of the SONs is the only ones that entail direct intervention on the ground and generate reduced emissions.

Based on the PanYRBPC’s Strategic Plans and its actions, MAPPES, with the support of the Argentina UN-REDD Programme, has developed a series of tools to identify potential social and environmental risks and increase the benefits of implementing the PanYRBPC, giving special consideration to the Consultation and Participation Framework (CPF) and the National Safeguards Strategy (NSS).

These participatory processes have helped inform the development of the PanYRBPC, and have guided the development of this RBP proposal for annexes, and allowed us on continual assessment of potential risks and impacts. The SONs are a key component of the PanYRBPC design and the development of this proposal address the concerns of key stakeholders.

In the ESMP annexes, a number of key stakeholders for this project have been identified, along with information about their roles in project-based implementation. They will be updated and refined as project inception, and to accompany the selection and screening of sub-projects.

Building on the extensive stakeholder engagement that has accompanied the implementation of the Forest Law, the ESMP annexes provide a framework for ensuring meaningful participation by stakeholders in projects implemented through participatory, transparent and equitable processes, with a focus on the needs, perspectives and interests of indigenous peoples. The ESMP also provides guidance to MAPPES as it relates to ensuring meaningful, effective and informed participation of stakeholders in the formulation and implementation of projects. Stakeholder engagement will be considered on-going process that involves in varying degrees depending upon the project, and the development of a monitoring mechanism by which people can make comments on project proposals and performance or raise grievances.


---
**Page 116**
---

In addition, the project proposes a number of activities that will support comprehensive participation processes, such as Component A, on "Territorial forest management," among other aspects, aims to increase the participation and empowerment of local communities in the governance of the Forest Law, with the objective of generating economic income but at the same time ensuring environmental sustainability. The elaboration of PICS (A.2.2) will contribute to improving access to resources of the Forest Law for local communities, as well as increasing their capacity to participate effectively and make decisions on forest management diagnoses (A.2.1), which will provide an opportunity for processes to understand the expectations, opportunities, and challenges of different actors involved.

Important resources for ensuring dialogue and implementing effective stakeholder engagement processes can be drawn from tools within the CAS, described in previous sections, as well as existing elements related to the Forest Law. This includes the following guidelines, published in 2014:

- Guidelines on Disseminating Information on OTRB Participatory Processes
- Guidelines on Methodologies for OTRB Participatory Processes
- Guidelines for Documenting the OTRB Participatory Process

The PICS that are described in Output A.2 can also serve as a useful tool to improve participation in forest governance, helping to promote free, prior and informed consultation processes. These can also help build consensus on land use plans and other decisions related to forest management.

Likewise, output A.1 which focuses on establishing and consolidating forest basins for timber- and non-timber products, will provide an opportunity for multi-stakeholder participation processes, involving the different actors involved in forest governance. This is particularly relevant for those who have not yet been able to access management plans or financing due to conditions of land tenure.

The MIRBI National Technical Committee and Provincial Technical Committee described previously, as related to Component B, will be responsible for developing a set of guidelines on participatory processes related to Component B, informed by expert inputs. The committees will work together to meet the objectives of the MIRBI National Plan, mainly oriented to training, monitoring the implementation of projects and development of forest management plans.

The Enlarged Forest Consultative Council and Local Consultative Committee described previously are also key to supporting provinces in project identification, development of activities, evaluation, monitoring, and implementation. These committees will be composed by representatives from government institutions or ministries of indigenous affairs, representatives of local communities organizations, and others.

Ongoing consideration will be made to ensure that relevant stakeholders are appropriately identified and involved at all stages of the project cycle for all activities.

Key participatory processes specifically linked on the development of this RBP proposal are reported in detail in section 2.3 of the ESM document of this funding proposal. It is worth mentioning also here that since the start-up of the preparation process for the funding proposal was approved by the GCF (December 2019), Argentina has been a signatory to the Paris Agreement.

In February 2020 the new authorities recognized the interest and commitment of the country in combating climate change, and decided to ratify the Paris Agreement. This decision is also aligned with the need to socialize the concept of the funding proposal with key stakeholders, such as COFEMA, MAgPyD, INAI, and the Ministry of Environment.

In March, it is stated at the meeting of the Forest Commission of the COFEMA, MAgPyD presented to environmental authorities of all the provinces the concept of the RBP funding proposal, objectives, initial elements of the project design, list of processes, and the proposed distribution of benefits. The proposal - and the benefit distribution scheme described in section 2.C1 of this funding proposal was unanimously declared of interest of the provinces and approved.
