In the same spirit, dialogue and consultations also progressed with FPAI related to a process for free, prior and informed consultation with indigenous peoples, resulting in the agreement for the joint-organization of a series of consultation events. The first of these would have been held in early April with the Merida Indigenous Peoples Council (MIPC) and the National Congress of Indigenous Communities (CNIC). A second meeting would have been held with a larger number of participants representing approximately 100 indigenous organizations from across Mexico, including representatives from all regions of the country. face-to-face meetings had to be put on hold, limiting the ability to carry out consultations as planned. As a consequence, the two consultation events were postponed until further notice.

The next step in this process was to organize a series of virtual meetings with indigenous peoples. The country team will continue to work closely with FPAI and other partners to ensure that these consultations are carried out effectively. A second round of dialogue and consultation participation had to continue through remote platforms, as the next paragraphs will explain.


An online survey was first prepared and sent out together with a summary of the draft Funding Proposal, to 260 actors (representatives from indigenous organizations, government agencies, civil society organizations, NGOs and academia) who had participated in the regional workshops for the construction of the PANCBC described in previous sections (and in length the EASA annexes of this funding proposal), allowing for a broad representation of the different stakeholders involved. The survey was also translated into Spanish and publicly available on the MADIS website for continuous feedback.


As of 21 September opinions and contributions from 126 actors, representing 75 institutions were received (35% indigenous organizations, 40% government agencies, 17% NGOs and civil society organizations, 6% indigenous peoples, 3% small scale farmers, 12% governments). Regarding gender: 70% of the replies came from women and 30% from male actors. When it comes to provinces, all but two provinces of Argentina were represented in the answers.


The survey asked for the respondent to indicate his/her engagement with native forest management and in the REDD+ process, and to indicate which kind of impact (positive or negative) the proposed project would have on his/her organization. The respondents were also invited to provide comments on the draft Funding Proposal, in order of importance, and included questions on how potentially improve the project.


When asked to identify the impacts of the proposal, 71% considered only positive impacts; 16% listed positive and negative impacts while 23% only listed negative impacts. The most frequently mentioned were: strengthening of sustainable forest management, continuation of practices that produce income for local communities, reduction in deforestation, increase in the participation of small scale farmers, strengthening of the involvement of local communities and social inclusion. Within the negative impacts, respondents highlighted the lack of a clear definition of the project’s scope, which could arrive to no late or generate false expectations from communities; awareness of the need of a suitable monitoring and control system to achieve desired results.


The four components of the project were ranked in order of priority: 1) Territorial based forest management; 2) Forest-based economic development; 3) Strengthening institutional and increasing overall capabilities; and 4) Enhanced response to fire forests.


When asked to suggest potential improvements to the proposal, 75% of respondents included details that already appeared in the summary circulated with the survey. The remaining 25% did not include any suggestion but provided a summary of the questionnaire. 25% did not include any suggestion and 28% included suggestions which will be duly reviewed by the country team. Some of these suggestions are: inclusion of more specific information on the diffusion of the tools, involving other actors of the agricultural sector, promoting financial incentives for the private sector, and including local entities in certification and control systems, among others.


To foster continued dialogue and contribution from indigenous peoples a specific online meeting was organized on July 8th. On July 27 MAYDES maintained a new meeting with the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MAGyP in its Spanish acronym) to present in more details the main contents and the most updated implementation plan for the REDD+ plus RHP project.

The meeting was attended by 9 representatives of different areas within the MAGyP, including the National Forest Service, Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Natural Resources (MARN), Ministry of Sustainable Productions, Secretariat of Family, Peasant and Indigenous Agriculture, Under Secretariat of Politics and Administration, and the General Secretary of the National Forest.

The meeting focused on the project proposal development and implementation. The main areas of joint work include MBIg, the strengthening of native species, and the promotion of sustainable production systems in the deforestation basins. They also ratified their support promoting sustainable production schemes, carbon-neutral to avoid deforestation, working together even sharing platforms.

Furthermore, they valued the global approach of the proposal.


On August 10, 2020, MAYDES presented the funding proposal main contents to the Central de Workeurs of Argentina (CTA in its Spanish acronym). The CTA is an organization that brings together first-degree unions and federations. It has a strong presence at national level.

The meeting was attended by 34 representatives of the CTA based in different provinces of the country. The main issues addressed were the need for a global approach to deforestation, the need for a balanced approach between rural and urban transition, and key drivers of deforestation in the country. They emphasized that the project should be implemented at national level with a strong focus on the territories.

The Project was highlighted, as well as the participatory methodology used in planning. The CTA expressed their predisposition to contribute to the implementation of the project by means of their presence and influence in the territory. They highlighted that the CTA network is available and can help bring territorial projects closer.


On August 14, 2020, MAYDES presented the funding proposal main contents to the Argentine Forest Engineering Federation (AFIF in its Spanish acronym). AFIF is a civil and non-profit association, that brings together first-degree unions and federations of forest engineering workers. It has a strong presence at public and private organizations of national and international order.

The meeting was attended by 86 representatives of AFIF based in different provinces of the country. The main issues addressed were the need for investment made through private activity. In addition, they proposed to analyze the MBIg regulations and their application in the forest sector. They highlighted that the project should be implemented at national level with a strong focus on the territories.

The Federation made a call for all forestry professionals to improve the technical quality of the plans that are presented by the Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Natural Resources (MARN). The MARN has a great investment through private activity. In addition, they proposed to analyze the MBIg regulations and their application in the forest sector.

The Federation highlighted that the project should be implemented at national level with a strong focus on the territories. They emphasized that the region has its own vectors or drivers of deforestation and degradation, and they should be addressed. They highlighted the need to strengthen the management of native products. They maintained that the management of these resources is not only related to the amount of timber but also to the amount of available resources.


The Federation thanked and highlighted the openness and predisposition to contribute to the improvement of the quality of life of those who live in forests. MAYDES shared the online survey link for AFIF to give additional feedback, suggestions and contributions.

The meeting was recorded (and) is available upon request.


On September 14, 2020, MAYDES held a virtual meeting with representatives of NGOs and Civil Society Organizations (CSOs). The meeting was attended by 87 representatives from different organizations. Some of the organizations that participated were: Fundación para la Conservación de la Naturaleza y el Medio Ambiente (FCN), Asociación Española de Protección del Patrimonio Cultural (AEPUC), the Argentine Climate Youth, the Association for the Promotion of Culture and Development (ACPD), the Association of Montes Greco, ProYungas.

### REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

#### E2. Risk assessment

E2.1 For the period of the achieved results

From the outset, it is important to establish a baseline for an assessment of the historical performance of the activities undertaken and their track record against the risk tolerance levels specified in the Risk Appetite Statement and the Financial Management Plan.

Please note that this should consider only the applicable and relevant parts of the two above documents in the feedback document.

Argentina has a regulatory and institutional framework that promotes collective and individual human rights, transparent governance and public access to information. The legal framework on transparency and access to information on native forest management is funding for government agencies, and there are specific institutions established by law to ensure the implementation of the PansPCC and other REDD+ initiatives, including during the period results.

In general terms, the country has a very comprehensive regulatory framework dealing with the prevention of corruption practices and fraud in public administration, as well as access to public information, which


---
**Page 120**
---

were in place during the results period. These include:

- United Nations Convention against Corruption (UNCAC) of 2003 (Law No. 269/07, O.G. of 09 June 2008)
- Inter-American Convention against Corruption (IACC) of 1996 (Law No. 2475/06, O.G. of 13 January 1997)
- American Convention on Human Rights of 1969 ("Pacto de San José, Costa Rica") (Law No. 2385/04, O.G. of 27 March 1988); and
- International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights of 1966 (Law No. 2331/03, O.G. of 6 May 1986).

Relevant domestic regulatory elements that were applicable during the results period include:

- National Constitution Article 41 requires the authorities to provide public environmental information;
- Law on Access to Public Information No. 2725 (O.G. of 29 September 2016) guarantees the effective right to access public information and promotes civic participation and transparency in public administration;
- General Law on the Environment No. 256/07 (O.G. of 28 November 2002) requires all individuals and legal entities to provide environmental information on the activities they perform (articles 16, 17 and 18); and
- Forest Law No. 349/07 (O.G. of 25 October 2007) requires the Office of the AGN and the SIGEN to oversee and audit the FICINAC (Article 36).

The institutional framework for addressing transparency that was in place during the results period includes:

- MAyDS and COFEMA, the two government bodies that have the most experience in the generation, systematization and provision of public environmental information;
- AGN (the Office of the National Auditor General), an oversight body of the National Public Sector, issues reports on non-compliance with the law as allowed for in the Forest Law; AGN has an office that receives reports on any non-compliance of the government agencies it audits;
- The Office of the Controller General (SIGEN), an oversight body of the National Public Sector, audits the implementation of the Forest Law and has mechanisms to request information in person or electronically from public entities;
- The Anti-Corruption Office of the Ministry of Justice and Human Rights (Law No. 2323/03, O.G. of 14 November 2003) is responsible for investigating corruption cases.

These elements, along with the continuous risk analysis and monitoring carried out in the framework of the implementation of the Forest Law and the development of the PANDRCC demonstrate Argentina's capacity to effectively manage risks, in line with the GCF risk management framework. While the risk analysis exercise carried out during the results period did not specify a difference between inherent or residual risks, assessments included consideration of both risk categories and indicate that residual risk tolerance levels were not exceeded, nor were there any indications of prohibited practices.

While the risk analysis exercises carried out do not specify a difference between inherent or residual risks, the assessments included consideration of both risk categories and indicate that residual risk tolerance levels were not exceeded. The PANDRCC also includes mechanisms to prevent the occurrence of prohibited practices. Details on the prevention of prohibited practices are available in section E4.1.

A multistakeholder process for identifying risks associated with the REDD+ activities and the Strategic Operational Pillars of the PANDRCC, as well as the mitigation measures identified, is detailed in the EIA. The main challenges to be addressed include: (i) the need to ensure that the project contributes to sustainable long-term financing of activities, including implementation of the Forest Law; work-government relations and coordination with other government entities at national level, provincial level, and municipalities, and between different institutions responsible for territorial development policies in the same jurisdiction; conflicting infrastructure development plans in territories where ownership is not defined

### Introduction

or in degraded areas; poor oversight of illegal logging and timber movements, forest encroachment due to misapplication of incentives for non-merchantable activities; high agricultural prices and profitability of commodities (mainly soybeans); and reluctance of decision-makers, native forest owners, farmers, etc., to adopt new policies.

### National Programme on Protection of Native Forests

These risks were managed within the framework of the Forest Law, and through the National Programme on Protection of Native Forests (article 12, Law No. 26331) and the National Fund for Enrichment and Conservation of Native Forests (article 14, Law No. 26331). The number of forest law enforcement instruments available under the Forest Law increased significantly since 2010, with the highest numbers in the result period.

### Strengthening Institutions

During the results period there was a progressive strengthening of institutions such as MADES, CoFIMA and competent subnational agencies as well as the development of new non-state institutional capacities to implement forest law. The National Forest Law Enforcement Authority (NFEA) and the Office of the Auditor General (OAG) made note of the progress made in this regard, compared to the report auditing the period 2013-2014.

The Ministry for the Environment and Sustainable Development is responsible for the foresters functions to implement the Forest Law. A specific office to oversee the operations (Cooperation and Control Unit) was established” (AGN, 2017,p. 36).

### National Directorate of Forests

In 2014, the National Directorate of Forests (DNB) of MADES created the DNB Social Participation Unit (APDIF). This unit is responsible for the implementation and monitoring of forest law enforcement activities in response to requests for information. Within the framework of the participatory processes for obtaining the first version of the provincial OITs around 2012-2013, DNB’s Social Participation Unit advised that the participation of indigenous peoples was not being considered as a priority by the Ministry.

### Participatory Processes

According to different Forest Law regulations, as well as initiatives and actions in force during the Results Period, the local enforcement authorities must facilitate effective participation of all stakeholders, holding meetings with them. The DNB Social Participation Unit has been working closely with these stakeholders, on the other hand. During the Results Period, there were also tools such as the Forum on the Forests (FONAF) and the National Forest Dialogue, which are platforms for dialogue between MADES and 45 territory-based organizations, supported by technicians and officials from different ministries.

### Project Implementation

A number of projects supported the implementation and enforcement of the Forest Law, and mitigation of identified risks in the results period:

- The UNDP supported the implementation of the National Native Forest Protection Programme. Through this project (October 2012 to June 2020), with a request for extension to December 2020), UNDP supported MADES in managing and transferring Forest Law funds. Considering that the project is still active, the Ministry will continue to manage these funds following the project. MADES will still take responsibility for the management of the funds and their use.
- The Forests and Community Project (IBRD 4893), approved in November 2015, seeks to support the implementation of the Forest Law by strengthening the capacity of indigenous peoples’ forests, particularly in the provinces of Salta, Santiago del Estero and Chaco, through the promotion of participatory forest management.
- To do so, “Integral Community Plantation” (PICs) is its main policy.

## E.3 Gender considerations

### E.3.1 For the period of the achieved results

Further information on the continuous risk analysis and screening that will be carried out is available in section E.1.2. However, if the risk tolerance limit was not exceeded in the implementation of activities carried out or use of proceeds, no will any prohibited practices be supported.

The Government of Argentina has been working on mainstreaming gender in different public policies. In this regard, it has developed the following institutional and legislative instruments:

* Law No. 26485, on the Full Protection for Prevention, Punishment and Evaluation of Violence against Women in their Interpersonal Relationships (O.E. 14 April 2009): Its objectives are, inter alia: to establish a framework for the prevention, punishment and evaluation of violence against women; to eliminate discrimination between women and men in all the walks of life.
* Law No. 27118, on Family Agriculture (O.E. 28 January 2015): Its objectives are, inter alia: to contribute to the development of family agriculture as a strategic element for the transition from work to women; and to the rights and benefits enshrined in this law, taking actions and implementing specific policies for women who are engaged in family agriculture. The law also seeks to promote sustainable rural and indigenous agriculture, with special attention to women’s conditions and needs (Article 4.c).
* Law No. 25490, on Mandatory Training in Gender Issues for all National Officials working in the “Three Pillars” of the State: This law establishes a framework for the implementation of Institutional Training in Gender and Violence against Women, with a view to training and raising awareness among public officials at different levels.

Within the framework of the National Plan (PANDyC), an implementation strategy was developed, with actions aimed at ensuring gender equality in forest management programmes and policies, taking into consideration the following:

**a)** All policy initiatives have direct and indirect impacts on the population, and these effects are different for men and women, according to what a given society considers masculine and feminine in each context - gender roles.

**b)** There is a significant part of the population that cares for and depends on forests; among them, women have specific knowledge and relationships that must be taken into account; and

**c)** Women, as well as other agents across the territory (youth, ethnic minorities, landless farmers) are usually relegated to secondary roles in decision-making processes related to forest management and tenure.

In this regard, various activities have been carried out involving men, women, indigenous peoples and other social stakeholders in the construction of the PANHICC. Participation in quantitative terms in these regional mechanisms has reached 50% for women and 48% for men; while in qualitative terms it is 39% for women and 66% (Table 30), therefore, the minimum goal was reached of ensuring a 30% share of women.

The results show that there are still many challenges for women and men working on the ground. These implementation tools will be included in the "Guide for mainstreaming gender in forest-based change management: to support decision makers and actors involved in the implementation of the REDD+ Programmes" and will serve as a reference guide and forums for reflection on the grounds.

On the other hand, the institutional framework under which the respect for the safeguard on the implementation of a gender approach is addressed, is made up of the GNCCH, which carries out initiatives to mainstreaming the gender perspective in transparency promotion mechanisms; the DNEC that prepared the PANHICC; and follows up on the implementation of the National Action Plan against Gender-Based Violence (2018-2023) with the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity, which develops policies, programmes and initiatives to empower women and men as agents in the prevention and reduction of violence, among them the National Action Plan against gender-based violence (2020-2027).

# REDD plus BHP FUNDING PROPOSAL

Argentina has a broad national and international legal framework on gender and human rights that provides guidelines on the protection of human rights of women and communities. Therefore, the guiding principles to implement this proposal incorporate compliance with both national and international legislation on gender-related matters and human rights, as well as the establishment of social order in accordance with the principle of ensuring gender equality as well as the rights and full participation of women.

The objective of the Action Plan is to strengthen the implementation of the REDD+plus BHP Funding Proposal that is promoting gender equality and mainstreaming the gender approach in the forest management-related policies and actions included herein.

The efforts and resources of the actions included in this proposal thus focus on women and communities that live in or near forests, with a view to achieving a sustainable development and production model that also reduces the gender gap in access resources and participate in the benefits of public policies.

In this regard, effective participation of women in climate change mitigation initiatives is of fundamental importance. Women are key actors in forest management and their inclusion in the design of forest management policies and, also, because their experience and specific knowledge are an important contribution to the implementation of these policies. The gender approach will be mainstreamed into all aspects of the implementation of the proposal: the identification of issues such as allocation of access, use and control of resources and assets, and distribution of tasks according to certain gender roles.

Therefore, in addition to the quantitative indicators that are proposed and qualitative ones, so as not to reproduce and intensify existing gender inequalities and reduce gender gaps, in access to resources and participation of women in forest management policies or actions (such as social and environmental safeguards), etc., to mainstream a gender approach into all aspects of the implementation of this proposal.

The guiding principles of the implementation will be:

* Compliance with national legislation on gender equality.
* Application of social, gender and environmental safeguards guidelines that ensure gender equality and the rights and full participation of women.

Within this context, the priority objective of the Gender Action Plan of this proposal is to strengthen the economic empowerment of women in forest management policies and actions by providing women equal access to these resources and the possibility to decide on their use, taking into consideration the impact they may have on their livelihoods. It be productive or reproductive; to avoid any increased use in said workloads that could lead to a decrease in productivity and income for women.

The Gender Action Plan will identify gender-sensitive actions which should be adapted and mainstreamed when the activities included in the funding proposal are implemented. Additionally, specific indicators to measure and follow-up on these actions are proposed (See Annex G). The expected results are the following:

* Diagnostic studies on forest management policies and actions as well as disgregation of tasks, disaggregated by gender.
* Effective participation of women and men in the different actions included in the proposal.
* Equitable distribution of the benefits arising from the implemented actions.
* Technical staff of Forest Law national and local enforcement authorities trained in gender-related matters and human rights.

A gender equality officer is responsible for the implementation of the activities outlined within the Gender Action Plan. The composition of the team, their roles and the activities that they will carry out are detailed in Annex F (Gender Equality Officer Analysis & Gender Action Plans). Within this Annex it has also been included a specific Budget for Gender Action Plan implementation.

Argentina has a comprehensive regulatory framework designed to prevent and combat corruption practices in public administration. This framework helps ensure that the prohibitions and restrictions defined in the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices, occurred during the implementation of activities that led to REDD+ results. Argentina has developed a legal framework in accordance with international treaties to prevent and combat corruption.

Argentina has ratified international instruments including the United Nations Convention against Corruption (Law No. 20697), the Inter-American Convention against Corruption (Law No. 24789), the American Convention on Human Rights (Law No. 25854) and the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (1986).

The country has a legal framework promoting transparency in the governance of native forests, and access to information related thereto. This framework is based on Law No. 27275 on Access to Public Information (Law No. 27275) and the General Environmental Law (Law No. 25673).

The Law No. 27275 regulates the access of public information. The objective is to guarantee the effective exercise of the right of access to public information by citizens, as well as the transparency in public administration. It also aims to guarantee the effective exercise of the right of access to public information by citizens and the transparency in public administration.

An agency of access to public information has been created, as an autonomous entity that will operate within the Public Administration. The Law No. 27275 establishes a series of rules for the creation of internal state procedures for the processing of complaints for breaching the obligations contained in the Law and General Regulation of Access to Public Information.

To avoid possible breaches of the aforementioned law, a series of secondary instruments regulate the subject. Specifically, the framework Law regulating national public employment, Decree 856/61 on the regime of access to public information by employees of the National Public Service, Decree 2074/93 on the regime of access to public information by employees of the National Public Service, Decree 1179/2016 regulating Article 18 "Regime of gifts to public officials" of Law No. 25188, Decree 1335/2017 regulating Law No. 25857, and Decree 419/99 on the Code of Ethics in the Public Service.

Regarding the framework of access to public information by citizens, it is regulated together with Decree 3025/01 establishing incompatibilities and prohibitions related to financial information units. Decree 1068/79 regulating the regime for the public service, Decree 407/99 on the Code of Ethics, and Decree 407/99 regarding administrative investigations.

This legal framework has allowed Argentina to make progress in its investigation and prosecution efforts to ensure that Argentina has robust legal and institutional structures in place which have helped to avoid prohibited practices during the results period.

In line with the above, a series of measures and grievance redress procedures, appropriate grievance resolution mechanisms were made available during the results period for this proposal. These are described in more detail in the USAID report.

To prevent conflicts and make it easier to address claims and complaints within the framework of the Forest Law, the following citizen participation instruments have been operational since 2007, and available during the period of results for the proposal:

- Mandatory participation process for preparing OFHINs;
- Mandatory consultation for adopting Land Use Change Plans in low conservation value forests (III - green), and prior authorization of the Forest Law's local enforcement authority;
- COFEMA, as a political-technical discussion forum at the federal level (and specifically, in Native Forests) to address conflicts between citizens and public authorities;
- Buque Verde (Green Mailbox) MAVIS enabled this online public consultation mechanism that receives queries, complaints and allegations. This site lists the different communication channels and also includes the contact details of each directorate within the MAVIS, in case of more specific queries.
- Front Desk of the MAVIS: when a query is received at the front desk, it is opened through the

FAO's Office of the Inspector General (OIG) has the mandate to independently review any complaints about non-compliance with FAOs social and environmental standards that cannot be resolved at programme management level or by other means. The OIG also investigates allegations of fraud, corruption, and violations related to the Organization’s Environmental and Social Standards. Complaints must be made in writing and communicated to ORG by mail, courier, e-mail, fax, directly or via any FAO office. The OIG also investigates complaints about alleged breaches of the Organization’s Code of Conduct and conventions. It conducts investigations and inspections in compliance with the FAG Guidelines for Internal Administrative Investigations.

In addition, FAO has a Whistle-Blower Protection Policy in place. All FAO personnel are required to report any breach of the Organization's rules and to cooperate with the Organization's oversight functions. An individual who reports a violation or suspected violation is protected from retaliation. ### ENSURING INTEGRITY OF PROJECTS

The project proposal, which sets out how the activities to be undertaken with the use of resources will meet the requirements of the GCF environmental and social safeguards, standards and policies relevant to indigenous peoples, will be developed in close consultation with indigenous peoples.

In line with relevant GCF, FAO and UNCCD standards, the project will be developed and implemented in such a way as not to undermine or negatively impact understanding of indigenous peoples so that they will benefit from the activities in a culturally appropriate manner; and will not suffer harm or adverse effects from the design and implementation of the project.

The project proposal will also include an Indigenous Peoples’ Policy Statement, which will be developed for project design and implementation. It will also be a key input for the development of an Indigenous People’s Plan (IPP), which will be undertaken at project inception.

The minimum requirements of the legal framework for indigenous peoples include the following:

- **National Constitutions:** recognize the ethnic and cultural preexistence of indigenous peoples; guarantee respect for their identity, the right to bilingual and intercultural education and recognize the legal status of collective rights.
  
- **ILO Convention 169 (Law 20737):** O.G. of 4th April 1992: United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples States “indigenous peoples have the right to participate in the adoption of decisions that affect their lives, cultures and environments and other matters which affect them directly” (Article 6). Additionally, it states that governments shall consult with indigenous peoples through appropriate procedures when legislative or administrative measures may affect them directly. The law also establishes that indigenous peoples are entitled to good faith and in a form appropriate to the circumstances, with the objective of achieving agreement or consent to the proposed measures (Article 6).

As mentioned in previous sections, Argentina voted in favor of the UNDRIP in 2007, which states “indigenous peoples have the right to participate in the adoption of decisions that affect their rights” and “governments shall consult with them using appropriate procedures when legislative or administrative measures may affect them directly.” The law also establishes that indigenous peoples are entitled to good faith and in a form appropriate to the circumstances, with the objective of achieving agreement or consent to the proposed measures (Article 6). Although the country has not passed a national law on free, prior

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (Page 18 of 24)

### Prior to and informed consultation, within the framework of the national policy on indigenous peoples, significant progress has been made in this matter, including the establishment of institutions in which indigenous peoples participate, such as the National Indigenous Affairs Institute (INAI) and the Council on Indigenous Peoples (CIP). The country has also developed a number of tools to support the participation of indigenous peoples in forest management activities. These include guidelines for the development of community-based forest management plans and other tools that were developed; the country has progressed towards the realization of this important right.

Particularly concerning forests, the Forest Law (OAG 46 of 26 December) includes several provisions to promote the participation of indigenous peoples in forest management activities. These include small cooperatives (small farmers) communities among them:

- It exempts the law from the obligation enforced on all those under areas not exceeding 10 hectares, owned by the indigenous peoples or small farmers (Article 2).
- It recognizes indigenous communities as forest-dependent populations and forest inhabitants and, thus, establishes that they must consider them in defining measures for the sustainable use of natural resources within the National Forest Law.
- It indicates that 30% of the resources collected by the Forest Law Fund must be allocated to develop projects aimed at promoting the participation of small farmers and indigenous peoples in technical assistance programs aiming at the sustainability of amenable activities carried out by small farmers and/or indigenous peoples and/or local communities (Article 15).
- Specifically, it indicates that the national forest management plan or any other forest planning or sustainable management projects shall recognize and respect the rights of the original indigenous communities that have been established in accordance with the law. The Forest Law Fund is also mandated to sub-national enforcement authorities to ensure that the provisions enshrined in the above article are fulfilled before authorizing any clearing of native forests, when allowed by the law.

To ensure indigenous peoples, local communities and other vulnerable groups can adequately access the grievance mechanisms provided for under the law:

- The GRM will be identified or designed in consultation with the affected or potentially affected communities of indigenous peoples and other relevant stakeholders.
- The GRM will facilitate the resolution of grievances promptly, through an accessible, fair, transparent and culturally appropriate process.
- The GRM will be culturally appropriate and readily accessible, at no cost to the affected communities, without compensation to the individuals, groups, or communities that raised issues or concerns.
- Where feasible and suitable for the project, the grievance mechanisms will utilize existing formal or informal grievance mechanisms, supplemented as needed with project-specific arrangements.
- There shall be a clear description of the ways in which stakeholders can submit their grievances.
- Language barriers/limitations will be taken into account and there will be provision for interpretation/translation when possible.
- There will be provision to keep complainants' identities confidential, especially in instances where the concern involves violence or threats.
- Specific avenues for raising complaints will be identified in consultations with various social groups within the affected communities.

The activities proposed in this project proposal which involve or could affect indigenous peoples have been reviewed, with consideration of impacts as well measures to reduce impacts. Screening of activities and sub-activities against the criteria set out in the impact assessment report has been carried out during implementation.

Throughout the process to define sub-activities and lines of interventions, and to implement activities through the project proposal, we will ensure that indigenous peoples are fully involved in the full design and safeguarding of indigenous peoples' rights so that they (a) benefit from activities and projects in a culturally appropriate manner; and (b) do not suffer harm or adverse effects from the design and

# Monitoring of native forests in Argentina

To ensure consistency of data, project activities developed at the local level will be monitored by the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMIS) of Argentina's NIFMS), which provides updated information on the location, area and condition of forests, either continuously or at regular intervals. The NFMIS also serves for the monitoring of the performance of Forest Management Units.

The NFMIS is a system that includes four components:

- **Monitoring of Native Forests**: This component provides information on the distribution and extent of forests and the stages of experiences, using remote sensing and GIS tools, and cartography. The main stages are the processing and processing of satellite images, visual interpretation, and analysis by reference to field data. The system is coordinated by the National Forest Monitoring System Management Unit.
- **Deforestation Early Warning System**: This tool monitors the loss of native forest in an ongoing manner, through automated processes based on satellite images. The system, that is operational in the Parana sub-region, covers the entire country and includes the provinces of Buenos Aires, La Pampa, and the Serrano sub-region, and to Argentine Yungas region, generates alerts every 16 days.
- **National Forest Inventory**: This component provides information on the forest inventory drawn up between 1998 and 2006 which entailed a first step towards understanding the extent and status of native forests in Argentina. The National Forest Inventory is currently being updated to include new areas, with particularity that the plan will be permanently installed to allow re-monitoring and monitoring of changes in forest cover. The total number of plots for the national inventory, including those used for re-measurement purposes, is 4,158 distributed across the country. Throughout 2020, results will be available for all forest regions.
- **Forest Management Units**: This unit compiles information on the sale of wood and its by-products, based on information provided by the provinces and other agencies, which leads to processing and updating of the database.

Other tools that will be used to support monitoring activities are:

* Forest Management, Control and Verification System ("SACoFor"): Its fundamental objective is to manage the forest product traceability process by managing, controlling and verifying its different stages. It has a direct impact on the quality of wood products in Argentina's market, as it increases the level of legality in the sector and it increases transparency and social control over the use of the resource.
* Integrated Forest Information System (OFIM, in its Spanish acronym): This is a digital platform that aims at gathering information on native forests in a single site. This system contains a series of modules, among which is the module of the National Registry of Plans. This Registry shows information on the management and conservation plans for native forests, as well as the status of the forest inventory of native forests framed under the Forest Law. The projects implemented within this proposal will be registered by this system.
* The measurement in the field is carried out by DNIR in the role of National Enforcement Authority in close collaboration with Local Enforcement Authorities and other local agencies.

The information surveyed will be processed by Argentina's NIFMS and systematized by DNIR in the role of compiler and coordinator of the INIF. Emissions reductions will be reported through the BMU-GEI-AR.

FASD will ensure the existence of a well-designed, operational and effective impact monitoring and

The monitoring system is to measure the causal and attributable change, the contribution and the overall causal results of the project. The monitoring system should be designed to understand efficiency, targeting and verifying the assumptions that the program is making. It should also be used to generate information, data and lessons learned for the evaluation process at all levels of implementation. Progress will be measured against baselines, targets and indicators.

The monitoring system will be comprehensive and entail the Project Workplan as well as the action plans for gender, social inclusion, poverty reduction and the social and environmental framework.

The day-to-day project management and implementation responsibility rests on a recently recruited Project Manager that will lead the PMU. She will be supported by a monitoring and evaluation specialist, who will lead the M&E team. The Project Workplan will be developed in close collaboration with the PMU to ensure the efficient implementation of the project. The PMU will inform the FAO and CGIAR Country Office of any delay or deviation from the approved work plan. If such deviations occur, no application for corrective measures can be adopted. The PM will ensure that all project staff are aware of their level of responsibility and accountability in monitoring and reporting project results. FMO will support the PM as needed.

The Project Team will be supported by a Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist (M&E) and technical quality assurance and troubleshooting support will be provided by FAO as needed.

A project inception workshop will be implemented in order to: a) identify potential needs in fine tuning the project work plan; b) review the baseline data and indicators, including the assumptions that must be used to build a common understanding on the project strategy and discuss any change in the overall context that might affect the project implementation. The workshop will also include: c) discussion with the Project Advisory Committee Council and MRBI National Technical Committee, including reporting and communication issues; d) review of the project work plan, including the roles and responsibilities of the project team members' responsibilities; and to finalize the M&E plans; e) review financial reporting requirements; f) planning and scheduling Board Project meetings; and g) finalize the first year work plan. The final Inception Report will be approved by the FAO Country Office.

The PM and the M&E Specialist will ensure that the indicators in the results framework are monitored annually. The Annual Reports will be submitted to the CGIAR Country Office and the FAO Country Office within 30 days after due to GCF deadline of 60 days after the end of calendar year. The final project annual report and the terminal evaluation report will be submitted by the PMU to the CGIAR Country Office and the FAO Country Office, and field-end evaluations will be undertaken under the responsibility of FAO Office of Evaluation (OE).

According to the aforementioned, monitoring and evaluation at the project level will be carried out in accordance with the Project Monitoring and Evaluation Plan (M&E Plan) that is developed as part of the overall evaluation policy across the project life cycle. The Project Cycle establishes international standards and procedures for project management, improving accountability and quality through the principles of results-based management, including the use of indicators and performance measurement systems, internationally accepted corporate standards and procedures, including quality assurance criteria for phases 1 to 3 of all FAO's projects. The M&E Plan will be developed in accordance with the Project Workplan and will be aligned with the performance assessment during the implementation of the project (Initial Assessment, Annual Program, Project Review and Final Evaluation).

The project team and the FAO Country Office will carry out the M&E Plan in accordance with FAO and GCF procedures.

Performance indicators for project implementation are presented in section C.2. The project document within FPMIS will also include additional information such as the corresponding means of verification, a mid-term plan and annual reports to be submitted by the PMU, including a mid-term report, a mid-term and final evaluation. This information will be available online. The following sections describe the main elements of this monitoring system: a) description of the project indicators; b) description of the means of the indicators, means of verification, and a full definition of the M&E responsibilities of the project staff.

# REDD plus BHP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Legal arrangements

### General legal framework
- Provide an analysis with respect to legal title to REDD+ results in the country. This should include an overview of the relevant laws and regulations.
- Covenant that no other party has a common claim to the results proposed to the GCF in accordance with national policy, legal or regulatory frameworks.

## Argentina's legal framework does not explicitly define emission reductions ownership rights
The country currently aims to wait for further developments concerning Article 6 of the Paris Agreement, before enacting


---
**Page 134**
---

any legislation relating to ER rights. Until then – as indicated in the NDC (revised in 2016 section 3.5, Use of Market). Any transfer of emission reduction units achieved in Argentina shall be explicitly authorized by the National Government and, unless otherwise provided for, all emission reductions in the context of the GCF REED-ROPS will be subject to such authorization.

Nevertheless, the country's legal framework provides the foundation to MADxS mandate for receiving, managing and administering payments for forest environmental services, including GHG emission fixation. This is demonstrated by the implementation of payment provision for ecosystem services programmes (PES) in Argentina. The National Forest Policy (NFP) was established according to the Argentinean’s legislation. MADxS is in charge of protecting the value of forest environmental services and promoting their conservation and sustainable use, as provided for in article 12 of Law No.33:107. Therefore, the agreement reached in the context of the Federal Environment Council (COFEEMA) between the provinces and MADxS in relation to the GCF REED-ROPS, is consistent with the constitutional framework and the national legislation on environmental services.

Hereby follows a clear explanation in support to these assertions, with reference to the pertinent legal instruments:

As Argentina is a federal state (by preamble of the Argentinean Constitution), provinces hold the original domain over natural resources existing in their territory, according to article 10. The provinces have the original dominion over the natural resources existing in their territory (Decree No.248 of the Constitution). Therefore, the (provinces) shall provide for the protection of the right, the rational use of natural resources and the conservation of biodiversity, including forest biodiversity and also provide for environmental information and education” (article 14 par. 2).

“The Nation shall regulate the minimum protection standards” enforceable at national level “while the provinces those necessary to reinforce them, without altering their local jurisdictions.” (Section 41 of the Constitution). In particular, as reflected in article 41 of the National Constitution, the law No.25:675/02 establishes minimum standards for the protection and conservation of natural resources existing within national territory. According to article 6, the Nation adopts the necessary conditions to guarantee the dynamics of ecological processes and the maintenance of ecosystems, as well as the sustainable use of resources in a sustainable development. It also establishes in its Article 23 the Federal Environmental System implemented through the National Environment Council (COFEEMA).

In accordance with the constitutional provisions cited, the law on minimum standards for the environmental protection of native forests No.26:533/07 (hereinafter “law”) defines the minimum environmental protection standards applicable to native forests and their conservation. The law also establishes a regime and criteria for the distribution of funds derived from environmental services (including forest environmental services) in accordance with article 14.

In particular, according to this law, the Nation has the competence to establish minimum standard rules for the entire national territory, in order to guarantee a minimum protection over forest resources, while each provincial government is responsible for the implementation and enforcement of such standards. As it relates to ERs, article 3 of the forest laws state that native forests provide the following environmental services among others: conservation of biodiversity; soil and water quality conservation; carbon sequestration and storage; and provision of ecosystem goods and services.

MADxS, designated as the competent national authority, is in charge of implementing the national forest protection programme, aiming to “promote the creation and maintenance of sufficient and functional forest reserves (the so-called forest core) of the national territory, in order to avoid adverse ecological effects and


---

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND
Page 14 of 154

There is a definition of Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) ownership rights, instead the law clearly states that MADS is responsible to protect the value of the environmental services provided by native forests (article 12, numeral c)). The provinces concur in supporting MADS' mandate. In this regard, the National Executive Power has issued a resolution (No. 360/2018) on the planning and use of native forests, according to specified conservation criteria. Specifically, each jurisdiction must carry out its own forest management plan, which is subject to review by the National Forest Authority in accordance with the law.

The National Environmental Authority, i.e., MADS as the request of the Enforcement Authority, has issued a resolution (No. 360/2018) on the planning and use of native forests for land out use planning of native forests in their jurisdiction (article 6). The national enforcement authority shall be the National Secretariat for the Environment and Sustainable Development** (i.e., MADS)** (article 11).

Considering that Argentina is a federal state, it is therefore necessary to reach consensus and promote agreements between the provinces and the central administration. i.e., MADS, for the implementation of the forest law.

The Federal Environmental Council (COFEMA) created by the General Law for the Environment (Anexo I) constitutes an advisory body that provides recommendations on environmental matters to the federal central administration. It also instructs the National Executive Power to propose, as appropriate, the issuance of decrees and resolutions in accordance with the law.

The Federal Environmental Council (COFEMA) is responsible for the effective application of laws of minimum budgets, the complementary role of provinces and their regulations in alignment with the national environmental policy, and the implementation of a comprehensive environmental policy and manages international financial resources derived from environmental projects (Articles 23 and 24 of the law; Anexos I of the law, Article 2, subsections 1 and 11).

Since the enactment of the forest law and the approval of its regulatory decree, COFEMA's resolutions and decisions have been issued in accordance with the provisions of the law, which is essential for the implementation of the forest law.

In relation to the subject matter, a resolution ratified by the Assembly of COFEMA, confirms Argentina's commitment to participate at the GCF RBP Pilot Programme promoted by MADS as the national REDD+ entity, and (ii) for MADS to offer part of the achieved ER results during the period 2014-2018 in order to support the implementation of the National Forest Management Plan (NFM).

MADS for this Funding Proposal was also presented and discussed and approved within COFEMA during that occasion.

Previous resolution 42/2018 signed by COFEMA, the country's results-based payments proposal was declared "of federal interest" and all members, including therefore all the provincial jurisdictions, unanimously agreed that MADS was the entity designated to progress with the preparation of the REDD+ project in accordance with the law.

The assembly also unanimously agreed to support the benefit-sharing distribution scheme presented by MADS for the implementation of the National Forest Management Plan (NFM) and the REDD+ project (approved by COFEMA Res. No 360/2018). The MADS, in its capacity as manager of the process of the RBP programme, will be responsible for the preparation of the results-based payments proposal for the previous year, indicating the amounts per province and forest category (Article 37, Forest Law).

The law 27520 established the National Climate Change Cabinet (GNCC) in its Spanish acronym technically coordinated by MADS through its Secretariat of Climate Change and Sustainable Development. The GNCC plays a role of utmost importance as a platform for inter-sectorial coordination and dialogue between different actors from various sectors, representing different bodies from the national and sub-national levels (provincial authorities). # REDD+ BRF FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND Page 183 of 244

### COFEPA is already ongoing and it would be important to achieve further agreements on carbon rights topics.

Argentina will include specific safeguards and criteria in the benefit sharing scheme to ensure that all parties, including vulnerable groups, have no complaints regarding the results that are being offered by GCF. The objective of these measures is to avoid any potential conflicts between indigenous communities or owners of plans in forest regions that have significantly contributed to achieving ERs; iii) paying special attention to the rights and interests of indigenous peoples.

Furthermore, to ensure a fair distribution of GCF-derived payments to vulnerable groups, MyDBs are entitled to administer ERs. They shall recognize and respect the rights of indigenous communities that have traditionally used forest resources for their livelihoods.

Lastly, it should be noted that in Argentina, following the guidelines of the UNFCCC, the construction of REDD+ pilot projects is subject to a national or regional level assessment rather than an individual or a forest region level. This approach does not permit to identify stakeholders or specific activities that originated ERs in its territory (e.g. farm-level) with scientific rigor and accuracy.

## To sum up:

* The country will use REDD+ results attained across Argentine territory to meet in NDC within the context of UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement, including the ERs offered to the GCF RBP pilot program. These results are based on the data collected from the baseline period (2013-2015) and those obtained from the project implementation. The NDC (revised in 2016: Section 3.5, Use of Markets) indicates that any reductions achieved is not transferable and shall not be used for other purposes than to meet the country’s NDC commitments.

* According to Argentina legislation, the provinces originally own natural resources, including native forests. The provincial governments are responsible for their management (GFRs) are administered by MyDBs, as reflected in the COFEPA Resolution 424/2020.

* The provinces, through COFEPA, unanimously agreed that MyDBs was to charge to prepare the feasibility study and the implementation plan of REDD+ pilot projects.

* The provinces approved the benefit-sharing distribution scheme proposed by MyDBs, by COFEPA Resolution 385/2019.

* According to Argentina legislation, and endorsed by COFEPA, MyDBs is authorized to regulate this subject in full respect of economic and environmental safeguards.

* The Ministry of Environment and Energy (MEE), through COFEPA, are the most appropriate platforms for federal and multilateral dialogues and articulations including for issues related to ER rights and related benefit-sharing arrangements and schemes.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP142

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Project background

The project presents Argentina’s REDD-plus RBP results for the period 2014–2016, with an estimated reduction in emissions of 18.73 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) derived from forest and land use change and forestry (LULUCF). It was developed by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) secretariat and published on the UNFCCC Land, REDD+, and Forests Platform. The project is a results-based payment for results-based payment (RBP) as part of the REDD-plus RBP programme; they are fully compliant with the requirements set out in Decision 18/07 of the Conference of the Parties to the Convention on Biological Diversity (Decision 18/07) and with UNFCCC standards. The results have undergone analysis based on the sources as shown in the annex.

Argentina has also implemented additional activities that support the implementation of its nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and in supporting the implementation of the Paris Agreement on climate change forests and Climate Change (PARBCC). The RBP project will focus on the following components:

- Component 1: Territorial forest management;
- Component 2: Forest management with integrated livestock;
- Component 3: Strengthening institutional responses to forest fires; and
- Component 4: Technical capacities of national and provincial government institutions to address the drivers of deforestation and undertake forest monitoring and control.

### Scorecard results

As per decision 18/07, the proposal has been assessed by the Secretariat with the support of land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) experts using the scorecard for REDD-plus RBP. The score was discussed and agreed upon with the independent Technical Advisory Panel. The scorecard results are summarized in table 1 and presented in greater detail in the annex.

| Table 1: Scorecard results |
| --- |
| **Scorecard section** | **Results** |
| Carbon elements | Score 48/48 All criteria “pass” |
| Forest Subgroups | All criteria “pass” |

<https://redd.unfccc.int/>

### Strengths and points of caution

6. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the remarks shown below:

#### Strengths
- The use of proceeds will be reinvested to deepen implementation of the ambitious National Forest Conservation Plan for Climate Change and for supporting the enforcement of Argentina’s Forest Law.
- The project provides a mandate for Territorial Planning for Native Forest by the provinces as well as incentives for forest conservation and rehabilitation through the National Fund for Enrichment and Conservation of Native Forests.

#### Points of caution
- As per the terms of reference, GCF will make the payments in a single disbursement.
- The project implementation will mostly be supervised directly by the accredited entity. Nonetheless, annual reporting will be provided to GCF.
- Risks related to forest fires need to be closely monitored by the project executing entities at local and national levels. The national forest monitoring system needs to be

# Assessment of performance against investment criteria

## Impact potential Scale: High

- The total volume of REDD+plus results achieved by Argentina and reported in the REDD-plus technical report for the period 2014–2016 was 165,172,705.82 tCO₂e (44,409,036.02 tCO₂e for 2014, 59,006,338.00 tCO₂e for 2015 and 41,757,329.78 tCO₂e for 2016). This represents a 56 per cent reduction in the historical emissions from deforestation during 2014–2016.
- These results stem from the gross emission reductions based on the activity "avoided deforestation and forest degradation" (AFDD) and are included as part of the total volume offered to GCF in the funding proposal. During the period 2014–2016, and concerning the results achieved by Argentina, the AFDD activity was implemented through direct investment or financial entity or participated in voluntary carbon market schemes. The results achieved by Argentina are in part the outcome of the early development of REDD-plus readiness interventions, including the establishment of a national REDD+ strategy for approval and implementation of the ambitious PAnBCyF. The total volume of emission reductions achieved by Argentina through the AFDD activity is included as part of the total volume offered to GCF.
- Results have been reported in the REDD-plus technical report submitted directly to the COP of the Parties to the UNFCCC on REDD-plus. The achievement of the results contributed directly to the GCF funding proposal and will be used for the implementation of projects, through the use of proceeds, which will lead to a continued reduction in emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, and enhancement of forest stocks and conservation.
- Among the four reference areas (two humid forests and two dry forests or forest regions), Argentina demonstrates ambition to scale up on the national level, mainly through the implementation of REDD-plus activities under the National Forest Policy and Strategy under Argentina's NDC. The four regions considered at the subnational level are Parque Chajofres, Sierra Tucumán (Sierra de Yungas y Sierra Misionera [Serra Paranáense] and Espinal [Córdoba] and Nandarí). These reference areas represent 10 million hectares of forest in these four regions covering around 50 million hectares (ha) comprising 65 per cent of the area of

all areas of Argentina and approximately 90 per cent of the country’s total forest land. Moreover, around 46 per cent of the indigenous peoples of Argentina live in these four regions.

12. In Argentina, approximately 16 per cent of national greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions are related to deforestation, which had an increasing trend until 2008, when the Forest Law was enacted. The law established a moratorium on forest concessions and protected certain regions holding native forests. The above-mentioned four regions have heterogeneous socioeconomic conditions and different levels of pressure from human activities such as deforestation and forest degradation are varied to respond to and comply development processes. The average deforestation rate in Argentina between 1986 and 2017 was 3,542 ha/year (ranging from 394,374 ha in 2002 to 183,368 ha in 2018). Argentina lost 5,313,919 ha native forests in this period.

13. The country continues to build an ambitious and powerful approach to REDD+ plus based on the activities considered in PanNACC, which constitutes the national REDD+ plus strategy; and the GCF funding proposal. PanNACC is a collaborative initiative with key partners, including the UN-REDD National Programme and the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility. Readiness Fund (PanNACC) builds upon five strategic structural pillars: governance; strengthening forest management institutions and capacity building; carbon stock valuation; and knowledge management. These structural pillars serve as cornerstone for the prioritization of activities in the funding proposal. The PanNACC strategy, based on use of the proceeds offers the opportunity to support Argentina in addressing the different drivers of deforestation and forest degradation (land-use change, management and illegal activity) and achieving additional REDD-plus results in the future.

### 2.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

14. The initiation of PanNACC helped Argentina to streamline and coordinate REDD-plus efforts through an ambitious and strategic approach. This has helped and is expected to continue to support the country in reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (Emission Reductions) and forest degradation (Forest Carbon). PanNACC supports the country in recording and reporting results in full compliance with UNFCCC provisions for REDD-plus results. The funding proposal will also help Argentina to strengthen its capacity on governance and forest landscape management so that undertaken by Argentina and allow for financial mechanisms to be blended in.

15. The reinvestment of the GCF proceeds into PanNACC through supporting an ambitious set of interventions proposed and described in the funding proposal will help Argentina to further reduce deforestation, forest degradation and forest fires. It will also strengthen linkages with the private sector and indigenous peoples, which will in turn help the country to move its forests from being a source of emissions to becoming a sink for carbon. The funds from GCF seek to complement existing funding sources (e.g., World Bank, International Bank for Reconstruction and Development) as well as to attract other donors and financial mechanisms to blend in for the implementation of PanNACC.

16. PanNACC establishes the ambitious actions required to reduce deforestation, forest degradation and forest fires by 2030. It also sets out how Argentina will strengthen its role in the country’s development via the following strategic operational pillars: (1) sustainable forests management; (2) strengthening governance of forests and land use; (3) restoration of degraded lands; (4) forest restoration and recovery, and (5) land-use planning. PanNACC is consistent with the general procedures and objectives of Argentina’s Law on minimum environmental protection standards for the conservation and sustainable development of native forests (Forest Law). The GCF proceeds will support the implementation of all PanNACC strategic operational pillars.

### Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

Argentina is a middle-income economy with a long history of macroeconomic instability and with limited national and subnational resources for the forest and land use sector. Argentina’s limited long-term economic performance has affected the country’s capacity to reduce poverty.

The enactment of the Forest Law provided a framework for controlling deforestation, promoting sustainable forest management, and encouraging the sustainable use and management of the forest landscapes in different regions holding native forests. The Forest Law allowed the creation and operationalization of the National Fund for Forests (Fondo Nacional de Bosques), which has channelled around USD 287 million in a 10-year period (2010-2019) to the different provinces. However, the lack of implementation of the Forest Law in some of the provinces as foreseen in the Forest Law, this has hindered the consolidation of the policy frameworks and their effective implementation.

meetings had to replace in-person sessions owing to the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic. Evidence of this process was provided in an annex to the funding proposal.

### 2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

Argentina’s REDD+ pilot project for 2014–2016 presented to GCF and the suggested budget both clearly highlight that a fixed price of USD 5 per tCO2eq will be applied to the project, which is cost-effective and in accordance with the TOB of the pilot programme. Furthermore, the agreement on the price of carbon credits with the private sector is also a key factor, which will allow Argentina to continue its reduction of emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+) beyond 2016.

The reinvestment into PABN/CC further helps Argentina to reach economies of scale together with other donors currently supporting PABN/CC and brings different donors and partners on board. The project will also benefit from the existing REDD+ experience, which will complement ongoing support from the World Bank and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD).

Furthermore, numerous lessons learned and best practices are expected to arise from the implementation of activities and coordination with other funding sources, which will be crucial in order to ensure that Argentina can continue its efforts beyond 2016. The project will be important with regard to the private sector and in seeking to increase its engagement in REDD+ pilot projects. This is particularly relevant for Argentina as it has a strong interest in agricultural and livestock supply chains.

Finally, Argentina has developed an internal accounting registry for REDD+ results that allows for the tracking of credits traded and avoidance of double counting of emission reductions. This system responds to the national monitoring, reporting and verification system and applies the same methodology used by the GCF. The project will also include a mechanism for self-claiming for payment for results or similar compensations, as well as those interested in registering initiatives that contribute to the national goal of emission reductions.

### III. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 3.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The Secretariat confirms the category B classification. The accredited entity (AE) has prepared an Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) as well as an environmental and social management framework (ESMF). The use of a third-party ESA provider was also prepared further to reviewed documents submitted by the AE. There appears to be no red flag on this proposal. A more detailed assessment and report will be prepared towards finalizing the decision-making process.

This project proposal is for RFP for Argentina’s REDD+ programme. The payment claimant is responsible for emission generation for the 2014–2016 period totaling 1,653,770 tCO2eq of which 18.73 million tonnes are eligible under this proposal. The proposal is claiming RFP for emission reductions generated in 2014–2016 as a result of the


---
**Page 151**
---

### Implementation of Argentina’s Forest Law (Law No. 26.311: Law on Minimum Environmental Protection Standards for Native Forests) which was passed in 2007.

The Forest Law provides a mandate for territorial planning for native forest by the provinces as well as incentives for their conservation and sustainable use, including through the implementation of land-use change plans (LUCP). Under the Forest Law, owners of forest lands may implement management plans, conservation plans or land-use change plans (LUCP).

The Forest Law is used to base the implementation of Argentina’s GF/CC, which will have four components:

- Territorial forest management, which involves planning and implementing forest management activities in accordance with the law by designing and implementing integrated community plans.
- Forest management with integrated livelihoods, which involves developing and implementing management for forests that are of medium conservation value (category II) and low conservation value (category III).
- Forest restoration and enhanced response to forest fires, which involves implementing forest restoration projects and land-use change plans for the prevention of forest fires and strengthening initial response capabilities in priority regions.
- Strengthening the technical capacities of national and provincial government institutions.

### Environmental and Social Risk Category and Safeguards Instruments. FAO is in its capacity as the Secretariat of the ESF, which is a component of the environmental and social management framework (ESMF) which also contains an indigenous people participation component. The ESMF provides a framework for applying safeguards applied to past activities that generated the emission reductions, while the ESMF provides the framework for assessing and managing the environmental and social (ES) risks and the ES impacts.

The AE has assessed the project to be of moderate risk (category B). The Secretariat agreed with this assessment. The AE has identified a number of issues regarding the forests through a participatory development process. The main risks of this project and the methods used to address them are as follows:

- **Environmental Risks:** The ESF will not involve the management of settlement/land conflicts. The past activities involved enforcement of the Forest Law and other laws including those related to territorial participation, land use change, forest restoration, and delineation of areas for voluntary land-use change or remnant forests which may result in the current occupants being displaced, or pressured to leave or accept the new rules of access to resources. These risks will be managed through a participatory development process carried out by the AE through or on behalf of the communities. Informal settlers/agricultural colonists could be pressured to leave their land and/or face restrictions on their activities in the forest. The AE’s due diligence report cited the alignment of national laws and regulations with its own ESF safeguards.

For the proposed use of proceeds, it is clear in the ESF that subprojects/activities which would involve involuntary relocation/displacement of settlers/occupants or those that would entail involuntary restrictions on land use and access to natural resources will not be eligible for funding. The AE has stated that these exclusion criteria reportedly still would be validated and confirmed in the ESS analysis to be carried out by the AE.

- **Indisputable impacts on the ecology and biodiversity:** There is always a possibility that efforts to conserve the forest could have undesirable negative impacts on the ecology of


---
**Page 152**
---

the site and on the quality of the forest itself. The ESA/covering checklist asserted that implementation of the Forest Law during the results period did not involve reforestation actions, land-use changes or use of any alien or non-native species. Although the Forest Law was amended in 2015 to include these activities under this law the approval of LUCPs is subject to environmental and social impact assessment.

Regarding the use of processes, forest restoration and forest management with livestock integration may incentivize the expansion of economic activities into primary/native forests. This could result in negative impacts on biodiversity and ecosystem services due to the introduction of non-native species which may turn out to be invasive and promote spread of diseases. The Forest Law prohibits the introduction of alien or non-native species, including the exclusion list. In particular, activities that require significant conversion of critical natural habitats (e.g., primary forests) are subject to a detailed environmental impact study. This will be considered, while the use of non-native species and genotypes to increase forest productivity is restricted. In addition, forest restoration and forest management with livestock integration may also result in negative impacts on biodiversity and conservation value forests.

The following section describes the activities that generated the emission reductions during the results period. The legal enforcement and/or implementation of previous activities and special projects may have had unresolved/undiscovered impacts or issues that could affect the ability of the project to continue generating emissions reductions. These impacts or issues did not result in unresolved/undiscovered impacts of past/results period activities but the Secretariat considers it prudent to include them as part of the risk assessment, based on supplementary data and/or findings from national and provincial oversight agencies.

The following section provides a summary of how the RFP activities and the proposed use of project revenues will be aligned with the Cancun Safeguards, the GCF environmental and social safeguards standards and the requirements for stakeholder engagement as outlined in the GCF’s Safeguard Standard 1.

3.3.1 Consistency of the design and implementation of REDD-plus activities with the Cancun safeguards and the GCF and accredited entity environmental and social safeguards

The ESA, which was the main due diligence instrument, relies heavily on the AE's review of the alignment of Argentina's national legal framework; and the AE found the framework to be satisfactorily aligned with its own ES standards and hence, by virtue of the AE’s accreditation, also in line with the GCF environmental and social safeguards. The ESA considered that (mainly in the form of multi-stakeholder workshops and joint learning with experts) that have been conducted under the auspices of the Forest Law and the Forest Law Implementation Plan in the context of the Forest Law. In addition, the ESA showed that each activity proposed under the Forest Law was subject to a detailed environmental impact study (EIS) and social impact assessment (SIA) and vetted against a set of strict environmental and social criteria before the activity was approved.

The ESA found that the country's labour laws are consistent with the AE’s devot work standards. It also indicated that pollution was unlikely to be an issue in the implementation of the Forest Law, as it is already regulated by national law. The ESA considered that mitigation activities and clarified whether pollution was an issue and whether adequate mitigation measures were in place. In addition, the ESA reviewed the environmental impact on and improved the ecology of the area in terms of presence of wild animals and disease vectors. However, the ESA points to the consistency of the country's legal framework with the AE’s standards.
