### 3.2 Gender policy

The accredited entity (AE) has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan (GAP), and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of GCF.

#### The gender assessment provided by the AE demonstrates Argentina's commitment to gender equality and women’s rights, as well as its adherence to international conventions that promote gender equality and the rights of women, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the Inter-American Convention for the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Belém do Pará Convention). In addition, Argentina ratified the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Belém do Pará Convention) and has a number of laws that address gender equality and women’s rights. The AE also provides information on laws on gender quotas and gender parity, which seek to promote the participation of women in the various areas of public life.

The Gender Equality Act is a milestone in the development of public policies with a gender perspective. Argentina adopted the UN Agenda for Sustainable Development


---
**Page 155**
---

# Gender Equality and Forest Degradation

The Green Climate Fund underscores its commitment to mobilize necessary resources to implement a gender approach, particularly taking into account the needs of women who are in a situation of greater vulnerability. Further, the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity launched the National Plan of Action for Women 2018-2023, which includes a plan drawn up with the participation of over 3,000 people from across the country.

Although the enabling environment exists, there remain significant gender gaps and challenges in women's access to land ownership. While women are increasingly involved in family and community-related care activities, women dedicate almost double the time to these compared to men (45 per cent vs. 23 per cent). Women also have lower rates of formal employment (35 per cent vs. 19 per cent), it still falls short of the employment rate of men, which is close to 50 per cent. Data also show that women have to have more education credentials than men to match their male counterparts in terms of employment and earnings.

In the lowest income group women outnumber men by 2 to 1 and in the highest income group they outnumber them by 3 to 1 (4,897 women vs. 1,560 men). In the lowest income group women outnumber men by 2 to 1 and in the highest income group they outnumber them by 3 to 1 (4,897 women vs. 1,560 men).

Progress in women's representation in political positions has also been achieved. Women hold 10 per cent of seats in parliament and 11 per cent of ministerial positions.

Women account for historically small percentages of landowners. Gender inequality in land ownership relates to male preference in inheritance, the privileges enjoyed by men in marriage, male bias in access to credit and other financial services, and women's limited participation in the land market, and this has exacerbated women's unequal access to finance, training and capacity building.

The advances of the agricultural frontier continue to threaten indigenous peoples' ancestral territories, forests and fresh water sources. These water sources are often reservoirs of biodiversity and provide food for wildlife. Women play a key role in managing these natural resources and water sources can be even more significant for women and can be more challenging in situations of insecure land tenure.

The assessment also indicates the differential impact of deforestation and forest degradation on women. Women's roles and engagement with the forest are mostly not given attention because of the perceived low value of the roles women play in sustaining the well-being of forests, which is often undervalued by men. Women's engagement with the forest can be undermined and their use of forest is for household consumption and unpaid, whereas men's engagement with the forest is more likely to be remunerative and linked to formal economic activity.

The assessment also indicates that the ongoing engagement and pressure of the real estate sector on forests has a significant impact on women in rural communities, depriving them of much-needed resources for sustaining their livelihoods, as well as resulting in loss of land tenure security and access to forest resources.

The impacts of forest degradation and deforestation also give rise to specific vulnerabilities for women, including increased vulnerability to violence. Women have low levels of participation in decision-making; and physical vulnerability due to situations of violence. Violence against women continues to be a prevalent issue, including in the forestry sector. Women continue to face numerous forms of gender-based violence and discrimination, including symbolic violence and exclusion by local leaders, traditional leaders and authorities.

## Risks

### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

#### Under the pilot programme for REDD+ plus BGF, GCF is requested to provide a payment of USD 10 million for the reduction in emissions of 18.73 MtCO2e out of the 165,172,705.42 tCO2e captured by the project during the period 2014–2016.

The total amount of emission reductions for which GCF funding is sought within 10 per cent of the total payable value as required under the TOR of the pilot programme.

#### Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (low risk)

The AE, FAO, has an extensive track record in the implementation of projects in developing countries. It is considered a reliable partner to support the REDD+ plus project in Argentina and it has already been involved with other GCF-funded projects in collaboration with MyDS, which will operate as a co-executing entity. It will also closely coordinate with other implementing partners, such as the Government of MAVIS carried out under the United Nations system showed a low risk for the entity.

### Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### Risk of double payments

The emission reductions achieved by the country will be registered in the Lima REDD+ Information Hub. In addition, MyDS will register payment received from GCF and other sources on its own platform, which is also connected to the Argentina's registry. The registry is operational, and serves to increase transparency, identify overlaps and double payments.

The Argentinean Government did not request payment for any entity, mechanism, scheme or market apart from under the GCF REDD+ plus project.

#### Use of proceeds

Argentina will use the proceeds from BGF to support the implementation of PanBioCC, which was proposed as a public policy instrument to comply with the national commitments assumed under the Paris Agreement and within the framework of

3.4 Results monitoring and reporting

A monitoring and evaluation plan with detailed information on project activities and outputs, including a final report and financial certification, will be carried out in accordance with the procedures of the AE and GCF and will include an initial report, annual performance reports (APRs), and a final report at the end of the project implementation.

Following the requirements of the TOB, the proposal will follow a simplified reporting procedure for which a specific template was developed by the Secretariat. The frequency of reporting will be determined in accordance with the funding proposal.

Monitoring the use of proceeds will be conducted at the main activity level in accordance with the description provided in the funding proposal.


3.5 Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018 and became effective on 4 October 2018.

The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and has the capacity and authority to implement the project. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board (I) the AE will have obtained an updated legal opinion from its counsel confirming that all necessary approvals from the AE in form and substance satisfactory to GCF confirming that all final internal approvals have been obtained; and that the AE has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Argentine Republic, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, risks which need to be further assessed by the Secretariat. The AE has confirmed that it has obtained all necessary approvals and immunities to the Government of Argentina in April 2016. Discussions are currently ongoing with the Government of Argentina regarding the issuance of a letter confirming having been sent by the Secretariat to the Government of Argentina on 17 September 2019.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both indicated that they will review the project documentation, including any further investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. The AE has confirmed that no projects to which GCF be made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

Green Climate Fund

Argentina’s second FREL including the sources of activity data (PAD) and emission factors (EFs) as well as the definition of forest land.

In the FREL, Argentina provided the existing forest definitions in the country and explained that it applied operative definitions to distinguish between forest land and other land uses. These definitions were used by Argentina to report to the Food and Agriculture Organization’s Global Forest Resources Assessment (FRA) 2015 and as an adaptation of the Food and Agriculture Organization’s definition of forest land, a definition of forest land and other forest land to account for the national circumstances in Argentina. This definition was also applied by Argentina in its GHG inventories which also include forest plantations.

Argentina informed AF that the FREL is consistent with the national GHG inventory to be included as part of the third BiUR, which is to be submitted in 2018.

The FREL is calculated on the basis of the annual average of GH emissions from deforestation and degradation “using data from the historical reference period 2002–2012.”

In preparing its FREL, Argentina followed UNFCCC decision 12/CP.17.

The technical assessment report (TAR) concludes that the information used by Argentina in constructing its FREL for the activity “deforestation and degradation” is accurate, reliable and complete and in overall accordance with the guidelines for submission of national communications under Article 4.1(b). The TAR considers that, in doing so, Argentina followed UNFCCC decision 12/CP.16, paragraph 5(a), and UNFCCC decision 12/CP.17, paragraph 10 on applying a stepwise approach.

Then TAR concludes that the information used by Argentina in constructing its FREL for the activity "reducing emissions from deforestation" is transparent and complete. The TAR notes that the transparency and completeness of the information provided have improved in the modified FREL submission, without having to alter the quantitative results.

**(v)** Is the FREL/FRL complete? (Has information been provided that allows for the reconstruction of the FREL/FRL?)

The TAR concludes that the information used by Argentina in constructing its FREL for the activity "reducing emissions from deforestation" is transparent and complete and in overall accordance with the requirements, including all information on reference levels.

**(vi)** Is the FREL/FRL consistent? (Were data and methodologies applied consistently over the time series used for the construction of the FREL/FRL?)

The TAR has not found any issues with the consistency of the data and methodologies used to construct the FREL.

**(vii)** Is the FREL/FRL accurate? (The date and methodologies used neither overestimate nor underestimate emissions and/or removals during the reference period, so far as can be judged)

The TAR identified several areas for future technical improvement that would contribute to increasing the accuracy of the FREL. These include:

(a) Use of available species-specific wood densities for deriving the biomass values used to obtain the FRL to increase the accuracy of the estimates.

(b) Establishment of additional sampling units in the forest regions of concern, particularly in the provinces of Misiones and Corrientes.

(c) Use of ground data to derive estimates of OTF instead of using a biophysical model for estimating the biomass of trees.

(d) Enhancement of the data acquisition process for the FOT class soil wetlands to avoid double counting or consequential identification of forest cover loss in this forest type.

**(viii)** Have all REED-plus activities that are significant sources of emissions been included?

The subtotal FREL proposed by Argentina includes CO2 emissions from deforestation and degradation. Deforestation is defined as the loss (clear-cut) of native forest cover from the conversion of forest land to other land use categories.

The AT acknowledges that "reducing emissions from deforestation" is the most significant activity of the five activities identified in decision 1/CP.16, paragraph 78. The AT noted that other activities could also be significant, in particular reducing emissions from forest degradation and land-use change. The AT further acknowledged the relevance of this activity to the CO2 emissions but indicated that it was not included in the national inventory as emissions from the processes leading to forest degradation prevented the use of the reference level for forest degradation in the FREL of the present submission.

During the discussion, Argentina informed the AT that it would address this issue and provided further information. Argentina included a summary of its comprehensive review of scientific and technical research projects related to the assessment of forests affected by degradation, which might provide guidance on how to include emissions from forest degradation in the national inventory. Argentina informed the AT that information from the second national forest inventory could be used for the reference level but also be used to estimate emissions from forest degradation.

The FREL does not include dead organic matter (above and below ground biomass). Argentina did not include dead organic matter pools (after land abandonment) and soil organic carbon (in mineral or peat soils), subject to further research. The AT noted that dead organic matter and soil carbon pools were not included in the national inventory of Argentina's third national forest inventory.

For forests located outside the country, the country has a low coverage of organic soils and that these are not located in the region covered by the FREL. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GF/8.27/REV.1 (A/64/32)

### ACCOUNTS FOR 20% PER YEAR OF THE TOTAL EMISSIONS (FOR LAND USE CHANGE AND FORESTRY (LULUCF)). HOWEVER, THE ATC CANNOT FIND DISAGGREGATED PERCENTAGES (OR ABSOLUTE VALUES) FOR EMISSIONS FROM FOREST LAND CONVERTED TO OTHER LAND-USE CATEGORIES (DEFORESTATION). THE AT CONSIDERS THAT THESE ARE IN FACT PART OF THE TOTAL ANNUAL EMISSIONS FROM ORGANIC MATTER RESULTING FROM DEFORESTATION AND CONCLUDED THAT THEY ARE PROBABLE.

IN RELATION TO THE LITTER POOL, ARGENTINA JUSTIFIED THE OMISSION OF THIS POOL ON THE BASIS OF THE INFORMATION PROVIDED BY THE INTERGOVERNMENTAL PANEL ON CLIMATE CHANGE (IPCC) DEFAULT VALUES, WHICH ASSIGN TO LITTER A CONTRIBUTION OF 1.82 AND 4.11% FOR FOREST LANDS WITH LOW AND MEDIUM BIOMASS, RESPECTIVELY, DEPENDING ON THE FOREST REGION. THE AT ALSO NOTES THAT THERE WAS NO INFORMATION PROVIDED BY ARGENTINA REGARDING SUBDIVISIONS BETWEEN THE ENCHASIS OF THE DEADWOOD POOL IN THE CONSTRUCTION OF THE FREL. THE AT CONSIDERS THAT, ACCORDING TO THE INFORMATION PROVIDED BY ARGENTINA, THIS POOL IS NOT INCLUDED. THE AT ALSO INCLUDES EMISSIONS FROM DEAD ORGANIC MATTER AND SOIL ORGANIC CARBON POOLS IN THE TOTAL ANNUAL EMISSIONS FROM DEFORESTATION. THE AT TAKES THIS APPROACH ON THE BASIS OF DATA COLLECTED DURING THE SECOND NATIONAL FOREST ASSESSMENT.

THE FREL INCLUDES ONLY EMISSIONS OF CO2. NON-CO2 EMISSIONS FROM FIRE ARE NOT INCLUDED BECAUSE THE FIRE DATA AVAILABLE IN THE COUNTRY PERIODIC REPORTING SYSTEM (PRS) DO NOT ALLOW FOR A CLEAR DISTINCTION RESULTING FROM DEFORESTATION ALONE. THE AT CONSIDERS THAT THE TREATMENT OF THESE EMISSIONS IS AN AREA WHERE ADDITIONAL WORK IS IMPROVEMENT SO AS TO MAINTAIN CONSISTENCY WITH THE GIG INVENTORY INCLUDED IN THE SECOND FREL, SAYING THAT THIS IS ONE OF THE CAUSES OF DEFORESTATION.

FOR THE CONSTRUCTION OF THE FREL, ARGENTINA USED THE 2006 IPCC GUIDANCE FOR NATIONAL GREENHOUSE INVENTORIES (2006 IPCC GUIDELINES) AS A BASIS FOR ESTIMATING THE ANNUAL CO2 EMISSIONS FROM DEFORESTATION.

Pursuant to the National Interpretation of REDD+ Safeguards,

Argentina’s regulatory and institutional framework promotes transparency and effectiveness in native forest governance when implementing FANCC(C). The Forest Law (2015) provides a strong legal basis for REDD+ projects, while various opportunities exist during the results period. In addition, citizens are provided with access to environmental information, which is also in line with Argentina’s commitments under international ratified instruments on corruption and has specific regulations regarding public participation.

The National Forest Authority (ANF) is responsible and makes it easier to address claims and complaints within the native forest governance framework. The FANCC(C) provides various citizen participation instruments have been made operational. Furthermore, there are mechanisms for public access by the public to information on actions and results of the implementation of the Forest Law or other actions concerning forest cover change.

In the period 2015-2016, Argentina undertook multi-stakeholder processes in order to ensure that the FANCC(C) is fully aligned with the Forest Law. The Forest and Community Project: definition of the strategy for the implementation of the FANCC(C), including design of FANCC(C). **(v)** Actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests and biodiversity, including that the actions referred to in UNFCCC decision 1/CP.16, paragraph 70, are not used for the conversion of natural forests into other land uses.

**(vi)** Actions to address the risks of reversals:

To address this safeguard, the procedure established in the ER Program Buffer Guidelines of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility was applied. This procedure allows countries to establish a mechanism to manage the risk of reversal (7.4P2/ER3002Q4) (40 per cent of the effect of deforestation and forest degradation is irreversible within 15 years in the period 2016–2030). The key risks identified are (i) natural (external factors such as climate change, pests, fires, diseases, land use changes) and (ii) internal demand and prices of commodities. Development of a national early warning system for deforestation is described in PAN/BC and have been addressed by other REDD+ plus initiatives in the country. One of the main risks of reversal that was identified is the lack of enforcement of the law on Natural Forests and its early warning system are key elements to anticipate and control this risk.

**(vii)** Actions to reduce displacement of emissions:

Argentina's legal framework supports to reduce the risks of displacement of emissions, as described in the ER Program Buffer Facility by having monitoring and early warning systems for deforestation, which is covered under the law on Natural Forests. The law of natural forests addresses this safeguard, since its scheme is applicable throughout the national territory, based on pre-


---
**Page 172**
---

established criteria that prevent the displacement of deforestation to other forests under one of the three categories of conservation of the law, regardless of jurisdiction. In addition, national policies and instruments for the management of native forests have mechanisms to detect, avoid, and manage the risk of displacement in the context of the implementation of the native forests law. The FAFM is also being used as a tool for land use planning (Land Zoning of Native Forests) and SNMM (Native Forest National Monitoring). As part of this process, several rounds of analysis studies and regional participatory workshops (in inter-provincial talents) were carried out to identify and work up such areas.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP142

Proposal name: Argentina REDD-plus RRP for results period 2014-2016

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country: Argentina

Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP), with the support of a land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) expert with experience in REDD-plus assessment and analysis, and the technical assistance provided by the Global Forest Carbon Center (GFCC) team of experts, assessed the proposal "Argentina REDD-plus RRP for results period 2014-2016" using the scorecard included in the terms of reference for the GCF pilot programme for REDD-plus.

### Summary of scorecard results

| Scorecard section | Results |
| --- | --- |
| Carbon elements | Score: 41 All criteria "pass" |
| Non-carbon elements | All criteria "pass" |
| Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits | Score: 2 |

Based on the scorecard results and following the terms of reference procedures, the independent TAP recommends that the Board consider the following:

(a) GCF volume of emission reductions: 18,731,707 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e)

(b) Additional 2.5 per cent for use of proceeds and non-carbon elements; and

(c) Proposed REDD-plus RRP USD 5/CO2e; USD 42,000,000

The following paragraphs describe the main findings on those sections of the scorecard where the Board should be guided in making its final decision. The scores for each criterion (see the annexes for further details).

II.1(c) is the Forest Reference Emission Level (FREL) accurate? (The data and methodology are either over- or under-estimating emissions and/or removals during the period, so far as can be judged). In paragraph 21 of the technical assessment report, the independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment team (AT) noted that the information collected during the first national forest inventory was not sufficient to determine whether the FREL is accurate. The emission factors (EFs) were small (particularly for Parque Chaqueño) where the most deforestation occurs. Argentina indicates in the report that for the second cycle of


---
**Page 179**
---

natural forest inventory, the number of samples will be increased, as well as size, as part of the stepwise approach. In addition, in paragraph 22 of the TAR, the AT noted that for other forest land class: the country should use ground data instead of biomass-based estimates. The AT also noted that the definition of other forest land adopted by the country might make it difficult to identify deforested areas through visual comparison of parts of the country with similar characteristics. This could have a negative impact on the enhancement of the data acquisition process for the other forest land class with new methods and techniques, which would be used in the future to improve the definition of this class. The score is 1 justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them.

### (ii) B.(i)(iii) Have all the REDD+ activities that are significant sources of emissions been included?

The submission from Argentina includes GHG emissions associated with deforestation, forest degradation, and dead organic matter. The AT noted that Argentina provided information related to processes that lead to forest degradation (e.g., wood extraction, fire, and logging). The AT also noted that the country has not excluded areas native forest cover) and acknowledged that, despite direct observations in the field suggesting otherwise, the country did not include emissions from these processes. The distribution of emissions from these processes prevented the inclusion of the activity "release of carbon dioxide from dead organic matter" as a significant source of emissions from forest degradation could also be significant. Argentina acknowledged in the modified FREL submission the relevance of this activity to the C02 emissions and indicated that it would include this activity in future submissions. The subsequent issues were not resolved due to the limitation of data, and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them.

### (ii) B.(i)(iv) Have all of the most significant pools been included?

Argentina includes above- and below-ground biomass in the construction of its FREL. Dead organic matter (i.e., dead wood and other dead organic material) is not included, as these are not included. The justification for these omissions is the lack of reliable data for estimating dead organic matter emissions. Argentina also noted that it was difficult to assess the insignificance of emissions from litter when using default GES provided by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC). Argentina has not excluded soils in the territory covered by the FREL. As a result of the interaction with the AT, Argentina elaborated on its approach for including dead organic matter and soil carbon in the FREL submission, indicating that the country has a low coverage of organic soils and that these are not located in the region covered by the FREL. Argentina emphasized that the 2016 IPCC GES does not provide default values for dead organic matter or soil carbon values that could be used as a basis for estimating changes in carbon stocks in dead organic matter and soil carbon. In response to the AT's request for additional information provided in the submission, Argentina intends to include emissions from dead organic matter and soil carbon in future submissions using an iterative approach based on the stepwise approach, on the basis of data collected during the ongoing second national forest inventory. The score is 1 justified because issues were not resolved due to the limitation of time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them.

Other dead land land that forms a natural ecosystem with a tree density of between 3 and 24 per cubic native soil, with trees that can reach a maximum height of 7 m or with a tree cover equal to or greater than 20 per cent, where the base area is less than 50 per cent. This includes areas dominated by shrubs or other vegetation species, within the basins have a minimum height of 0.3 m. It includes palm and red bed formations. Dead forest land is defined as the line (clear cut) of native forest cover from the conversion of forest land to other land use categories.

#### (a) Have all gases that are a significant source of emissions been included?

- Lack of robust data to allow the estimation of non-CO2 emissions from fires associated with “reduced impact logging” and other land uses, which could be a significant greenhouse gas.

#### Paragraph 9 of the TAR notes that forest fires are among the main drivers of deforestation. However, the significance of fire-to-deforestation has not been assessed.
- The score is justified because the issue was resolved due to the limited time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them; and

#### (b) Is the technical annex consistent with the reference period?
- The reference period for the technical annex corresponds exactly to the reference periods in the initial annual update report.
- The FREL submission that has been technically assessed and covers exactly the same four regions of the country as the initial annual update report. No new data or information was provided, and no previous FREL/FRL submission was made.

#### (c) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex consistent?
- Merely providing a reference to the results over time does not necessarily mean that they are consistent.
- In paragraph 15 of the technical report on the technical analysis of the technical annex to the initial annual update report, it is stated that the TAP and LULUCF experts indicated that Argentina used consistent methodologies and data to generate activity data and emissions estimates for the four forest regions. The TAP also noted that the approach, based on the same minimum mapping unit of 4–10 hectares (ha); used consistent definitions of forest cover, deforestation, and reforestation; and above-ground biomass pools, volumetric equations, biomass expansion factors, wood densities, and harvest fractions; applied the same methodology to all four forest regions for forest land areas and the same methodology to all other GHG sources.
- The TAP confirms that the results achieved are consistent with FREL. However, it was taken into consideration that the data provided by Argentina on the carbon stock in forests is limited, particularly, the data provided by Argentina on the digital coverage of deforestation for 2014 and 2015. The TAP and LULUCF experts noted that the activity data reports are not always consistent with the estimates reported for the total deforested areas, particularly for the forest regions of Chaco, Formosa, Misiones, and Corrientes and Espinal for 2014 and 2015. The TAP and LULUCF experts also noted (para. [19]) that the activity data reports are not consistent with the estimates reported for total deforested areas.
- The score is justified because issues were not resolved due to the limited time and the country has provided information on how it seeks to overcome them; and

#### (d) Is the technical annex consistent with the reference period?
- (Does the annex either over- or under-estimate emissions and/or removals)?

### The REDD+ Plus Framework

This funding proposal was submitted by Argentina under the GCF pilot programme for REDD+ plus BRRs. Argentina offered to generate 18,732 MCO-eq reduction emissions reductions (ERI) achieved by the country for the period 2014–2016 from the reduction of grass deforestation and degradation in forested areas. The total amount of CO2-eq emitted during this period is 165,172,705 tCO2-eq reported in the REDD-plus technical annex to the third BUR that was assessed technically under the UNFCCC process in 2020. The assessment of the accuracy and reliability of the REDD+ plus BRRs was conducted by the GCF Technical Review Panel (TRP).

These results are achieved by the country based on avoided deforestation against the FREL (Forest Reference Emission Level) for Argentina, which includes four forest regions: La Pampa, San Luis, Catamarca (Parameras), SelvaTucumano Boliviana (Yungas), and Espinal (only the Calchaí and Sanduyab districts).

Argentina’s FREL was calculated based on the historical average of gross emissions of the period 2002–2013. This period reflects the country's deforestation dynamic since the years prior to the implementation of the National Action Plan on Forests and Climate Change (NAPFCC). The four forest regions included in the FREL represent the majority of the Argentine territory covered by natural forests [50 million hectares (ha)]. These four regions also represent the area where the greatest deforestation occurred. They are also the areas that have been identified as key reservoirs for the management and conservation of native forest are focused. About 66 per cent of the indigenous population lives in these four regions.

An assessment of accuracy and reliability of Argentina’s FREL, which corresponds to annual average 101,142,849 tCO2-eq (for the period 2002–2013) is also provided in the exercise of the REDD+ plus BRRs. The assessment was conducted by the GCF Technical Review Panel (TRP) conducted under the UNFCCC technical assessment process in 2019. The TAR of the FREL, published on 5 December 2019, is available at <https://www.greenclimate.fund/en/technical-reports>. The TRP has confirmed that the FREL is complete, transparent and in accordance with the guidelines included in the annex to UNFCCC Decision No. 31/CP.17.

In addition to the 18,731,707 tonnes of CO2-eq of net payable volume (which is 11 per cent of the total during the results period 2014–2016), Argentina offered to set aside/deactivate 2,957,033 tonnes of CO2-eq as a buffer against the risk of reversals. This amount represents the reversals achieved in the same period, as a buffer against the risk of reversal. During the assessment process for the REDD+ plus BRRs, the percentage of CO2-eq that can be set aside to be net aside was increased to 40 per cent (7,492,683 tonnes of CO2-eq). The reversals risk analysis is based on the methodology and results provided in the Technical Assessment Report (TAR) of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility. The description of the methodology and the revised results are provided in annex V of the funding proposal.

The NAPFCC was launched as part of the National Programme, which started in April 2015 and finished in December 2019. The National Action Plan on Forests and Climate Change (known by its Spanish acronym NAPFCC) is a key component of Argentina’s national strategy for climate change and REDD+ strategy. Argentina submitted the PANPCC to the UNFCCC process in January 2019. During the entire process of consultation and participation to outline its national REDD-plus strategy, it was determined that only native forests would be considered within the REDD-plus framework.


---
**Page 182**
---

The preparation of the PANaBC included an analysis of the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation, as well as of the strategic guidelines for implementing the Forest Law, and measures foreseen in the nationally determined contribution (NDC). Details of the strategic structural pillars and operational pillars that form part of the PANaBC are provided in annex 3 to the funding proposal and are summarized as follows:

(a) Strategic structural pillars aim to overcome structural barriers for implementation through strengthening forest governance at all levels, including the development of management, control and monitoring capacities; recognition of the importance of native forests and their role in climate change mitigation.

(b) Strategic operational pillars consider actions that represent direct interventions on native forests and that have associated mitigation measures, such as land-use planning; sustainable use and conservation in productive landscapes; and restoration, recovery and forest fire prevention.

The PANaBC seeks to reduce net emissions by 27.5 MtCO2e from native forests by 2030, which is equivalent to 14% of the total NDC target for the country.

The planned project activities described in the funding proposal are aligned with the pillars and could significantly strengthen the PANaBC implementation process through addressing structural barriers (land tenure, forest management, development, fires) and the underlying causes of deforestation and forest degradation, by strengthening economic incentives, improving access to information and knowledge, and oversight capacities at the provincial and national level; and strengthening the overall forest governance.

The four components of the project are as follows: component A - support territorial forest management; component B – contribute to forest management with integrated livestock; component C – strengthen forest governance through capacity building and oversight capacities of national and provincial government institutions to address drivers of deforestation and forest degradation at all levels; and component D – improve access to information.

Most of the total benefits (95.7 per cent) derived from the RfPs will be used to implement the above-mentioned four components, while the rest (4.3 per cent) will be used for project management and monitoring and evaluation activities.

Output A.1 of component A - establish "sustainable forest basins" for timber and non-timber products. Beneficiaries of this activity are, among others, forest producers, micro and small-scale cattle farmers, and local communities.

Output A.2 of component A – local communities manage their collective territories through integral community plans (Dzanga area(s), PPGs), according to regulations in force at the national level, with support from provincial and national governments and local communities. Share: 21 per cent;

Output B.1 of component B - forest management with integrated livestock. Under this component livestock farmers will implement models of forest management with integrated livestock. Beneficiaries of this activity are small- and medium-scale cattle farmers. Share: 19 per cent.

All calculations were made on the basis of parameters set in assumptions at the time of the estimate and preparation of the funding proposal. As a result, any update of these assumptions should be made and updated according to the availability of new information. The amount of cost that could be achieved through other management options has not been considered as part of this cost estimate.

By the Resolution No. 360/2018, OFMRA, the forest basins are defined as those regions or geographical areas where they have a common objective and a marked location of actors working with the use, transformation and marketing of timber and non-wood products.

### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

The project is not intended to initiate a paradigm-shift process in Argentina’s forest management. Instead, it will complement and boost the country’s forest sector management from traditional, unsustainable management methods to sustainable management, boosted by the country’s own efforts and the implementation of the REDD+ plus process. However, the project does contribute to accelerating this shifting process targeting rapid upscaled implementation.

#### Annex 4 of the funding proposal illustrates the process of shifting from unsustainable management of native forests to a sustainable management process and how the REDD+ plus mechanism will support this process. The TAP considers that the project has high potential to directly contribute to the acceleration of REDD-plus activities by enabling the project to accelerate REDD-plus implementation and the shifting process. The REDD-plus mechanism was negotiated in 2013, and it is now being implemented in many countries worldwide with success stories worldwide to sustainable methods. Successful implementation of the REDD-plus process with the support of TCFP proceeds could initiate faster replication of this process for all native forests of Argentina.

#### In addition to the funding proposal, for implementation of processes priority will be given to the following: (1) strengthening forest-dependent communities and their capacity to participate in this process of strengthening the regional economy; special attention will be given to forest-dependent communities that are not currently involved in the project. The TAP considers that the project has high potential to accelerate the shifting of Argentina’s native forest traditional management to sustainable management.

#### Transformation is planned by the project in livestock and forest systems management practices, as well as in the legal framework through the implementation of the “National Forest Management with Integrated Livestock Plan”.

### 1.4 Sustainable development potential

The funding proposal all REDD plus activities has high potential to contribute to the sustainable development through sustainable forest management. As it is described in paradigm shift section, the project will contribute to the acceleration of the shifting process and lead to strengthen the local economy parameters, increase production efficiency, competitiveness of micro and small industries.

Such activities as adding value to goods and services from natural forests, offering support in legalization of access to natural resources to forest-dependent communities and improving their capacity for sustainable management of native forests will contribute to a more sustainable forest sector. Implementation of the “National Forest Management with Integrated Livestock Plan” is considered an important independent TCFP as most transformative action on the way of sustainable forest management.

### 1.5 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

38 Argentina has adopted a republican, representative and federal form of government, with a decentralized system of administration. There are 23 provinces and one autonomous city of Buenos Aires. Within this structure, the provinces are autonomous, and existed even before the nation was established as such, and the provinces maintain their power to manage natural resources. The provinces have the right to negotiate with the original owners of the natural resources within their territory, including the native forests. Therefore, the federal government has no direct control over the management of these natural resources - including native forests. Thus, the national regulatory framework for forest management is not as strong or comprehensive as in other countries.
39 The provinces have a constitution (state structure) that sets out the powers and responsibilities of each province. Each province has its own constitution, which conflicts with the need to manage the environment and natural resources - including native forests. Such constitution (state structure) of the State requires very high managerial capacities at the national level as well as provincial levels in general, but particularly to manage a sustainable way sectors such as agriculture, which conflict with the need for protection of the economy and – in this particular case- forests conflicting particularly with the agriculture sector.
40 Between 1950 and 2016 Argentina experienced 14 recessions (one or more years running at negative growth), with an average duration of 1.8 years. The country has thus been in recession for a third of the time since 1950.

Deforestation is a problem in Argentina, especially for local communities that inhabit or depend on forests. The rate of deforestation has been decreasing since 2014 but has not been matched by an improvement in poverty indicators. The regions, with the provinces of the Argentine north-west, account for the highest poverty rates in the country.

11. These activities cause soil erosion, desertification, pollution and invasion of exotic species. Forest degradation is a gradual process that alters the forest's ability to provide goods and services. However, Agencia de Bosques does not have or establish defined criteria for determining the extent of forest degradation. Therefore, based on the need to select criteria and quantify and locate degraded forest lands spatially in technical and scientific terms, it was decided to include them as part of the process of monitoring forest degradation process, and therefore forest degradation has not been considered in the FREL. The monitoring of forest degradation is a priority for Argentina and will be included as one of strengthening the national forest monitoring system, which will take place through the implementation of the REDD+ project.

17. Between 2002 and 2018, Argentina lost 5,319,199 ha native forests. The average deforestation surface area in that period amounted to 312,583 ha per year, decreasing from 394,271 ha/year between 2002-2006.

30. The increase of emissions from deforestation in 2017–2018 shows that the country's capacity to maintain the REDD+ plus results achieved in 2016 is still unsustainable and should be strengthened. This is one of the priorities of the project under consideration including proposal.

35. The NDC target committed by this Country is to reduce emissions by 27 MGOEeq/yr by 2030, which should be achieved through the implementation of FABNC+, which is the main target for the funding proposed and use of the GCF proceeds.

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Medium

The funding proposal describes the efficient distribution of GCF proceeds, and there is potential to reach high efficiencies if all other components of GEF plus system operate effectively. The proposed project will contribute to the territorial level in forest regions and provinces, and to strengthen the capacities of communities and institutions at this level. 20 per cent are allocated to strengthening the technical capacities of institutions at the provincial and national level to address drivers of deforestation, to monitor and control forest cover, to enhance the capacity for sustainable forest management, and to increase access to investments in forest basins, including significant increase of women engagement (component e).

It is important to highlight that the resources allocated to the local level will be assigned by open competition and through the provinces, to indigenous peoples, local communities and producers' associations. The selection criteria are based on the projects that were selected by the Provincial Committee and will be authorised by the National Committee so as to ensure transparency in the process of selecting projects for funding. This is mainly to reinforce institutional capacities that ensure compliance with existing regulations.

Allocation of funds to the local level will be prioritised in accordance with the areas that exert greater pressure on native forests and have more vulnerable local communities living therein.

Investment of proceeds is planned in the following components: territorial forest management, early warning system, fire prevention and control, and enhanced response to forest fires. The estimated cost per GtCO2 produced by the project is USD 17 which is far per cent higher compared with the GEF pilot programme payment of USD 5 per tEQA offered to GCF. In this regard, it should be noted that this GCF price is based on a market price and it is not calculated in accordance with the cost-benefit analysis for the implementation of forest projects.

According to the funding proposal, some initial considerations will have to be reduced/eliminated with future national recovery plans, and the costs of implementation should be increased. An additional USD 10 million was allocated on early warning systems (which to date have provided information about 22,734 ha deforested) and on the ground control of forest fires in the provinces of Chaco and Formosa. This is also due to the engagement of communities in forest protection through specific projects, as a means of potential reduction of deforestation and degradation of native forests, and other activity which could significantly contribute to the increased effectiveness of GCF proceeds implementation.

It is the opinion of the TAP that anticipated efficiency and effectiveness of the funding proposal is medium, due to the increasing trend of deforestation in 2017-2018 caused by forest fires.

**Page 188**

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval by the GCF Board with the following two recommendations:

(a) Adopt a monitoring and reporting system that makes it possible to distinguish between anthropogenic and natural drivers of fires, as part of the REDD-plus monitoring forests; and

(b) in order to avoid potential double counting and payments for the ERs achieved in the native forest sector, facilitate the introduction of obligatory regulations for reporting to the REDD-plus Host Country's registry.

[**] Have all REDD-plus activities that are significant sources of emissions been included?

The term "degradation" proposed by Argentina includes CO2 emissions associated with “green deforestation”. Green deforestation is defined as the loss (clear-cut) of native forest cover from the conversion of forest land into other uses.

As reported in paragraph 14 of the TAR, Argentina provided information related to processes that lead to forest degradation (e.g. wood harvesting and logging, fire, soil erosion, introduction/establishment of exotic species into the native forest area) and acknowledged that, despite the fact that deforestation is not occurring, significant forest degradation is occurring; the quantification and spatial distribution of emissions from this activity are included in the calculation of the activity “reducing emissions from forest degradation”.

The AT noted that in addition to "reducing emissions from deforestation" other activities could also be significant, in particular

In terms of climate change, the National Executive Power through Decree 891/2016, created the National Forest Management System (NFMS) as an instrument for governance of forests and climate change.

Argentina's national forest management system (NFMS) provides updated information on the state of forests services in the country since 1998, representing a key institutional tool for national decision-making and international commitments related to sustainable use of forests. The NFMS was already operational during the results period (2017-2019), allowing us to continuously strengthen technical and institutional capacities.

The funding proposal reports (p.27) that more than 10 years of implementation of the law on forest conservation and sustainable deforestation has reduced from more than 485,600 ha in 2007 to less than 154,390 ha in 2019 (coinciding with the promulgation of this law at the end of 2007) continuing up until 2014 and then it stabilizes at around 8.5 percent per year. This is a significant reduction in deforestation as a regulatory instrument. # Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples

## Report, all these different modalities of "communities" shall be jointly and indefinitely called local communities.

In the results period, REDD plus readiness actions and those leading to emission reductions were carried out in a context of participation and respect for the rights of indigenous peoples, forest-dwelling and forest-dependent communities, according to national legislation and international law, including the international regulatory framework. However, there is no specific law on participatory and free, prior and informed consultation of indigenous people and their representatives, as provided by Article 7 of the Indigenous Participation Council.

The National Constitution of the Argentine Republic recognizes the rights of the country's native peoples and indigenous peoples. In such, the country is a Party to Convention 169 of the International Labour Organization (ILO) and Law No. 25034 of the Argentine Republic. Countries (Argentina Law No. 26873), which establishes that "indigenous peoples have the right to participate in decision-making on matters that affect their rights" and that governments must consult with them before adopting legislative, administrative or administrative measures that affect them. Likewise, the law on native forests establishes that "any project for the clearing of sustainable management of native forests must respect the rights of native peoples.

The actions and measures of the PIAN/CC were designed with the participation of indigenous peoples and communities (all communities are listed in the funding proposal) who depend on the forest and its resources. In addition, during 2017 and 2018, a series of workshops was held in the different regions where the participation of indigenous peoples was promoted. In addition, a specific working group with technical support from the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development worked on the design of the PIAN/CC. In this design process, the contributions made by representatives of the Council of Indigenous

People were important: The PANGLC outlined its internal social and environmental and social safeguards on strengthening local communities, which tends to "improve the situation of local communities" and "promote the recognition of land tenure based on the recognition of territories of the native peoples and local communities."

The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities, in the actions referred to in paragraphs 70 and 72 of decision J.1C/16.

Argentina's interpretation of the Cancun Safeguards is as follows: "The implementation of the PANGLC and other REED+ initiatives shall promote the full and effective participation of all relevant stakeholders, in particular indigenous peoples and local communities." The Argentine Republic has a regulatory framework that promotes the participation of indigenous people directly or indirectly, in the management of native forests. In this framework, the following instruments are recognized: the ILO Convention 169 which recognizes the ethnic and cultural pre-existence of the native peoples; the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples; the Independent Countries of the International Labour Organisation, which recognize that indigenous people have a right to participate in decision-making on matters that affect their rights", and that governments should consult them through appropriate procedures.

To guarantee the participation of multi actors in the design of the PANGLC four extended round table meetings were held with more than 500 participants. The first meeting focused on the full and effective participation of representatives of the government, civil society and indigenous peoples. A National Working Group was created for this purpose and a Native Peoples Working Group were created; and four sectoral round table meetings on agriculture and forests.

That actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity, ensuring that the national forest law (Law No. 25.674) is not used for the conversion of natural forests, but are instead used to incentivize the protection and conservation of native forests, enhancing ecosystem services, and to enhance other social and environmental benefits.

The Argentine National Constitution establishes the duty of the authorities to protect the environment and biological diversity., and Law No. 24.375, ratified by the country, aims at the conservation of natural forests. According to this law, the law on native forests (Law No. 26.311) preserves conserving natural forest areas in Argentina. The law provides for three levels of conservation value: high, medium, and low. Clearing of forest land is only allowed in the lowest class, with prior authorization and approval from the National Forest Management Assessment.

- The national law enforcement authority for native forests (SafDyS) and has tools such as the National Monitoring System for Native Forests (SINAF).

## (vi) Actions to address the risks of reversals

The native forests law has among its objectives the conservation, regulation and control of the reduction in the use of native forests throughout the national territory. This is done with a view to avoid the risks of reverses of emissions. For this, Argentina allocates 30% of the funds from the Green Climate Fund for the planning and maintaining the monitoring network and the information system of deforestation and forest degradation in native forests. These are fires which are defined as those fires that, whether produced by natural or human causes, affect the use of native forests, the landscape and that affect the soil and the biodiversity of the area.

Law No. 26183 establishes the mechanisms for the prevention, repression and combat of forest and rural fire(-L-1 in native forests).

As a result of the native forests law, between 2010 and 2017 (5/10/2017), 40% of these funds were assigned to the provinces to develop and maintain the monitoring network and information system of deforestation and forest degradation. This is done by implementing the mechanisms to avoid and reduce the risk of reversal.


## (vii) Actions to reduce displacement of emissions

Argentina's legal framework helps reduce the risks of displacement of emissions in native forests through the planning, implementation and early warning systems for deforestation, which make it possible to identify areas at high risk. The law on the use of native forests addresses this subgoal, since its scheme is applicable throughout the national territory, based on pre-established criteria.

The funding proposal also details how the disbursement of funds will be conducted: (1) 72% allocated to investments at the territorial level in forest regions and provinces; and to strengthen the capacities of communities and various projects for sustainable development. These funds will be targeted towards interventions to decrease deforestation and forest degradation – and related carbon emissions – as a territorial level in forest regions and provinces. The remaining 28% is allocated to strengthening technical capacities of institutions at the provincial and national levels, including capacity building on monitoring forests and control forest cover; to enhance and operationalize the safeguards system and other institutional arrangements for sustainable investments in forest basins, including boosting women's engagement.

Allocation of funds to the local level will be prioritized in accordance with the needs of communities living in productive forests and have more vulnerable local communities living therein.

The GCF proceeds are expected to be used in the following four components: (1) restoration of degraded lands and landscapes; (2) work with integrated livestock; enhanced response to forest fires; and strengthening technical capacities at provincial, national and sub-national government institutions to address the drivers of deforestation, to monitor forest and control deforestation and forest degradation.

## Impact potential

The AE acknowledges the potential risks identified by the ITAP. The AE believes that the proposed activities under the GCF RBP will continue reinforcing already strong institutional arrangements, which are expected to reduce levels of vulnerability and likelihood of deforestation and forest degradation, thus continuing to minimize the above mentioned risks.

# Introduction

This Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAAAP) has been prepared by Argentina, within the framework of the Funding Proposal for REDD+ Results-Based Payments to be submitted to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The GAAAP indicates that the proposal is aligned with the gender policies of GCF. As in previous years, this document provides a gender analysis and an evaluation of the impacts of the proposal with the objectives of the GCF gender policy.

The purpose of document is to analyze gender gaps in Argentina related to forests and climate change. The main areas of intervention identified as included actions are: the REDD+ RBP proposal to so enable compliance with national and international commitments on gender equality and women's empowerment, including the 2015 Paris Agreement (COP21) and the National Climate Plan on Forests and Climate Change (PANFCC), the Argentina’s National REDD+ Strategy); among other things, the plan proposes benefits from gender-related progress achieved in the REDD+ readiness phase (2013-2014). The document also addresses the need to ensure that women are included in the institution adoption and adaptation of the 2016 Agenda's indicators and targets, as well as in the generation of knowledge on gender equality and women’s empowerment in forests. This is reflected in gender-related legislation and regulations, and in the design of initiatives and programmes with a view to promoting gender equality.

The gender perspective is addressed in this document with respect to the three autonomies of women: economic, political and physical. Economic autonomy is understood as the ability of women to provide for themselves and their families through work or other means that do not depend on men; it includes the best way to do so. Salaries are not the only source of autonomy, rather this also includes access to training, education, common goods, loans, the solidarity economy and public services (FPS, 2015). Political autonomy is understood as women's ability to participate in decision-making and their full participation on equal grounds. Finally, physical autonomy refers to a woman’s right to make decisions about her body, including the right of women over 18 years old to decide on her body, sexual and reproductive health.

Regarding gender mainstreaming in forest management, the actions included in this proposal are: the promotion of women's participation in decision-making processes that have greater pressure on natural forests; and where a high proportion of the rural population lives. This population depends mainly on native forests for their livelihoods (FAO, 2015). In the case of the Chaco forest region, for example, where the advance of the agricultural frontier and uncontrolled forest exploitation have caused the loss of native forests, as well as the degradation of the soil and water resources. Women are also affected by the lack of access to credit and financial services from the sale of forest products, whilst livelihood in many cases also depend on food from forests.

The activities to be carried out in the Project, which are described in more detail in the funding proposal (FP), will contribute to the achievement of gender equality. The elimination of discrimination on forests, create opportunities for equitable access to new economic resources through the participation of women and men in decision-making processes related to forest management; the basis of gender and promote the improvement of the quality of life of communities living in forests.

### 2.4 Education

According to the latest data published by UNESCO (2019), Argentina has a literacy rate of 99%. Women's literacy rate (99.0%) is higher than that for men (98.4%).

Figure 1 data shows that in most educational levels there are no significant differences between men and women. However, at the university level, only 27% of women complete secondary school studies, where men stand 4 percentage points above women, while at university level, 22.3% of women complete their studies compared to 17.4% of men.

| Figure 1. Educational attainment of over-24-year-old men and women. |
| --- |
| Source: Observatorio de la Violencia contra las Mujeres (Observatory on Violence against Women). INADI, Based on the PMS 2018. |

However, these data do not always translate into equal proportions in terms of access to paid work within each educational level. As can be seen in figure 2, 45.6% of the total number of employed women have a university degree compared with only 37.9% of men (17.8 percentage points). In contrast, for the group of secondary school graduates (47.8%). This shows that women have more education credentials than men in paid work and, therefore, women without a university degree have more difficulties than men in accessing paid work.

#### 2.3 Health

In 2014, the average life expectancy at birth in Argentina was 76 years - 80 years for women and 72 years for men. In the period 1960-2014, the country's life expectancy increased by 11 years, 10 years for men and 12 years for women. It should be noted that the difference in the expectancy between men and women was 8 years at birth in 1960 (16 years) and decreased to 7.6 years in 2014. The difference between men and women decreased in 2015 to 6.6 years (Figure 3).

Since 1992, there has been an increase in the number of women in the Lower and Upper Houses of the National Congress, although it has never exceeded 60% (Figure 4).

### Figure 5. Share of Women as one national Congress (%), 1990–2018.

Source: Political Electoral Observatory, Ministry of the Interior, 2019.

In the lower houses or unicameral legislatures of all the provinces, the average share of women is one third (33%), above the 30% floor established by the provincial electoral systems for the lists of candidates (Figure 7). The average female participation in the provincial senates is 24%, with very high deviation rates. At the lowest end of female participation are the Senate of Catamarca (with no women) and the Senate of Chaco (with only one woman). In contrast, the Senate of Buenos Aires is made up of 55.6% of women and it is the only provincial legislature with a majority of women.

Regarding the percentage of women in charge of hierarchy political positions in the National State’s organizational chart, a decline is visible between 2015 and 2018 (Figure 8). ### 2.6 Poverty

In Argentina, 25.9% of households are below the poverty line. Poor households can be divided into two subgroups: destitute households (17%) and non-destitute households (82%). If this information is analyzed in terms of individuals, it means 33.5% of the total population lives below the poverty line (Figure 9).

### Access to land

The gap between men and women in land ownership in Latin America is historically very large, with women holding only for historically small percentages of land owners. Gender inequality in land ownership relates to male preference in inheritance, the privileges enjoyed by men in marriage, male bias in land distribution and in the land market.

## 3. Legal framework for the protection of women and the promotion of gender equality in Argentina

Argentina has ratified several international instruments against all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1979, which was subsequently enacted into the National Constitution in the 1994 constitutional amendment. In addition, the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence Against Women (Istanbul Convention) was ratified by Argentina in 1996 by Law No. 24623. These laws ensure that the Argentine Government and the provincial states are committed to protecting women from all forms of discrimination.

It is important to highlight that the improvements achieved in the legislation protecting women, which will be briefly described as follows: "are the result of the fight waged by and the awareness levels of women themselves, as well as the increasing role institutional and social political structures" (Ministry of Justice and Human Rights, 2012).

### 1.1 International protocols and frameworks on gender and human rights ratified by Argentina

* Universal Declaration of Human Rights
* American Declaration of the Rights and Duties of Man
* International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights
* International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
* Optional Protocol to the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
* International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination

# Laws that promote women’s physical, economic and political autonomy

## Laws in this regard have been enacted and may be classified as follows:

- **Laws on economic autonomy:** such as laws on pensions and care, intended to ensure the day-to-day physical and emotional well-being of people who have some degree of dependency or are at risk of poverty because they do not work.
- **Laws on political autonomy:** such as laws on gender quotas and gender parity, which seek to promote equal participation of women in the various Government Branches on an equal footing with men.
- **Laws on physical autonomy:** such as Law No. 26485, on Full Protection for Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women; Law No. 27301 on Gender Equality; or the recently enacted Micaela Law (Law No. 27999) on Mandatory Training in Gender Issues for all public servants.
- **Laws related to Economic Autonomy:** Laws promoting women’s economic autonomy include those that provide for the social organization of care and income distribution. The main laws relating to social organization of care include those that grant leave for care duties: i. Law No. 24180 on Leave for Care Duties (Law No. 22431) which was passed in 1981 and created a comprehensive protection system for people with disabilities; ii. Law No. 25673 on Universal Access to Social Security Benefits; iii. Law on Universal Accessibility for People with Disabilities (Law No. 24314 of 1996); iv. Creation of the Comprehensive Protection System for People with Disabilities (Law No. 1565 of 2002). Other laws that are related to women’s labour rights vis-à-vis their care duties, which also affect their economic autonomy include: i. Law No. 24389 on Parental Leave; ii. Law No. 27301 (which consists of a non-contributory payment per child made to only one of the parents/guardians); and iii. Law on Special Leave for Mothers who are Employed as a Result of the Birth of a Child with Down Syndrome or other Severe Disabilities.

Additionally, the legal framework envisages protection for pregnant adolescents through two laws: i. Legal Framework that Protects from Class for Pregnant Students (Law No. 25373 of 2000); and ii. Law on Exploring Public Education Opportunities for Pregnant Adolescents or Continuity of the Academic Year in the case of Pregnant Students or Mothers during the Lactation Period (Law No. 26189). Other laws that are related to care responsibilities is the Law on Shared Parental Rights and Duties (Law No. 23248 of 1985).


---
**Page 229**
---

The laws that address (income distribution) include the following:

Laws that regulate the labour market, such as Law No. 20743/1976 on the Employment Contract Regime, which governs the individual relationships of men and women workers in the private sector, and provides for equal treatment regardless of sex, race, nationality, religion, political ideas, union affiliation or age* (article 17) and promotes equality between men and women with no distinction as to sex, religion or race (article 81). This is supplemented by the Special Employment Regime for Women (Law No. 20954/1983).

Furthermore, retirement and pension legislation has brought about significant breakthroughs in terms of the economic autonomy for women. The main laws include: Law No. 25270 on the right to have access to social security benefits regardless of sex or marital status (article 6), which was followed by the incorporation of Housewives into the Integrated Pension and Retirement System; Law No. 30948/1999, which provides for a pension for women who have worked in domestic service for at least 30 years of work at work with social security contributions and are currently unemployed; Law No. 26754, which grants the right to retirement to women who have been employed in public and private sectors as the State the regulation of pension and retirement systems (2008); Law No. 26793/1999, which grants a moratorium to independent workers that have not reached retirement age (2014); and the National Historical Reparation Program for Retirees and Pensioners (Law No. 27508 of 2016).

Laws related to Political Autonomy

The first law specifically addressing political rights in Argentina was Law No. 19205, known as the "Women's Suffrage Law", enacted in 1947. It is the oldest of all the laws in force that currently enhance women’s rights. It is an iconic law granting women both the right to vote and the right to fully participate in political life. The law was followed by the Law on Gender Equality and Women's Participation in decision-making, such as the Law on Gender Quotas (Law No. 20412/1991), which prescribed that at least one-third of candidates for public office must be women; the Law No. 25378, the Argentine Constitutional amendment (1996), which included true equality of opportunity between men and women in political life; the Law on Political Party Funding (Law No. 2402/1996) through affirmative action for the regulation of political parties and in the electoral system (article 37); Law No. 25474, enacted in 2002, which established mandatory quotas for women in trade unions (30%); In recent years, there have been several laws that have improved women's representation: Law No. 26142 on Gender Party in Political Representation, enacted in 2017. This legal provision entails an improvement in current female participation standards by amending the National Electoral Code to include a quota for women candidates and their inclusion as co-presidents of political parties and representatives and for Mercosur parliamentarians, that every other candidate on the list must be a woman (article 3); Law No. 27508/2016, which establishes the list alternative candidates. Lists that do not meet this requirement will not be officially recognized.

Lastly, the Law on Political Party Funding was enacted in 2018 (Law No. 27504), which requires that 30% of the funds allocated to political parties must be used for the training, promotion and development of women’s political leadership skills within the political party (article 12).

Laws related to Physical Autonomy

Argentina has a vast corpus of legislation that ensures prevention and punishment of violence against women, with a view to advancing their physical autonomy. Below, the most relevant laws are listed:

* **1980 - Law No. 23751** - Approval of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW).

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

## Legislative Framework for RBD+ in Argentina, period 2016-2018

### Key Laws and Amendments

* **1987 - Law No. 23513 – Divorce Law**
* **1994 - Law No. 24417 – Protection against domestic violence. Possibility to request precautionary measures.**
* **1996 - Law No. 24050 – Approval of the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Belen do Pará Convention).**
* **1999 - Law No. 25878 – Crimes against sexual integrity. Amendment of the Argentine Criminal Code. Defines sexual violence as a public action and includes rape in the category of crimes of rape. It also recognizes different types of sexual assault and spells out aggravating circumstances.**
* **2001 - Law No. 25544 – It mandates that any pregnant woman must be tested for HIV, following the WHO guidelines.**
* **2002 - Law No. 25673 – It creates the National Programme for Sexual Health and Responsible Family Planning.**
* **2003 - Law No. 25918 – Anti-discrimination Law: "Any actions or omissions based on reasons such as ethnic group, religion, nationality, ideology, political opinion or trade union affiliation, sex, gender identity, sexual orientation, age, disability or health status are prohibited."**
* **2004 - Law No. 25928 – Unborn children.**
* **2005 - Law No. 26137 – Protection of Girls and Adolescents.**
* **2006 - Law No. 26130 – Regulation of Surgical Contraception.**
* **2006 - Law No. 26155 – National Programme for Comprehensive Sexuality Education.**
* **2007 - Law No. 26489 – Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Gender-Based Violence against Women in their Interpersonal Relationships.**
* **2009 - Law No. 26468 – Full Protection for Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women in their Interpersonal Relationships.**
* **2009 - Law No. 26731 – Gender violence.**
* **2012 - Law No. 26743 – Gender identity.**
* **2015 - Law No. 27210 – It creates the Corps of Women and Men Attorneys for Gender-Based Violence against Women in their Interpersonal Relationships.**
* **2016 - Law No. 27324 – Equality in Education: Prevention and Eradication of Gender-Based Violence against Girls and Adolescents.**
* **2017 - Law No. 27598 – It amends the Argentine Criminal Code for purposes of clearly defining the actions that constitute the crime of sexual abuse.**
* **2017 - Law No. 27634 – Special Jurisdiction for Women Convicted of Femicide.**
* **2018 - Law No. 27452 – Drives Law on Domestic Reparation System for Boys, Girls and Adolescents. Victims of femicide receive compensation from the Government amounting to a maximum payment of $30,000.**
* **2018 - Law No. 27453 – Child Abuse: Amendment of article 72 of the Argentine Criminal Code. It defines child sexual abuse as a public action offence.**
* **2019 - Law No. 27635 – National Programme for Prevention and Assistance to Gender for Officials working in the Three Branches of the State at all levels.**
* **2019 - Law No. 27648 – It creates a hotline (1445), street harassment as a form of violence against women. It creates a hot line to provide assistance, free of charge, to gender-based violence victims.**
* **2019 - Law No. 27636 – Law on Sting: It establishes a Single Standardized System for Clothing Sizes (SUNTH), in its Spanish acronym) for standardized body measurements.**
* **2019 - Law No. 27645 – It creates the National Programme for Prevention and Assistance to Gender-Based Violence against Women. The amendment proposed to Law No. 28485 was approved, and the law now includes political violence as one form of gender-based violence.**
