### 1.3. Gender, rurality, and environmental issues in Argentina’s legislation

There is a regulatory framework regarding environmental issues that has mainstreamed the gender perspective with various degrees of explicitness, as follows:

Starting at the top of the Argentine legal pyramid, the 1994 constitutional amendment added article 45 to the Constitution. This article guarantees women equal rights and access to a healthy and balanced environment, fit for human development and for productive results that meet current needs without compromising those of future generations. It also states that in dealing with environmental damage and pollution, on a priority basis, there is no legal obligation, as provided by law.

The applicable authorities shall endeavor to ensure the protection of this right, a national policy that should be based on the principles of respect for human rights, cultural diversity, and biological diversity, and shall promote environmental information and education. The Federal Government may establish minimum standards in terms of environmental management and quality standards and the provinces must adopt any necessary provisions to supplement them, without any attention to local jurisdictions.

The additional articles include at least two innovative elements: the constitutional recognition of collective rights or interests, on the one hand; and the power vested with the Federal Government to issue laws on environmental protection, on the other. This is a significant step forward in the national environmental policy within the context of a federal country like Argentina.

Article 75, paragraph 12 of the Argentine Constitution, recognizes “the ethnic and cultural pre-existence” of indigenous peoples and their right to the enjoyment of their natural resources and land possession and ownership of the land they have traditionally occupied. Additionally, it acknowledges their “right to participate in the management of their natural resources and any other matters that concern them.” The article also states that the State shall adopt measures to ensure equal opportunity and equal treatment.

Legislative and promote affirmative action measures that ensure equal opportunities for women and men in all areas of public life. This is a principle recognized in this Constitution and in the applicable international human rights treaties, particularly regarding children, women, and ethnic and people with indigenous origins.

Along the same lines, the 1986 ILO Convention No. 169 (on Indigenous and Tribal Peoples in Independent Countries) was approved by Law No. 24071. This Convention, which has constitutional status, recognizes the rights of indigenous peoples to their lands, territories, and resources.

In two different sections, it mentions equal opportunity and treatment for men and women. On the one hand, article 3 states: “1. Indigenous and tribal peoples shall fully enjoy human rights and fundamental freedoms without discrimination on account of sex.” Article 4 adds that “discrimination shall be applied without discrimination to male and female members of these peoples.” On the other hand, article 20 states: “Indigenous and tribal peoples shall have equal opportunities and equal treatment in employment for men and women, and protection from sexual harassment.”

The 2015 amendment to the Argentine Civil and Commercial Code included the rights of indigenous peoples in article 38, as it provides that “Recognized indigenous communities have the right to communicate with their representatives on matters concerning how territories occupied and any other territories that are suitable and sufficient for human development shall be used, low subject to the provisions of article 75, paragraph 12 of the Argentine Constitution.”

Finally, there are laws and regulations that promote these policies, promoting their participation in decision-making and benefit-sharing, namely:

Law No. 25673: General Law on the Environment (2002)

It establishes minimum standards to achieve sustainable and appropriate environmental management and protection for the conservation of natural resources and implementation of sustainable development. Article 2 spells out the purposes of the law, including inter alia:


---
**Page 232**
---

### Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Document linked to the National Program for the Promotion of Women for RIEIN in Argentina, period: 2016-2018

#### c) Promoting social participation in decision-making processes.

t) Introducing changes in social values and behaviours that may enable sustainable development. Additionally, articles 16, 17 and 18 ensure access to public environmental information, while articles 19, 20 and 21 provide social participation guidelines that govern the entirety of environmental policies.

**Law No. 26331**, on Minimum Environmental Protection Standards for the Enrichment, Restoration, Conservation, Sustainable Use and Management of Native Forests (2007)

Although the Forest Law makes no explicit provision for gender issues, it does prescribe the obligation to consider gender in all its provisions. Article 4 sets out a procedure that complies with the provisions regarding access to public environmental information and citizen participation as foreseen in the General Law on the Environment.

**Law No. 27318**, on Historical Reparation of Family Agriculture for the Construction of a New Rurality in Argentina (2015)

This law defines family, peasant and indigenous agriculture to be a matter of public interest due to its contribution to the people's food security and sovereignty, and as a result of its practicing and promoting levels of livelihood and production systems that preserve biodiversity and sustainable use of information processes. Article 4 sets forth the goal of the law, which include gender considerations:

- Contribute to eliminate divides and gender stereotypes
- Ensure equal access for men and women in land ownership and management
- Promote the participation of women in decision-making bodies and implement specific public policies that favour women devoted to family agriculture

**Law No. 26180**, Emergency Law on Possession and Ownership of Lands and Fund for Assistance to Indigenous Communities (2005)

National Law No. 26180 was passed at the end of 2005 for a four-year term, to address the situation of territories occupied by indigenous communities in the country, consistent with Article 75, paragraph 17, of the National Constitution, and to partly fulfill Article 14, para. a., of ILO Convention 169.

This law was extended three times: In 2009, Law No. 26554 was passed in 2013; Law No. 28494, and in 2017 Law No. 27400 were enacted, extending validity through to November 2021. This is an emergency and public order law.

Its objectives are as follows:

- Declare the territorial emergency of indigenous peoples' communities in the country.
- Stay judgment enforcement, court or administrative proceedings aimed at eviction or occupation of lands by indigenous communities.
- Order a technical, legal and cadastral survey of the Indigenous Communities and, if necessary, of the currently, traditionally and publicly occupied lands.

### Gender and Public Policies

#### 4.1 Main public policies with a gender approach

Within the framework of current regulations, Argentina has developed social public policies with a gender perspective that have had a direct impact on the improvement of women's educational attainments.


---
**Page 233**
---

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

## The National Action Plan for Gender Equality (NAPGE) in Argentina: period 2016-2018

The increase in their economic income, the development of new economies and consolidation of stronger social and community relations, providing more employment opportunities and greater participation in decision-making. The following are among the most important regulations:

* **Universal Child Allowance (UCA):** Executive Resolution 130/2003 consisting of a monthly, non-contributory allowance for each child under age of 8 (or with no age limit in the case of a disabled child), up to a maximum of five children per household. The greatest impact is on girls and adolescents: 54% of UCA beneficiaries are girls aged between 0-17 years old, respectively, of the total population of Boys, Girls and Adolescents in the region (ANSES, 2013). Likewise, 97% of the beneficiaries of the Allowance are Women. In this regard, the National Institute of Statistics and Census (INDEC) estimates that UCA is received by about 4 million Argentine households. This is a social protection measure for the most vulnerable sectors.

* **Housing Renovation:** A programme to renovate homes of around 100 thousand people who had not made contributions to the Argentine Social Security System, and who were in need of renovation, mainly targeted to housewives who were able to access a minimum pension.

* **The Progresar Programme (Resolution No. 239/1998):** A programme that aims at improving the quality of life of the most vulnerable families and neighborhoods, and at promoting the development of the personal and social capacities of recipients. It is implemented in 40 municipalities with a population over 50 thousand inhabitants. Social occupational opportunities; it is targeted to women with children, heads of household, who are in the productive sector, unemployed or underemployed, and who have completed or are completing formal studies, training in trades, access to health through health schemes, as well as access to cultural and recreational activities for over one hundred thousand female heads of households.

* **Pro Huerta Programme (Resolution No. 239/1998):** It is implemented by the Argentine Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Food (Ministerio de Agricultura, Ganadería y Alimentación, MAF), aimed at the self-production of agroecological food, to improve food sovereignty, promote the participation and organization of vulnerable sectors of the population, and encourage the modernization of rural production. The programme is implemented in 10 municipalities; currently includes more than 8,000 orchards and 130,000 small farms that provide a food and nutritional security for over one million people.

* **The National Women's Council signed an agreement to promote the programme among women experiencing gender-based violence, with a view to promoting the physical and economic autonomy of these women.**

The creation of the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity (in December 2009) was a milestone in the development of public policies from a gender perspective. This substantive area was set up on acknowledging that women's rights are human rights; that since the decade of the nineties, pluri-national meetings of women and LGTBQ+ persons have been organized to build knowledge collectively, to demand respect for their rights and to fight against discrimination, violence and all inequalities and all forms of male chauvinist violence. Since 2015, the #NosotrasMovenemos movement has further strengthened this demand by a pluralistic, diverse, dissident, heterogeneous and empowered feminist movement.

On 3 July 2020, the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity launched the National Plan of Action against gender-based violence (NAPGBV), which is framed in the framework of the NAPGE and covers three thousand people from across the country, with the support and commitment of other

Ministries and State agencies, including the Ministry for the Environment and Sustainable Development.

The following are the strategic guidelines of the National Plan of Action:

1. PREVENTION of gender-based violence, coordinating all National State programmes, measures and actions.
2. ASSISTANCE to persons experiencing gender-based violence, coordinating all National State programmes, measures and actions.
3. PROTECTION of persons experiencing gender-based violence, coordinating all National State programmes, measures and actions.

The development of these guidelines includes several measures that can be seen in the full document, including institutional strengthening at an institutional and territorial strengthening to address gender-based violence.

The Ministry for the Environment and Sustainable Development undertook the commitment to provide support with regard to:

- Development of contents to disseminate the possibilities of comprehensively addressing gender-based violence from an inter-sectorial perspective for different environments such as the indigenous peoples, rural populations, women in urban areas and exploitation.
- Training in trades and vocational training to fund and support social and productive projects, either in the form of co-operative or independent start-ups, targeted to the campesinos and indigenous peoples.
- Development of tools to harmonize the different languages and cultural practices so as to facilitate access for indigenous people, migrant and rural populations experiencing gender-based violence.

### 4.2 Gender and the Sustainable Development Agenda

In line with the principles that explain how the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development considers a world in which there is "universal respect for human rights and dignity" and in which "all women and girls enjoy full gender equality, and where all legal, social and economic obstacles impeding their empowerment are removed", Argentina has included gender equality as an essential goal of the 2030 Agenda (United Nations, 2019a). The commitment to gender equality, as well as the inclusion of this goal in the 2030 Agenda, was made at the time of signing the 2030 Agenda, since this has been stated in the declaration and in the SDGs (targets and indicators).

Regarding SDGs, the Agenda includes a specific goal to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls. This is Goal No. 5 ("Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls") explicitly support this commitment. Thus, it can be noted that both the cross-cutting gender approach and the inclusion of gender equality as a part of the mandate and must guide the implementation of sustainable development policies.

By adopting this 2030 Agenda, Argentina also undertook the commitment to mobilize the necessary resources to implement this gender approach, particularly taking into account the needs of people who are at risk of poverty or greater vulnerability.

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

## 4.3 The gender approach in the National REDD+ Strategy

Within the framework of the National Action Plan on Forests and Climate Change (PANFCC), an implementation strategy was developed, with actions aimed at ensuring gender equality in forest management programs and policies, taking into consideration the following:

* All political actors involved in decision-making processes in the area; and these effects are different for men and women, according to what a given society considers masculine and feminine in each context (i.e. gender roles);
* There is a specific group of the population that cares for and depends on forests: among them, women have specific knowledge and relationships that must be taken into account;
* Women, as well as other agents across the territory (youth, ethnic minorities, landless farmers) are usually relegated in decision-making processes regarding the territory, and this trend must be reversed.

In this regard, various activities have been carried out involving men, women, indigenous peoples and other actors. The results of these activities show that during the period 2017-2018, women accounted for 33.3% of the participants and men for 66.6% (Table 6), therefore, the minimum goal was reached of ensuring a 50% share of women.

The number of women participating in the meetings and affirmative measures taken to increase their level of representation are important for gender equality in the participatory process. However, it is necessary to continue working on this issue through specific actions in inclusive terms.

The following aspects were identified during the aforementioned activities:

* Women from community spaces (grassroots organizations and indigenous peoples) highlighted that they have a different relationship with forests compared to men: they consider them as values in themselves and not by virtue of their commercial value. They repeatedly mentioned the need to ensure water as one of their rights, and pointed to the institutional barriers related to compliance with ongoing programmes and the laws in force.

Empowered women leaders and women overall have had to face numerous obstacles to gain access to forest governance spaces, including their exclusion by local leaders, traditional leaders and authorities.

Gender-sensitive approaches are needed to address these gender-based barriers and obstacles to acquire property. In addition, studies were carried out to evaluate the gender approach in addressing and respecting each REDD+ component: to ensure that women have equal opportunities as men in forest management and climate change; and to present management tools for decision-makers and technicians working in the field. These implementation tools comprise the following:

• Gender Perspective in the National Forest Policy and Management Plan: to support decision makers and entities involved in the implementation of the PANFVC.

On the other hand, the institutional framework under which the respect the implementation of a gender perspective in REDD+ is guaranteed. The National Forest Policy and Management Plan comes out initiatives to mainstream the gender perspective in transparency promotion mechanisms; the National Climate Change Directorate that prepared the PANFVC and follows the gender approach guidelines; the National Commission for the Implementation of the Law on Indigenous Peoples' Participation that addresses gender aspects within MAyDS; the National Ombudsman's Office that has an Action Plan for Gender Equality and the National Institute for Women, which develops policies, programmes and initiatives to empower women, and promotes gender equality and eradication of violence15.

### 1. The gender perspective in native forests

#### 1.1 Rural population and native forests

According to the last National Census on Population and Housing Census (INDEC), only 9% of the total population of Argentina lives in rural areas. However, 83% of the rural population lives in districts with native forests; and 64% of the country's indigenous population is found in areas with native forests. This shows the importance of native forests for the communities living therein, and whose livelihoods depend on the forest.

Another fundamental aspect is that most peasant and indigenous communities live below the extreme poverty threshold. Within this context, the forest represents a strategic resource to support livelihoods, and can also help households to overcome poverty, particularly if measures to address the difficult access to credit markets are implemented (Figure 11). Sixty-four percent of the county's population with Limited Basic Needs (LBN) lives in districts with native forests, whilst 89% of the rural population with LBN lives in these districts (Figure 12). Native forests may therefore be a key element for sustaining cultural diversity and the family economy, providing opportunities to generate income and jobs, and a fundamental support for the dynamics of production for self-consumption.

The maintenance of native forest cover can be fundamental for sustaining cultural diversity and the family economy, and for protecting the rights of the most vulnerable population. In this context, it is important to consider the impact of the Forest Law on these aspects (Law No. 26,331), as it is the main measure that seeks to regulate forest use in rural communities living in native forests.

## Gender gaps in rural communities living in native forests

One of the problems identified by the FANDEyC was the differential impact of deforestation and forest degradation on women. This analysis, enriched by debates at multi-sector meetings at the regional level, provides a few elements to analyze the reasons for the vulnerability of women living in forests.

Below are the outcomes of this analysis:

* **Demographic factor:** In demographic growth scenarios, a greater pressure is expected on ecosystems that are already related to the production and supply of resources in support of daily life; that is to say, not only the direct impact related to human survival - livelihoods - is envisaged, but also the indirect impacts on the set of needs for social reproduction.


---
**Page 239**
---

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

## Cultural factor: forest degradation and deforestation is linked to the symbolic prevalence of those goods and services with a quantifiable economic value within the community, which are more appreciated from a commercial standpoint as, for instance, forest ecosystem services.

From a gender perspective, this means that women's productive work in the low value and low valuation of women's reproductive work, part of it linked to the care economy.

## Economic factor: At this point there is tension between what is considered economically productive and successful vis-à-vis what is considered important for sustaining life. The prevalence of the monetary economy over the subsistence economy has led to a reduction in the appreciation of the importance of economic and productive aspects, above those related to the care and sustainability of life.

A gender perspective: Women's productive work in the low value and low valuation of women's reproductive work is linked to the feminized notions of society - since they emulate forms of social reproduction.

## Production factor: Pressures due to use. Overall, the real estate pressure or the expansion of the agricultural frontier is a determining factor in the patterns of permanence, use and control of forest resources by men and women within communities.

As a result of the pressure on forests, community women and men that depend on them may be deprived of their access to these resources because they are not recognized as owners of the territory and their particular knowledge of it. Because of the different gender roles within communities, men and women are affected differently when expelled due to real estate pressure.

## Political-institutional factor: This includes tensions between different levels of government, asymmetric power relations in the community, and the lack of participation by women in the forest's management capacity by oversight agencies. Regarding precarious tenures, it must be noted that although it is a problem that affects entire populations, it differentially affects women who are a minority within the identification processes and generally have less access to land or the means to obtain it.

A difference of vulnerability exists in issues related to forests and deforestation due to the fact that certain types of vulnerability faced by women live in native forest ecosystems: economic vulnerability, linked to the restriction in accessing resources and financing; political vulnerability, related to their low level of participation in decision-making; and physical vulnerability, due to displacement.

Regarding economic vulnerability, the gender roles in a given community must be taken into consideration. Gender roles consist of social expectations with regard to the behavior expected from men and women within that society. These are determined by the cultural system and by a hierarchical system based on which power relations between the sexes are built. The rural sector is no exception, and there are two relevant aspects to be taken into account at the time of intervention: gender roles and the division of labor.

## Sexual division of labour: Productive and monetized tasks, usually related to paid work, are mainly carried out by men (paid forest and livestock activities) and unpaid reproductive tasks are mainly carried out by women. In addition, a higher valuation is usually assigned to productive work than to reproductive work, placing men in positions of power, decision-making, management and distribution of resources.

The division of labor is based on the gendered socialization that has been consolidated over the centuries since the times of the conquest, and is subject to being reinforced or weakened by the evolution of the social, political, and women's organizations that either question it or consider it natural.

### Invisibility of women's productive work: This is based on the marked asymmetry in power


---
**Page 240**
---

relations that cuts across the entire agrarian structure, leading to gender gaps in access, use and control of resources and goods, in opportunities, in participation in decision-making.

These factors translate into significant inequalities to the detriment of women, who are often not identified as producers. When providing offers of technical assistance, loans or training, women are not considered valid interlocutors, thus confirming their exclusive domestic reproductive role, granted by society. Women are also excluded from participating in decisions related to production-related decisions, although this is more within the realm of households. However, farming-related roles are still nowadays dominated with men; and technical assistance and loans continue to be targeted at male farmers (Figure 8). This situation has a negative impact on women’s access to women, and the improvement of their production and marketing conditions to favor their economic autonomy.

For all women to achieve economic autonomy: it is necessary to build a society in which work - in all its forms - is recognized and valued.14

Regarding access to Forest Law plans, in the period 2014 - 2018, the distribution of Forest Law implementation plans has been mostly assigned to private individual producers accounting for 80% of the financing provided through Conservation and / or Management Plans, under Law No. 26.331. Among these Forest Law Plans, 75.84% of the plans were assigned to men (1,860) and 26.15% (662) to women (Figure 13).

48.7% of the beneficiaries were women (4887) and 56.3% are indigenous (8924). These indicators show the effectiveness of a positive discrimination policy such as that implemented by the national impact of distributing benefits under the Comprehensive Community Plan (CCP) scheme. This line of work is essential to strengthen the role of women in rural areas, especially those who live and work in the forests. It is thus relevant to strengthen community production experiences because they are the ones that best protect forests, create embeddedness and propose development thought of locally for and with the communities, with good outcomes in terms of social inclusion and gender equity.

Through the planned RBRD-RBP project diagnostic will be updated and strengthened so to include indicators on women’s participation. The project will also contribute to strengthening the gender perspective. Disaggregated information is expected to be obtained on the challenges faced by households headed by women, as well as their capacity for inclusion in the development process on the ground. The implementation of the RBP project aims, among other things, at contributing to the strengthening of economic autonomy of communities that live in rural forests, particularly by providing women equitable access to these resources and the possibility to decide on their use.

Regarding political autonomy, the objective of the project is to promote participation spaces for women so that their contributions can be integrated into the collectively-designed PIPs, ensuring that their voices are taken into account and are duly considered at the time of the project’s investments and implementation.

In this regard, in the case of indigenous women, the observations made by the Indigenous Peoples area of the Ministry of Women, Gender and Violence against Women Development (currently MAPIG) with regard to mainstreaming the gender perspective were as follows:

- The inclusion of gender must take place according to the cultural and ethnic principles of social organization and representation of women in spaces for consultation and dialogue, but rather an invitation to facilitate inclusive forest management.

- Community-based actions are involved in participatory processes, consultation and planning on the ground. It is therefore important to understand the preexistence of workforces (consonancias) on community-based action and their role. The latter entails broadening the understanding of the impact of public policies; and thus, the knowledge, traditions and values of indigenous peoples must be an integral part of forest management.

Finally, with regard to physical vulnerability, we know that measures or projects that aim at transforming rural areas can generate changes in the social organization of women which may generate controversial and/or violent situations. Likewise, promoting the participation of women in the different stages of a project can provide them against existing gender inequalities.

The National Observatory on Violence against Women within the structure of the Ministry of Women, Gender and Diversity, issued a report in 2018 entitled: “Mujeres rurales y violencia de género: una mirada desde la prevención y atención” (Rural women and violence against women: An approach based on Hotline 144). The report describes the specifics of gender-based violence in rural areas.

Domestic violence against women can take on different expressions in a rural context. Some of them

31


---
**Page 242**
---

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Document linked to the National Program for gender-based violence prevention for RBD+ in Argentina, period 2016-2018

## Concern property, such as the lack of access to land ownership and the possession of farming tools.

There can also be specific forms of economic violence: for instance, women who live in agroecological gardens cultivated by women or refuse to feed animals while women are out of home. The risk of gender-based violence is higher when women are victims of violence perpetrated by their intimate partners; it is significantly higher in urban areas compared to rural areas, although the acceptance of intimate partner violence is higher in the latter.

The project will deliver gender training to all staff agents participating in project implementation, so as to avoid the reproduction of gender stereotypes and discriminatory practices.

With regard to the mechanisms for addressing potential situations of gender-based violence, since the enactment of Law 26485 on "the Comprehensive Protection of Women", passed in 2009, a hotline (No. 144) was set up pursuant to Article 9. This emergency telephone number is used in Argentina and can be called from anywhere within the country at any time. It operates for 36 hours a day, 365 days a year and it is a protection system aimed at granting comprehensive support to people who are victims of gender-based violence. The service provides information, advice, orientation, reassurance, referral and follow-up can be provided in response to a call that may be made either by someone else on behalf of the victim or directly by the person who has been subjected to the victim. It is an interdisciplinary system within a framework of coordination with national provincial and municipal organizations, as well as with civil society organizations working in this field.

Preempting points to be further detailed in the Gender Action Plan, and responding to the above-mentioned mechanisms for addressing gender-based violence:

1. Carry out a gender analysis on the roles and tasks of women and men in forest management, as well as the conditions of access to and control of resources. This analysis will be developed in the planning and drafting stages that will take place at the beginning of the implementation phase.
2. Promote participation spaces for women so that their contributions can be integrated into the content of the project's activities and into the decision-making processes, which are accurate and could be considered as the time of projects' investments and implementation
3. Train on the techniques involved in the project’s activities in gender matters so as to avoid the reproduction of discriminatory practices.
4. Use inclusive language, with explicit mention to women in all the project's calls for proposals and decisions.
5. Promote the accessibility of women to participation and decision-making forums.
6. Take into account the local context and the specificities of each community, especially when dealing with indigenous peoples
7. Undertake two key measures: to face possible situations of gender-based violence. Prevention is considered most important, including through the above-mentioned trainings and participatory spaces. Secondly The Project, supported by coordination with the Ministry of Women, will carry out a sensitization phase to address situations of gender-based violence that could arise during the project.

# Gender Assessments and conclusions recommendations

## 6. Gender Assessments and conclusions recommendations

Argentina has a broad national and international legal framework on gender and human rights that provides guidance on the protection of human rights of women and men. Therefore, the guiding principles to implement this proposal include compliance with national and international legislation, as well as the application of gender equality policies, social inclusion strategies, and environmental safeguards and guidelines to ensure gender equality as well as the rights and full participation of everyone.

Moreover, the empirical analysis included in the first section of this document shows that the main gender gaps in Argentina are linked to income levels and participation in policy decisions. The efforts aimed at strengthening the economic autonomy of women living in rural areas and communities that live in the forest, to help them achieve a sustainable development and production model that also reduces the gender gap to access resources and participate in the benefits of public policies.

Based on this analysis, the Gender Action Plan (GAP) is proposed as an integral part of the RBP Project, presented in the next section of this document. The priority objective of the GAP is to give the RBP project participants a tool to strengthen the economic autonomy of women living in rural areas and forests historically by giving them more control over access to these resources and the possibility to decide on their use. The GAP will provide inputs also so that this decision-making, which can take into consideration the needs and interests of all stakeholders involved, including those who have other activity envisaged in the RBP, may have on their productive or reproductive workload to avoid any undervaluation of women's contributions.

The GAP will be implemented through a participatory process with the aim of integrating it in existing practices and processes to develop forest management plans or actions, along with other social and environmental safeguards, i.e., to mainstream gender in the planning, decision-making and implementation phases of RBP projects.

One of the problems that was identified at the time of the preparation of the RBP funding proposal, was the existing low participation of indigenous and peasant women in forest benefits under the Forest Law. While some advances for enhanced recognition and integration were recorded in Argentina also during the development of the FANMCC, one of the challenges to implement the gender equality policies is that there are still many obstacles related to the lack of information about the roles involved in its implementation, in the benefits and in the processes. Only through the participation of men, women, young people, and other stakeholders can their knowledge, experiences, skills, desires, social inequalities and violence be voiced. Inequality and power relations may be decided by listening and paying attention to what the stakeholders say, to avoid reproducing them and to bring about change in the processes.

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) has already been recommended as a core element of the Gender Action Plan as from the planning stage.

Also relevant to the mainstreaming of the gender perspective in the implementation of the proposal is the development of tools such as gender equality indicators, the identification of resources and distribution of tasks according to certain gender roles in the specific territories where local interventions will be carried out. 

Gender mainstreaming requires the identification of gender-sensitive actions and satisfaction indicators for all the priority actions included in the funding proposal, as reported in Section 7.1 (Table 8).

The gender-related recommendations arising from the assessment for the implementation of the components are as follows:

- Ensure that forest activities will neither discriminate against women nor strengthen gender-based inequalities.
- Identify the differentiated needs of women and men other stakeholders with regard to the activities that will be carried out.
- Promote effective participation of women in decision-making to achieve equitable benefits

# Gender Action Plan

The Gender Assessment (section 1 to 6 of this document) provides current content and a general evaluation of the gender dynamics, inequalities and the situation in Argentina. It also includes the situation regarding the implementation of the GADP and the identification of specific indicators.

Based on the results of the assessment and its recommendations (Section 6), the Gender Action Plan presented in this section identifies gender-sensitive actions which shall be adopted and mainstreamed into the project activities. The plan also proposes a set of gender-specific indicators to measure and follow-up on these actions are also proposed.

At project inception, this Action Plan will be updated annually and, including a timeline for implementation, it will reflect the most up-to-date national and territorial level. Such update, of particular importance, also considering the ongoing COVID-19 health emergency, will help to confirm the relevance of the gender-sensitive actions included in the GAAP and to adapt them if needed.

The document will be shared with the AE with the GCF Secretariat for records. This information will also be disclosed in the appropriate GCF and GEF safeguard portals.

In the Action Plan, the following sections are provided: 1) Main problems and challenges of the included activities; as the information is currently unavailable. An updated baseline will be established during the preparation of the updated GAAP at inception phase, so that the impacts of implementing the actions included in this document can be measured.

Instances for the identification of main problems and challenges of women, groups of women, men and youth have already been planned; for example the participatory diagnostic and planning process organized to support the development of local interventions. Participatory Diagnostics and planning tools include the following:

- **Family Meetings:** They will allow a large target population of the Project, providing information disaggregated by gender, enabling vulnerability analysis and evaluation information.
- **Participatory Diagnosis:** They will allow a diagnosis prepared for the community on the main problems and needs to be addressed by the project. Needs and proposals will be registered and disaggregated by gender.
- **Consultations:** They are organized in intersectoral spaces with organizations, including those organizations that represent the specific interests of women's groups.

The following tools will be developed to support project implementation:

- **Engagement Protocol:** It will consider inclusion of any potential barriers or bad practices faced by women and men during consultations. This includes limited venues and timing of meetings, fear to voice own opinions within certain contexts, uncomfortable feeling to participate in a consultation due to lack of previous engagement/experience.

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Document linked to the National Program for Forests for Women (PNFm) for REDD+ in Argentina, period 2016-2018

To ensure women can participate and speak safely freely, and in a culturally appropriate context, this Protocol will take into consideration aspects such as: use of gender-sensitive meeting spaces / dissemination using inclusive language / clear, timely, complete information / adequate communication methodologies (VVP, community mapping, etc.) / specific meeting spaces for women.

## Action Protocol for cases of gender-based violence (GBV): A Protocol to be developed together with the National Gender Commission

Gender-based violence is a serious intervention in GBV. This protocol will be linked with the project grievance redress mechanism (see ESF 3.24 for details) and be prepared in consultation with women's organizations, which are key actors in preventing gender-based violence (see page 36, physical vulnerability), will be disclosed at meetings and made available to all participants. The protocol will also include a list of resources that will be provided to all staff involved in the project so as to enable them to prevent violence and act appropriately when faced with situations of gender-based violence.

### Impact declaration

Strengthen the economic autonomy of the communities that live in native forests, with an emphasis on women's equal access to these resources, and the political autonomy of women to decide their future, while promoting their participation in all stages of the project.

## Expected results:

- Diagnostic studies on access, use and control of resources and assets as well as distribution of rights and benefits
- Effective participation of women and men in the different actions included in the proposal.
- Equitable distribution of benefits among women and men from forest management actions.
- Technical staff of Law No. 26.331 national and local enforcement authorities, as well as project staff, trained in gender-related matters and human rights.

## The four components of the RRP related to which the GAP will be applied can be summarized as follows, for full details, refer to top section C.2 "Use of processes and non-carbon benefits" (the numbers correspond with the numbering used in the RRP).

### A. Territorial forest management (SDP 2/678/78)

This includes planning and implementation of forest management projects/interventions at forest basin level, and the design and implementation of integrated forest management systems (MIGB) at the community level.

### B. Forest management with integrated livestock (MIGB), in its Spanish acronym (SOP 7). MIGB systems will be designed and implemented with clear environmental and social safeguards for the various types of livestock production located in it and in categories of SDP. Main beneficiaries: small to medium cattle farmers.

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Document linked to the full document: [Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan for REDD+ in Argentina, period 2014-2016](https://example.com/document)

## C. Forest fire prevention (ISGP 103)
This component is intended to implement strategic provincial plans for the prevention of forest fires in priority regions and reinforce institutional capacities at all levels involved in forest fires. Main beneficiaries: provincial authorities, forest holders, communities and urban society.

### D. National and provincial government institutions
Technical capacities to address drivers of deforestation, to monitor forest and control are strengthened (ISGP 3). This component entails enhancing the technical capacity of national and provincial institutions for early detection and prevention of fires, including a continuous enhancement and operationalization of Argentina's NIFMS, its MRV functions, including for an enhanced early detection and prevention of fires, and operationalizing the Safeguards Information System (SIS) in order to ensure that REDD+ results are transparent. This component also includes developing commercialization services, markets and supplementary strategic investments as well as knowledge management systems with national and local enforcement authorities, and beneficiaries mentioned in components A,B and D.

# Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan

Document linked to the funding proposal for results-based payments (RBP) in Argentina, period 2014-2016

## 7.2 Role of the gender expert and Gender Team

A gender expert will be recruited at national level to technically lead and coordinate the implementation of the Gender Action Plan (GAP Coordinator). He/She will be the head of the Project Management Unit (PMU) and shall be responsible for building capacity in per forest region proposed throughout the plan, as well as those included later, and following up on such indicators.

The GAP Coordinator will be supported by at least five gender experts (one per forest region), who will be embedded in the territory-based National Forest Extension Programmes (PNEF in its Spanish acronym). The combination of the GAP coordinator and the five gender experts in the forest regions will ensure a comprehensive approach to gender mainstreaming.

For the implementation and reporting of the GAP, the coordinator will collaborate with MNA, EEI, and FP partners (see sec. C.2.5) gender focal points as needed; he/she will be in charge of drafting the Gender Action Plan (GAP), ensuring its quality and consistency, coordinating the members participating to the Project Board, reporting on the progress in the GAP implementation and supporting the decision-making process as needed. He/She will also be supporting decision-making processes at the national level through the National Councils and National Technical Committees. The gender experts recruited at regional level will support the Local Forest Consultative Committees and Provincial Technical Committees.
